. It outputs a bit indicating whether or not the opening is valid.

Definition 3.4
.

A hash family with local opening
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Ver})
is required to satisfy the following properties.

Opening completeness.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, any
N=N(\lambda)\leq 2^{\lambda}
, any
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{N}
, and any index
i\in[N]
,

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{l}b=x_{i}\\
\wedge~\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt},i,b,\mathbf{o})=1\end{array}\;:\;\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{hk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda}),\\
\mathsf{rt}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{x}),\\
(b,\mathbf{o})=\mathsf{Open}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{x},i)\end{array}\right]=1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Computational binding w.r.t. opening.

For any poly-size adversary
\mathsf{A}
, there exists a negligible function
\mathsf{negl}(\cdot)
such that for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt},i,0,\mathbf{o}_{0})=1\\
\wedge~\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt},i,1,\mathbf{o}_{1})=1\end{array}\;:\;\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{hk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(\lambda),\\
(1^{N},\mathsf{rt},i,\mathbf{o}_{0},\mathbf{o}_{1})\leftarrow\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{hk})\end{array}\right]=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Theorem 3.5

(
[
Mer88
]
)
.

Assuming the existence of a collision resistant hash family there exists a hash family with local opening (according to
Definition

3.4
).

Definition 3.6
.

A hash family with local opening
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Ver})
is said to be collapsing if any
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary
\mathsf{A}
wins in the following game with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
:

1.

The challenger generates
\mathsf{hk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathsf{A}
.

2.

\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{hk})
generates a classical value
(\mathsf{rt},j)
and a quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
.

\mathsf{A}
sends
(\mathsf{rt},j,\bm{\sigma})
to the challenger.

3.

The challenger does the following:

(a)

Apply in superposition the algorithm
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt},j,\cdot,\cdot)
to
\bm{\sigma}
, and measure the output. If the output is
0
then send
\bot
to
\mathsf{A}
. Otherwise, denote the resulting state by
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}

(b)

Choose a random bit
b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

(c)

If
b=0
then send
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{A}
.

(d)

If
b=1
then measure
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
in the standard basis and send the resulting state to
\mathsf{A}
.

4.

Upon receiving the quantum state (or the symbol
\bot
),
\mathsf{A}
outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
.

5.

\mathsf{A}
wins if
b^{\prime}=b

Theorem 3.7

(
[
Unr16
,
CMSZ21
]
)
.

There exists a hash family with local opening that is collapsing assuming the post-quantum hardness of
\mathsf{LWE}
.

3.3
Noisy Trapdoor Claw-Free Functions

In what follows we define the notion of a
noisy trapdoor claw-free function family
. This notion is simpler than the notion of a
dual-mode noisy trapdoor claw-free function family
which was used for certifiable randomness generation in
[
BCM+18
]
and by Mahadev
[
Mah18
]
in her classical verification protocol for
QMA
. This simpler notion suffices for our work.
18
18

18

Our formulation is from
[
BKL+22
]
(without the dual-mode requirement).

Definition 3.8
.

A noisy trapdoor claw-free function (
\mathsf{NTCF}
) family is described by
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithms:

(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Invert},\mathsf{Check},\mathsf{Good})

with the following syntax:

\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})
.

This
\mathsf{PPT}
key generation algorithm takes as input a security parameter
\lambda
(in unary) and outputs a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
.

We denote by
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}
the domain of the (randomized) function defined by
\mathsf{pk}
, and assume for simplicity that
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}
is an efficiently verifiable and samplable subset of
\{0,1\}^{n(\lambda)}
, where
n(\lambda)=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
. We denote by
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{pk}}
the range of this (randomized) function.

\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x})\rightarrow\mathbf{y}
.

This
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithm takes as input a public key
\mathsf{pk}
, a bit
b\in\{0,1\}
and an element
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}
, and outputs a string
\mathbf{y}
distributed according to some distribution
\chi=\chi_{\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x}}
.

\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y})\rightarrow((0,\mathbf{x}_{0}),(1,\mathbf{x}_{1}))
.

This deterministic polynomial time algorithm takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
, and an element
\mathbf{y}
in the range
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{pk}}
and outputs two pairs
(0,\mathbf{x}_{0})
and
(1,\mathbf{x}_{1})
with
\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}\in\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}
, or
\bot
.

\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\rightarrow 0/1
.

This deterministic poly-time algorithm takes as input a public key
\mathsf{pk}
, a bit
b\in\{0,1\}
, an element
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}
and an element
\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{pk}}
and outputs a bit.

\mathsf{Good}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{d})\rightarrow 0/1
.

This deterministic poly-time algorithm takes as input two domain elements
\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}\in\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}
and a string
\mathbf{d}\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}
. It outputs a bit that characterizes membership in the set:

\mathsf{Good}_{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}}:=\{\mathbf{d}:\mathsf{Good}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{d})=1\}

(1)

We specify that
\mathsf{Good}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{d})
ignores the first bit of
\mathbf{d}
.

For the purpose of this work, we allow
\mathsf{Good}
to output a vector (as opposed to a single bit).
19
19

19

We use this extension to analyze our succinct commitment scheme.
Specifically,
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}=\mathsf{Good}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{d})
is a vector in
\{0,1\}^{k}
(for some
k=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
). It is computed by partitioning the vector
\mathbf{d}
into
k
blocks, denoted by
\mathbf{d}[1],\ldots,\mathbf{d}[k]
and outputting
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}=(\langle{\mathbf{a}[1],\mathbf{d}[1]\rangle},\ldots,\langle{\mathbf{a}[k],\mathbf{d}[k]\rangle})
, where
\mathbf{a}[1],\ldots,\mathbf{a}[k]
are non-zero vectors determined by
\mathbf{x}_{0}
and
\mathbf{x}_{1}
. We define

\mathsf{Good}_{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}}:=\{\mathbf{d}:\mathsf{Good}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{d})\neq 0^{k}\}.

We require that the following properties are satisfied.

1.

Completeness:

(a)

For all
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\in\mathsf{Supp}(\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda}))
, every
b\in\{0,1\}
, every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}
, and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Supp}(\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x}))
,

\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y})=((0,\mathbf{x}_{0}),(1,\mathbf{x}_{1}))

such that
\mathbf{x}_{b}=\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Supp}(\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},\beta,\mathbf{x}_{\beta}))
for every
\beta\in\{0,1\}
.

(b)

For all
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\in\mathsf{Supp}(\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda}))
, there exists a perfect matching
\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{pk}}\subseteq\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}\times\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}
such that for all
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1})
, it holds that
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},0,\mathbf{x}_{0})\equiv\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},1,\mathbf{x}_{1})
if and only if
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1})\in\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{pk}}
.

(c)

For all
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\in\mathsf{Supp}(\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda}))
, every
b\in\{0,1\}
and every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}
,

\Pr[\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=1]=1

(2)

if and only if
\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{Supp}(\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x}))
.

(d)

For all
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\in\mathsf{Supp}(\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda}))
and every pair of distinct domain elements
\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}
, the density of
\mathsf{Good}_{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}}
is
1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

2.

Efficient Range Superposition:

For every
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\in\mathsf{Supp}(\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda}))
and every
b\in\{0,1\}
, there exists an efficient
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm to prepare a state
\ket{\varphi_b}
such that:

\ket{\varphi_b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\equiv}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{pk}}\\
\mathbf{y}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{pk}}\end{subarray}}\sqrt{p_{\mathsf{pk}}(b,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}\ket{\vecx}\ket{\vecy}

(3)

for some negligible function
\mu(\cdot)
. Here,
p_{\mathsf{pk}}(b,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
denotes the probability density of
\mathbf{y}
in the distribution
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x})
.

3.

Adaptive Hardcore Bit:
For every
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary
\mathsf{A}
there exists a negligible function
\mu
such that for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk})=(\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{d},v):\mathsf{Check}({\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}})=1~\wedge~\mathbf{d}\in\mathsf{Good}_{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}}~\wedge~\mathbf{d}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{1})=v]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\mu(\lambda),

where the probability is over
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
, and where
((0,\mathbf{x}_{0}),(1,\mathbf{x}_{1}))=\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y})
.

Proposition 3.9
.

[
BCM+18
]

There exists a
\mathsf{NTCF}
family assuming the post-quantum hardness of
\mathsf{LWE}
.

In this work we rely on the fact that every
\mathsf{NTCF}
family is
collapsing
, as defined below.
20
20

20

This definition is similar to
Definition

3.6
adapted to a
\mathsf{NTCF}
family.

Definition 3.10
.

A
\mathsf{NTCF}
family
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Invert},\mathsf{Check},\mathsf{Good})
is said to be collapsing if any
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary
\mathsf{A}
wins in the following game with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
:

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathsf{A}
.

2.

\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk})
generates a classical value
\mathbf{y}\in{\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{pk}}}
and an
(n(\lambda)+1)
-qubit quantum state
\bm{\sigma}=\bm{\sigma}_{{\cal S},{\cal Z}}
, where the
{\cal S}
register contains a single qubit and the
{\cal Z}
register contains
n(\lambda)
many qubits.

\mathsf{A}
sends
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\sigma})
to the challenger.

3.

The challenger does the following:

(a)

Apply in superposition the algorithm
\mathsf{Check}
to
\bm{\sigma}
, w.r.t. public key
\mathsf{pk}
and the image string
\mathbf{y}
, and measure the bit indicating whether the output of
\mathsf{Check}
is
1
. If the output does not equal
1
, send
\bot
to
\mathsf{A}
. Otherwise, denote the resulting state by
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}

(b)

Choose a random bit
b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

(c)

If
b=0
then it send
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{A}
.

(d)

If
b=1
then measure the
{\cal S}
register of
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
in the standard basis and send the resulting state to
\mathsf{A}
.

4.

Upon receiving the quantum state (or the symbol
\bot
),
\mathsf{A}
outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
.

5.

\mathsf{A}
wins if
b^{\prime}=b

Remark 3.11
.

An equivalent definition of collapsing is obtained by replacing
Item

3d
with the following:
If
b=1
then send to
\mathsf{A}
the state
Z_{\cal S}[\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}]
.
In this work we use both of these formulations, since it is sometimes easier to work with one and other times with the other.

Proposition 3.12
.

[
Zha22
]
Every
\mathsf{NTCF}
family is collapsing.

In what follows we define an extension of the collapsing property and argue that any
\mathsf{NTCF}
family satisfies it. This extension may appear to be unnatural, but we make use of it when proving the binding property of our commitment schemes.

Proposition 3.13
.

For every polynomial
\ell=\ell(\lambda)
, every
\mathsf{NTCF}
family
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Invert},\mathsf{Check},\mathsf{Good})
is
\ell
-extended collapsing, where the
\ell
-extended collapsing definition asserts that every
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary
\mathsf{A}
wins in the following extended collapsing game with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
:

1.

The challenger generates
\ell
independent public keys
\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots\mathsf{pk}_{\ell}\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{\ell})
to
\mathsf{A}
.

2.

\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{\ell})
generates a subset
J\subseteq[\ell]
, classical values
\{\mathbf{y}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
where each
\mathbf{y}_{j}\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{pk}_{j}}
, and a
|J|\cdot(n(\lambda)+1)
-qubit quantum state
\bm{\sigma}=\bm{\sigma}_{\{{\cal S}_{j},{\cal Z}_{j}\}_{j\in J}}
, where each register
{\cal S}_{j}
consists of a single qubit and each register
{\cal Z}_{j}
consists of
n(\lambda)
-qubits.

\mathsf{A}
sends
(J,\{\mathbf{y}_{j}\}_{j\in J},\bm{\sigma})
to the challenger.

3.

The challenger does the following:

(a)

For every
j\in J
apply in superposition the algorithm
\mathsf{Check}
to the
({\cal S}_{j},{\cal Z}_{j})
registers of
\bm{\sigma}
w.r.t.
\mathsf{pk}_{j}
and
\mathbf{y}_{j}
, and check that the output is
1
. If this is not the case send
\bot
to
\mathsf{A}
.

(b)

Otherwise, choose a random bit
b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and measure the registers
\{{\cal S}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
in the standard basis if and only if
b=1
.

(c)

Send the resulting state to
\mathsf{A}
.

4.

Upon receiving a quantum state (or the symbol
\bot
),
\mathsf{A}
outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
.

5.

\mathsf{A}
wins if
b^{\prime}=b

Proposition

3.13
follows from
Proposition

3.12
together with a straightforward hybrid argument.

4
The Distributional Strong Adaptive Hardcore Bit Property

The binding property of our succinct commitment scheme relies on a variant of the adaptive hardcore bit property, which we define next.

Definition 4.1
.

A
\mathsf{NTCF}
family
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Invert},\mathsf{Check},\mathsf{Good})
is said to have the
distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit
property if there exists
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms
\mathsf{A}
and
C
such that the following holds:
\mathsf{A}
takes as input
\mathsf{pk}
and a quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
, and outputs a tuple
(\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{x},\bm{\rho})
such that
\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=1
and
\bm{\rho}
is a state containing at least
n+1
qubits. Denote by
{\cal O}_{1}
the registers containing the first
n+1
qubits, and denote by
{\cal O}_{2}
all other registers.
C
takes as input the state
\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}}
and outputs
\mathsf{aux}\leftarrow C(\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}})
. Denote by
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}
the post measurement state, and assume that

\bm{\rho}
satisfies that with overwhelming probability over
\mathsf{aux}
,
for every
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{k}
the probability of measuring registers
{\cal O}_{1}
of
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}
in the standard basis and obtaining
\mathbf{d}\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}
such that
\mathsf{Good}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1})=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
is negligible in
\lambda
.
Then

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{d}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{1}),\mathsf{aux})\approx(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{1},U,\mathsf{aux})

(4)

where
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
,
(\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{x},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
,
((0,\mathbf{x}_{0}),(1,\mathbf{x}_{1}))=\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y})
,
(\mathsf{aux},\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}})\leftarrow C(\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}})
,
\mathbf{d}
is obtained by measuring registers
{\cal O}_{1}
of
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}
in the standard basis, and
U
is uniformly distributed in
\{0,1\}
.

Moreover, there exists
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{pre}}
, which is efficiently computable from
\mathsf{sk}
, such that given
(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{x}_{b})
and
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{pre}}
one can efficiently compute
\mathbf{x}_{1-b}
such that if
\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x}_{b},\mathbf{y})=1
then
\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{pk},1-b,\mathbf{x}_{1-b},\mathbf{y})=1
, and
Equation

4
holds even if
C
takes as input
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{pre}}
in addition to the state
\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}}
.

In
Appendix

E
, we show that the construction of an
\mathsf{NTCF}
from
[
BCM+18
]
satisfies the distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit property.

5
Classical Commitments to Quantum States

In this section we define the notion of a classical commitment to quantum states.
Our definition is stronger than the notion of a
measurement protocol
, originally considered in
[
Mah18
]
and formally defined in
[
BKL+22
]
,
21
21

21

We refer to this weaker notion as a “weak classical commitment,” and recall its definition in
Appendix

D
(for completeness).
in several ways. First, the opening basis is not determined during the key generation phase. Namely, the key generation algorithm,
\mathsf{Gen}
, takes as input only the security parameter (in unary), as opposed to taking both the security parameter and the opening basis. In particular, the opening basis can be determined after the commitment phase, and can be chosen adaptively based on any information that the parties have access to.
Importantly, our binding property is significantly stronger. It guarantees that for any
\mathsf{QPT}
cheating committer
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}
that commits to an
\ell
-qubit quantum state, there is a
single
extracted quantum state
\bm{\tau}
such that for
any

\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
and
any
basis opening
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})
, where
b_{i}=0
corresponds to measuring the
i
’th qubit in the standard basis and
b_{i}=1
corresponds to measuring it in the Hadamard basis, the opening obtained by
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})

is computationally indistinguishable from measuring
\bm{\tau}
in basis
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})
, assuming the opening of
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
is accepted.
In contrast, in the soundness guarantee of a weak commitment scheme, the extracted state
\bm{\tau}
may depend on
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
, which can be chosen adaptively after the commitment phase and hence is not truly binding.
22
22

22

Indeed, the weak classical commitment scheme from
[
Mah18
]
is only binding in the standard basis, and offers no binding guarantees when opening in the Hadamard basis.

5.1
Syntax

In what follows we define the syntax of a commitment scheme. We present two definitions. The first is the syntax for a

non-succinct
commitment scheme, where the length of the commitment string that commits to an
\ell
-qubit quantum state, grows polynomially with
\ell
. More specifically, in this definition the length
\ell
is determined in the key generation algorithm, and the run-time of all the algorithms grow polynomially with
\ell
. In
Section

5.1.1
we define the syntax for a
succinct
commitment scheme, where the verifier’s run-time grows poly-logarithmically with
\ell
.

Definition 5.1
.

A (non-succinct) classical commitment scheme for quantum states is associated with algorithms
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Commit},\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Ver},\mathsf{Out})
and has the following syntax:

1.

\mathsf{Gen}
is a
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithm that takes as input a security parameter
\lambda
and a length parameter
\ell
(both in unary), and outputs a pair
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell})
, where
\mathsf{pk}
is referred to as the public key and
\mathsf{sk}
is referred to as the secret key.

2.

\mathsf{Commit}
is a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm that takes as input a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and an
\ell
-qubit quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
and outputs a pair
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
, where
\mathbf{y}
is a classical string referred to as the commitment string and
\bm{\rho}
is a quantum state.

3.

\mathsf{Open}
is a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm that takes as input a quantum state
\bm{\rho}
and a basis
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
(where
b_{j}=0
corresponds to opening the
j
’th bit in the standard basis and
b_{j}=1
corresponds to opening it in the Hadamard basis). It outputs a pair
(\mathbf{z},\bm{\rho}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}))
, where
\mathbf{z}
is a classical string, referred to as the opening string, and
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}
is the residual state (which is sometimes omitted).

4.

\mathsf{Ver}
is a polynomial time algorithm that takes a tuple
(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}),\mathbf{z})
, where
\mathsf{sk}
is a secret key,
\mathbf{y}
is a commitment string (to the quantum state),
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
is a string specifying the opening basis, and
\mathbf{z}
is an opening string. It outputs
0
(if
\mathbf{z}
is not a valid opening) and outputs
1
otherwise.

5.

\mathsf{Out}
is a polynomial time algorithm that takes a tuple
(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}),\mathbf{z})
(as above), and outputs an
\ell
-bit string
\mathbf{m}\leftarrow\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}),\mathbf{z})
.

The protocol associated with the tuple
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Commit},\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Ver},\mathsf{Out})
is a two party protocol between a
\mathsf{QPT}
committer
{\mathsf{C}}
and a
\mathsf{PPT}
verifier
{\mathsf{V}}
and consists of two phases,
\mathsf{COMMIT}
and
\mathsf{OPEN}
. During the
\mathsf{COMMIT}
phase,
{\mathsf{V}}
takes as input security parameter
\lambda
and a length parameter
\ell
and
{\mathsf{C}}
takes in an arbitrary quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
. During the
\mathsf{OPEN}
phase,
{\mathsf{V}}
takes as input a basis bit
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
. The protocol proceeds as follows:

•

\mathsf{COMMIT}
phase:

1.

[{\mathsf{C}}\leftarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{V}}
samples
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell})
and sends the public key
\mathsf{pk}
to
{\mathsf{C}}
.

2.

[{\mathsf{C}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{C}}
computes
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
and sends the commitment string
\mathbf{y}
to
{\mathsf{V}}
.

•

\mathsf{OPEN}
phase:

1.

[{\mathsf{C}}\leftarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{V}}
sends an opening basis
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})
to
{\mathsf{C}}
.

2.

[{\mathsf{C}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{C}}
computes
(\mathbf{z},\bm{\rho}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}))
and sends
\mathbf{z}
to
{\mathsf{V}}
.

3.

[{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{V}}
checks that
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}),\mathbf{z})=1
, and if so it outputs
\mathbf{m}\leftarrow\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}),\mathbf{z})
as the decommitment. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

Remark 5.2
.

One could define
\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Ver},\mathsf{Out}
to operate on one qubit at a time. Namely, one could define
\mathsf{Open}
to take as input a quantum state
\bm{\rho}
an index
j\in[\ell]
and a basis
b\in\{0,1\}
, and output a pair
(\mathbf{z},\bm{\rho}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},(j,b))
, and define
\mathsf{Ver}
and
\mathsf{Out}
to take as input
(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(j,b),\mathbf{z})
and output a bit (indicating accept/reject for
\mathsf{Ver}
and indicating an output bit for
\mathsf{Out}
). Indeed, in our definition of a succinct classical commitment to quantum state, stated in
Section

5.1.1
below,
\mathsf{Open}
,
\mathsf{Ver}
and
\mathsf{Out}
operate on one qubit at a time. In addition, our constructions in
Appendix

A
are defined where
\mathsf{Open}
,
\mathsf{Ver}
and
\mathsf{Out}
operate on one qubit at a time.

Note that in the syntax above the length of the public key
\mathsf{pk}
as well as the length of the commitment
\mathbf{y}
grows with the number of qubits in the committed state (denoted by
\ell
). In this work we also construct
succinct
commitments where the length of
\mathsf{pk}
and the commitment
\mathbf{y}
grow only with the security parameter (and grow only poly-logarithmically with
\ell
).
In what follows we define the syntax of a
succinct
classical commitment scheme for multi-qubit quantum states.

5.1.1
Syntax for Succinct Commitments

The syntax of a succinct commitment is similar to that of a non-succinct commitment scheme (defined above), with the following main differences:

1.

The key generation algorithm (
\mathsf{Gen}
) takes as input only the security parameter
\lambda
and does not depend on the size
\ell
of the committed quantum state.

This change ensures that the runtime of
\mathsf{Gen}
does not grow with
\ell
.
23
23

23

One could give
\mathsf{Gen}
the parameter
\ell
in binary, but our scheme does not require it.

2.

The opening algorithm (
\mathsf{Open}
) opens one qubit at a time.
Namely, it takes as input the post-commitment quantum state
\bm{\rho}
, a single index
j\in[\ell]
and a basis
b\in\{0,1\}
, and it outputs an opening to the
j
’th qubit.

The reason for this change is that in some of our applications we commit to a long quantum state but open only a small portion of it. For example, this is the case in our compilation of a
X/Z
quantum PCP into a succinct argument (see
Appendix

C
).

3.

The succinct commitment has two additional components. The first is an interactive protocol that verifies that the prover “knows” a non-succinct commitment string corresponding to this succinct commitment. This protocol is referred to as
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
, and is a protocol between a poly-time (classical) prover
P
and a
\mathsf{PPT}
verifier
V
.
24
24

24

We note that since
P
is a classical algorithm the quantum state remains unchanged.
The second is a
\mathsf{Test}
protocol that tests that the committer can open all the qubits in a valid manner. We note that
\mathsf{Test}
is executed with probability
1/2
, and if it is executed then
\mathsf{Open}
is not executed (since
\mathsf{Test}
destroys the quantum state needed for the
\mathsf{Open}
algorithm).
25
25

25

This is also the case in the
\mathsf{Test}
phase in Mahadev’s measurement protocol
[
Mah18
]
.

Formally a succinct commitment scheme for quantum states consists of

(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Commit},\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit},\mathsf{Test},\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Ver},\mathsf{Out})

such that

1.

\mathsf{Gen}
is a
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithm that takes as the security parameter
\lambda
(in unary) and outputs a pair
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

\mathsf{Commit}
is a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm that takes as input a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and an
\ell
-qubit quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
and outputs a tuple
(\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
, where
\mathsf{rt}
is a succinct classical commitment to
\bm{\sigma}
(of size
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda,\log\ell)
),
\mathbf{y}
is its non-succinct counterpart, and
\bm{\rho}
is the residual quantum state.

3.

\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
is an interactive protocol between a poly-time prover
P
with input
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y})
and a
\mathsf{PPT}
verifier
V
with input
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt})
. At the end of the protocol,
V
outputs a verdict bit in
\{0,1\}
, corresponding to accept or reject (
1
corresponding to accept and
0
corresponding to reject). The communication complexity is
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda,\log\ell)
.

4.

\mathsf{Test}
is an interactive protocol between a
\mathsf{QPT}
prover
P_{\mathsf{Test}}
with input
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})
and a
\mathsf{BPP}
verifier
V_{\mathsf{Test}}
with input
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt})
. At the end of the protocol,
V
outputs a verdict bit in
\{0,1\}
, corresponding to accept or reject.

5.

\mathsf{Open}
is a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm that takes as input a quantum state
\bm{\rho}
, an index
j\in[\ell]
, and a basis
b_{j}\in\{0,1\}
(where
b_{j}=0
corresponds to measuring the
j
’th qubit in the standard basis and
b_{j}=1
corresponds to measuring it in the Hadamard basis). It outputs a pair
(\mathbf{z},\bm{\rho}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},(j,b_{j}))
, where
\mathbf{z}
is a classical string of length
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda,\log\ell)
, referred to as the
opening string
, and
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}
is the residual state (which is sometimes omitted).

6.

\mathsf{Ver}
is a polynomial time algorithm that takes a tuple
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(j,b_{j}),\mathbf{z})
, where
\mathsf{sk}
is a secret key,
\mathsf{rt}
is a succinct classical commitment string to an
\ell
-qubit quantum state,
j\in[\ell]
,
b_{j}\in\{0,1\}
is a bit specifying the opening basis, and
\mathbf{z}
is an opening string. It outputs
0
(if
\mathbf{z}
is not a valid opening) and outputs
1
otherwise.

7.

\mathsf{Out}
is a polynomial time algorithm that takes a tuple
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(j,b_{j}),\mathbf{z})
, and outputs a bit
m
.

Remark 5.3
.

We extend
\mathsf{Ver}
and
\mathsf{Out}
to take as input
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{z})
instead of
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(j,b_{j}),\mathbf{z})
, where
J\subseteq[\ell]
and
\mathbf{b}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}
, in which case the algorithms run with input
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(j,b_{j}),\mathbf{z})
for every
j\in J
. We extend
\mathsf{Open}
in a similar manner.

The succinct commitment protocol associated with the tuple

(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Commit},\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit},\mathsf{Test},\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Ver},\mathsf{Out})

is a two party protocol that consists of three phases,
\mathsf{COMMIT}
,
\mathsf{CHECK}
and
\mathsf{OPEN}
, as follows:

•

\mathsf{COMMIT}
phase:

1.

[{\mathsf{C}}\leftarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{V}}
samples
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and sends the public key
\mathsf{pk}
to
{\mathsf{C}}
.

2.

[{\mathsf{C}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{C}}
computes
(\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
and sends the succinct commitment string
\mathsf{rt}
to
{\mathsf{V}}
.

•

\mathsf{CHECK}
phase:

1.

Run
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
protocol between the prover
P
with input
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y})
and the verifier
V
with input
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt})
. If
V
rejects then the commitment
\mathsf{rt}
is rejected and the protocol ends.

2.

Otherwise, choose at random
c\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

3.

If
c=0
then go to the
\mathsf{OPEN}
phase.

4.

If
c=1
then run the
\mathsf{Test}
protocol, where the prover
P_{\mathsf{Test}}
takes as input
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})
and the verifier
V_{\mathsf{Test}}
takes as input
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt})
. If
V_{\mathsf{Test}}
rejects then the commitment
\mathsf{rt}
is rejected and otherwise it is accepted. At the end of the
\mathsf{Test}
protocol the commitment protocol ends (the
\mathsf{Open}
phase is not executed).

•

\mathsf{OPEN}
phase:

1.

[{\mathsf{C}}\leftarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{V}}
sends a subset
J\subseteq[\ell]
and an opening basis
\mathbf{b}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}
to
{\mathsf{C}}
.

2.

[{\mathsf{C}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{C}}
computes
(\mathbf{z},\bm{\rho}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}))
and sends
\mathbf{z}
to
{\mathsf{V}}
.

3.

[{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{V}}
checks that
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{z})=1
, and if so outputs
\mathbf{m}\leftarrow\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{z})
as the decommitment bit. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
.

5.2
Properties

We require that a commitment scheme satisfies two properties,
correctness
and
binding
, defined below.

5.2.1
Correctness

We define the correctness guarantee separately for the non-succinct and the succinct setting, starting with the former.

Definition 5.4

(Correctness)
.

A (non-succinct) classical commitment scheme is correct if for any
\ell
-qubit quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
, and any basis
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
,

\mathsf{Real}(1^{\lambda},\bm{\sigma},\mathbf{b})\equiv\bm{\sigma}(\mathbf{b}),

(5)

where
\bm{\sigma}(\mathbf{b})
is the distribution obtained by measuring each qubit
j
of
\bm{\sigma}
in the basis specified by
b_{j}
(standard if
b_{j}=0
, Hadamard if
b_{j}=1
), and
\mathsf{Real}(1^{\lambda},\bm{\sigma},\mathbf{b})
is the distribution resulting from the following experiment:

1.

Generate
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell})
.

2.

Generate
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
.

3.

Compute
(\mathbf{z},\bm{\rho}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b})
.

4.

If
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{z})=0
then output
\bot
.

5.

Otherwise, output
\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{z})
.

Definition 5.5

(Succinct Correctness)
.

A succinct classical commitment scheme is correct if for any
\ell
-qubit quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
, any basis
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell},
and any subset
J\subseteq[\ell]
,
the following two conditions holds:

\mathsf{Real}_{c=0}(1^{\lambda},\bm{\sigma},J,\mathbf{b}_{J})\equiv\bm{\sigma}(J,\mathbf{b}_{J})~~\mbox{and}~~\mathsf{Real}_{c=1}(1^{\lambda},\bm{\sigma},J,\mathbf{b}_{J})\equiv 1

(6)

where:

•

\bm{\sigma}(J,\mathbf{b}_{J})
is the distribution obtained by measuring each qubit
j\in J
of
\bm{\sigma}
in the basis specified by
b_{j}
(standard if
b_{j}=0
and Hadamard if
b_{j}=1
).

•

\mathsf{Real}_{c=0}(1^{\lambda},\bm{\sigma},J,\mathbf{b}_{J}
) is the distribution resulting from the following experiment:

1.

Generate
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Generate
(\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
.

3.

Run the protocol
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
between
P
with input
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y})
and
V
with input
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt})
. If
V
rejects then then output
\bot
.

4.

Otherwise, compute
(\mathbf{z},\bm{\rho}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}))
.

5.

If
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{z})=0
then output
\bot
.

6.

Otherwise, output
\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{z})
.

•

\mathsf{Real}_{c=1}(1^{\lambda},\bm{\sigma},J,\mathbf{b}_{J}
) is the distribution resulting from the following experiment:

1.

Generate
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Generate
(\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
.

3.

Run the protocol
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
between
P
with input
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y})
and
V
with input
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt})
. If
V
rejects then then output
\bot
.

4.

Execute
\mathsf{Test}
where the prover
P_{\mathsf{Test}}
takes as input
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})
and the verifier
V_{\mathsf{Test}}
takes as input
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt})
. If
V_{\mathsf{Test}}
rejects then output
\bot
and if it accepts then output
1
.

5.2.2
Binding

In what follows we define the binding condition. Intuitively, our binding guarantee is that a cheating committer cannot change the way they open based on
any
information they learn after the commitment phase, and that the opening distribution is consistent with the distribution of a qubit.

For simplicity, we consider only cheating algorithms that are accepted with high probability. This can be ensured by repetition.
Namely, for every
\epsilon,\delta>0
by repeating the commitment and opening protocol
O\left(\frac{\log(1/\epsilon)}{\delta}\right)
times, if a cheating
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}
is accepted in all of executions with probability at least
\epsilon
then a random execution is accepted with probability at least
1-\delta
.

We first define the binding property for the (non-succinct) commitment and then define it for the succinct commitment.

Definition 5.6

(Binding)
.

A classical (non-succinct) commitment scheme to a multi-qubit quantum state is said to be computationally binding if there exists a
\mathsf{QPT}
oracle machine
\mathsf{Ext}
such that for any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}
, any
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
-size quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
, any polynomial
\ell=\ell(\lambda)
, any basis
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})
, and any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}
, for every
i\in\{1,2\}

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})

(7)

and

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})

(8)

where
\eta=O\left(\sqrt{\delta}\right)
and

\delta=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell})\\
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})\end{subarray}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\{1,2\},\\
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\in\{\mathbf{b},{\bf 0},{\bf 1\}}\end{subarray}}\Pr[\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b}^{\prime},{\mathsf{C}}_{i}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}))=0].

(9)

and where
\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})
is defined as follows:

•

(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell})
.

•

(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
.

1.

Compute
(\mathbf{z},\bm{\rho}^{\prime})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b})
.

2.

If
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{z})=0
then output
\bot
.

3.

Otherwise, let
\mathbf{m}=\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{z})
.

4.

Output
(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m})
.

\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},{{\mathsf{C}}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})
is defined as follows:

1.

(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell})
.

2.

(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
.

3.

Let
\bm{\tau}_{{\cal A},{\cal B}}=\mathsf{Ext}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})
.

4.

Measure
\bm{\tau}_{\cal A}
in the basis
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})
to obtain
\mathbf{m}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

5.

Output
(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m})
.

Remark 5.7
.

Throughout this write-up to avoid cluttering of notation we omit the superscript
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}
from

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})~~\mbox{ and }~~\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},{{\mathsf{C}}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma}),

and denote these by

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})~~\mbox{ and }~~\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext},{{\mathsf{C}}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma}),

respectively.

Remark 5.8
.

We prove that our commitment scheme is sound with
\eta\leq 10\sqrt{\delta}
.
Note that
\delta
is a bound on the probability that the openings of
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}
are rejected not only on basis
\mathbf{b}
, but also on basis
{\bf 0}
and
{\bf 1}
. We note that for
Equation

8
we do not need to bound the probability that
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}
is rejected on basis
{\bf 0}
and basis
{\bf 1}
, and indeed we do not bound these probabilities in the proof (see
Lemma

B.10
). The reason we need to bound these probabilities to prove
Equation

7
is that our extractor uses the openings of
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}
on basis
{\bf 0}
and
{\bf 1}
to extract the quantum state.

Remark 5.9
.

We mention that we prove a stronger condition than the one given in
Equation

8
. This is done in
Lemma

B.10
. The strengthening is due to two reasons.
First, we prove
Equation

8
by induction on
\ell
, and for the induction step to go through we need to strengthen the induction hypothesis, and as a result we prove a stronger guarantee. Second, we allow the cheating algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
to depend on a part of the secret key
\mathsf{sk}
. This is needed to obtain our succinct interactive argument for
QMA
in
Section

C.1
and is needed for our applications in
Appendix

C
.

Definition

5.6
assumes that
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
opens all the qubits of the committed state. Indeed, we use
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
to extract an
\ell
-qubit quantum state. In what follows we define the notion of binding for a succinct commitment, where
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
may only open to a subset
J\subseteq[\ell]
of the qubits, and hence cannot be used to extract the entire state (as was done in
Definition

5.6
). While we can extract a state consisting of
|I|
qubits from
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
, for our applications we will need to extract the entire
\ell
-qubit state, even if
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
only opens to the qubits in
J
(without blowing up the communication). This is precisely the purpose of the

\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
protocol; instead of extracting from
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
we extract from the (cheating) prover
P^{*}
of the
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
protocol. We note that even though
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
is a succinct protocol, its interactive nature will allow us to extract the (non-succinct)
\ell
-qubit state from
P^{*}
.

Definition 5.10

(Succinct Binding)
.

A succinct classical commitment scheme to a multi-qubit quantum state is said to be computationally binding if there exists a
\mathsf{QPT}
oracle machine
\mathsf{Ext}
such that for any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}
, any
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
-size quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
, any polynomial
\ell=\ell(\lambda)
, any
\mathsf{QPT}
prover
P^{*}
for the
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
protocol, any
\mathsf{QPT}
prover
P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}
for the
\mathsf{Test}
protocol, any
J\subseteq[\ell]
and
\mathbf{b}_{J}=(b_{j})_{j\in J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}
, any
\epsilon>0
, and any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}
, for every
i\in\{1,2\}

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\zeta}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma},\epsilon)

(10)

and

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})

(11)

where
\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})
is defined as follows:

1.

Generate
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Compute
(\mathsf{rt},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
.
26
26

26

Note that a malicious
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}
may choose
\mathsf{rt}
maliciously without a corresponding non-succinct commitment string
\mathbf{y}
. We assume without loss of generality that all the auxiliary information it has about
\mathsf{rt}
is encoded in
\bm{\rho}
.

3.

Compute the
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
protocol between
P^{*}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\bm{\rho})
and
V(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt})
. If
V
rejects then output
\bot
.
Denote the resulting quantum state of
P^{*}
at the end of this protocol by
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post}}
.
27
27

27

If
P^{*}
was honest then it would have been classical and hence
\bm{\rho}
would have remained unchanged. But since we are considering a malicious
P^{*}
it may alter its quantum state during the
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
protocol.

4.

Compute
(\mathbf{z}_{J},\bm{\rho}^{\prime})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post}},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}))
.

5.

If
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{z}_{J})=0
then output
\bot
.

6.

Otherwise, let
\mathbf{m}_{J}=\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{z})
.

7.

Output
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{m}_{J})
.

Let
\delta_{0}
be the probability that at the end of the protocol
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
the verifier rejects. Namely,
\delta_{0}
is the probability that
Item

3
above outputs
\bot
. Denote by
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post}}
the state of
P^{*}
after the
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
protocol. Let
\delta^{\prime}_{0}
be the probability that the verifier
V_{\mathsf{Test}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt})
outputs
\bot
in the
\mathsf{Test}
protocol when interacting with
P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post}})
. Let

\delta=\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\Pr[\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{b}_{J},\mathbf{z}_{J})=0]~~\mbox{ and }~\mathbf{z}={\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post}},\mathbf{b}_{J}),

(12)

and let

\eta=O\left(\sqrt{\delta_{0}+\delta}\right)~~\mbox{ and }~~\zeta=O\left(\sqrt{\delta_{0}+\delta^{\prime}_{0}+\delta}\right)+\epsilon.

(13)

\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma},\epsilon)
is defined as follows:

1.

Generate
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Compute
(\mathsf{rt},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
.

3.

Let
\bm{\tau}_{{\cal A},{\cal B}}=\mathsf{Ext}^{P^{*},P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},\bm{\rho},1^{\lceil 1/\epsilon\rceil})
.

4.

Measure the
J
qubits of
\bm{\tau}_{\cal A}
in the basis
\mathbf{b}_{J}
to obtain
\mathbf{m}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}
.

5.

Output
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{m}_{J})
.

Remark 5.11
.

In the succinct soundness definition above we assume that
\ell=\ell(\lambda)
is polynomial in the security parameter. We could also consider
\ell
that is super-polynomial in
\lambda
, in which case we will obtain binding assuming the post-quantum
\ell
-security of the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption; i.e., assuming that a
\mathsf{poly}(\ell)
size quantum circuit cannot break the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption. The proof for a general (super-polynomial)
\ell
is exactly the same as the one where
\ell=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
, the only difference is that now we consider adversaries that run in
\mathsf{poly}(\ell)
time.

Appendix A
Constructions

In this section we present our constructions. We first construct a classical commitment scheme for committing to a
single
qubit state. This can be found in
Section

A.1
.
Then, we show a generic transformation that converts any single-qubit commitment scheme into a multi-qubit commitment scheme. This can be found in
Section

A.2
. In this scheme the size of the public key and the size of the commitments grow with the length of the quantum state committed to. Finally, in
Section

A.3
we show how to construct a succinct multi-qubit commitment scheme, where the size of the public key as well as the size of the commitment grows only with the security parameter (and poly-logarithmically with the length of the quantum state committed to). We analyze these schemes in
Appendix

B
.

A.1
Construction for Single Qubit States

In this subsection, we describe our commitment scheme for a quantum state that consists of a single qubit, denoted by
\alpha_{0}\ket{0}+\alpha_{1}\ket{1}
. We use as a building block the commitment algorithm
\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}
from
[
Mah18
]
for the
multi-qubit
case. This algorithm makes use of a
\mathsf{NTCF}
family

(\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}},\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{NTCF}},\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{NTCF}},\mathsf{Check}_{\mathsf{NTCF}},\mathsf{Good}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}).

The public key
\mathsf{pk}
used by
\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}
to commit to an
\ell
-qubit state is of the form
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{\ell})
where each
\mathsf{pk}_{j}
is a public key generated by
\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(1^{\lambda})
.
28
28

28

We mention that
[
Mah18
]
used a dual mode
\mathsf{NTCF}
family, where each
\mathsf{pk}_{i}
is generated either in an injective mode in a two-to-one mode, depending on the opening basis which is assumed to fixed ahead of time.
The
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm

\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}\left((\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{\ell}),\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}\alpha_{\mathbf{s}}\ket{\vecs}_{\cal S}\right)

outputs the following:

1.

A measurement outcome
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell})
, where each
\mathbf{y}_{j}\in\mathsf{R_{\mathsf{pk}_{j}}}
.

2.

A state
\ket{\varphi}
such that

\ket{\varphi}\equiv\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}\alpha_{\mathbf{s}}\ket{\vecs}_{\cal S}\ket{\vecx_\vecs}_{\cal Z},

(14)

where
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{s}}=(\mathbf{x}_{s_{1}}\dots,\mathbf{x}_{s_{\ell}})
where each
\mathbf{x}_{s_{j}}\in\mathsf{D_{\mathsf{pk}_{j}}}
and is such that

\mathbf{y}_{j}\in\mathsf{Supp}(\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk}_{j},s_{j},\mathbf{x}_{s_{j}})).

Construction A.1

(Commitment Scheme)
.

Our construction uses a noisy trapdoor claw-free (
\mathsf{NTCF}
) function family
(\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}},\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{NTCF}},\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{NTCF}},\mathsf{Check}_{\mathsf{NTCF}},\mathsf{Good}_{\mathsf{NTCF}})
and the algorithm

\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}
defined above.
Our algorithms are defined as follows:

•

\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda}):

1.

For every
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,n+1\}
sample
(\mathsf{pk}_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(1^{\lambda})
, where
n=n(\lambda)
is such that the domain of each trapdoor claw-free function is a subset of
\{0,1\}^{n}
.

2.

Let
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1})
and
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1})
.

3.

Output
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})
.

•

\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\alpha_{0}\ket{0}+\alpha_{1}\ket{1}):

1.

Parse
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1})

2.

Compute
(\mathbf{y}_{0},\ket{\varphi_0})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\alpha_{0}\ket{0}+\alpha_{1}\ket{1})
, where

\ket{\varphi_0}_{{\cal S},{\cal Z}}\equiv\sum_{s\in\{0,1\}}\alpha_{s}\ket{s}_{{\cal S}}\ket{\vecx_s}_{{\cal Z}}

Here,
\mathbf{x}_{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
\mathbf{y}_{0}\in\mathsf{Supp}(\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{pk}_{0},s,\mathbf{x}_{s}))
for every
s\in\{0,1\}
.
Note that register
{\cal S}
consists of
1
qubit and
{\cal Z}
consists of
n
qubits.

3.

Apply the Hadamard unitary
H^{\otimes(n+1)}
to
\ket{\varphi_0}
to obtain

\displaystyle\ket{\varphi_1}_{{\cal S},{\cal Z}}

\displaystyle=H^{\otimes(n+1)}\ket{\varphi_0}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n+1}}}\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}}\underbrace{(-1)^{\mathbf{d}\cdot(0,\mathbf{x}_{0})}(\alpha_{0}+(-1)^{\mathbf{d}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{1})}\alpha_{1})}_{\beta_{\mathbf{d}}}\ket{\vecd}_{{\cal S},{\cal Z}}

4.

Apply the algorithm
\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}
with
\mathsf{pk}_{1}
to register
{\cal S}
of
\ket{\varphi_1}_{{\cal S},{\cal Z}}
, and for every
i\in\{2,\ldots,n+1\}
apply
\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}
with
\mathsf{pk}_{i}
to register
{\cal Z}_{i}
of
\ket{\varphi_1}_{{\cal S},{\cal Z}}
. Denote the output by
(\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{n+1})
and the resulting state by

\ket{\varphi_2}_{{\cal S},{\cal Z},{\cal Z}^{\prime}}\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n+1}}}\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}}\beta_{\mathbf{d}}\ket{\vecd}_{{\cal S},{\cal Z}}\ket{\vecx'_{1,d_1},\vecx'_{2, d_2},\ldots,\vecx'_{n+1, d_{n+1}}}_{{\cal Z}^{\prime}}

where
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
and every
d_{i}\in\{0,1\}
,

\mathbf{y}_{i}\in\mathsf{Supp}(\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{pk}_{i},d_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,d_{i}})).

Note that the
{\cal Z}^{\prime}
register consists of
n\cdot(n+1)
qubits, and we partition these qubits to registers
{\cal Z}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,{\cal Z}^{\prime}_{n+1}
, each consisting of
n
qubits.

5.

Rename the register
{\cal S}
to
{\cal Z}_{1}
and split the register
{\cal Z}
into registers
{\cal Z}_{2},\dots,{\cal Z}_{n+1}
of
1
qubit each.
Permute the registers to obtain a state
\ket{\varphi_3}
such that

\ket{\varphi_3}\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n+1}}}\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}}\beta_{\mathbf{d}}\ket{d_1}_{{\cal Z}_{1}}\ket{\vecx'_{1,d_1}}_{{\cal Z}^{\prime}_{1}}\dots\ket{d_{n+1}}_{{\cal Z}_{n+1}}\ket{\vecx'_{n+1, d_{n+1}}}_{{\cal Z}^{\prime}_{n+1}}

6.

Output
(\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{n+1})
and
\ket{\varphi_3}
.

•

\mathsf{Open}(\ket{\varphi},b)
:

If
b=1
(corresponding to an opening in the Hadamard basis) then output the measurement of
\ket{\varphi}
in the standard basis, and if
b=0
(corresponding an opening in the standard basis) then output the measurement of
\ket{\varphi}
in the Hadamard basis.

•

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{z})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1})
.

2.

Parse
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{n+1})
.

3.

If
b=1
then do the following:

(a)

Parse
\mathbf{z}=(d_{1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,d_{n+1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{n+1})
and let
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n+1})
.

(b)

Compute
((0,\mathbf{x}_{0}),(1,\mathbf{x}_{1}))=\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
.

(c)

Verify that

–

\mathsf{Check}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}({\mathsf{pk}_{i},d_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{i}})=1
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
.

–

\mathbf{d}\in\mathsf{Good}_{\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}}
.

If any of these checks does not hold output
0
and otherwise output
1
.

4.

If
b=0
then do the following:

(a)

Parse
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{n+1})
where each
\mathbf{z}_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}
.

(b)

For every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
compute
((0,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0}),(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1}))=\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{sk}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{i})
.

(c)

If there exists
i\in[n+1]
such that
\mathbf{z}_{i}\notin\mathsf{Good}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1}}
then output
0
.

(d)

Else, for every
i\in[n+1]
let
m_{i}=\mathbf{z}_{i}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1})
.

(e)

If
\mathsf{Check}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{pk}_{0},m_{1},(m_{2},\dots,m_{n+1}),\mathbf{y}_{0})\neq 1
, output
0
. Else, output
1

•

\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{z})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1})
.

2.

Parse
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{n+1})
.

3.

If
b=1
:

(a)

Compute
((0,\mathbf{x}_{0}),(1,\mathbf{x}_{1}))=\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
.

(b)

Parse
\mathbf{z}=(d_{1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,d_{n+1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{n+1})
and let
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n+1})
.

(c)

Output
m=\mathbf{d}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{1})
.

4.

If
b=0
:

(a)

Parse
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{n+1})
.

(b)

Compute
((0,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1,0}),(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1,1}))=\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{1})
.

(c)

Output
m_{1}=\mathbf{z}_{1}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1,1})
.

A.2
Construction of Commmitments for Multi-Qubit States

There are two ways one can extend our single-qubit commitment scheme to the multi-qubit setting. The first is to commit to an
\ell
-qubit state qubit-by-qubit by generating
\ell
key pairs and using the
i
’th key pair to commit and open to the
i
’th qubit.
This construction results with key size and commitment size that grow linearly with
\ell
, and is presented below.
The second approach is to extend our single qubit commitment scheme to a
succinct
multi-qubit commitment scheme. This is done in two steps. First, we construct a
semi-succinct
multi-qubit commitment scheme, which is the same as the non-succinct one, except that we generate a
single
key pair
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})
and commit to each of the
\ell
qubits using the same public key
\mathsf{pk}
. This results with a commitment string
(\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell})
. Then we show how to convert the semi-succinct commitment scheme into a succinct one. We elaborate on this approach in
Section

A.3
.

Construction A.2

(Scheme for Multi-Qubit States)
.

Given any single-qubit commitment scheme

(\mathsf{Gen}_{1},\mathsf{Commit}_{1},\mathsf{Open}_{1},\mathsf{Ver}_{1},\mathsf{Out}_{1})

we construct a multi-qubit commitment scheme
consisting of algorithms

(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Commit},\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Ver},\mathsf{Out})

defined as follows, where we define
(\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Ver},\mathsf{Out})
to operate one qubit at a time (see
Remark

5.2
):

•

\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell})
:

1.

For every
i\in[\ell]
sample
(\mathsf{pk}_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{1}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Let
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{pk}_{\ell})
and
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sk}_{\ell})
.

3.

Output
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})
.

•

\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{pk}_{\ell})
.

2.

We assume that
\bm{\sigma}
is an
\ell
-qubit state, and we denote the
\ell
registers of
\bm{\sigma}
by
{\cal S}_{1},\ldots,{\cal S}_{\ell}
.

3.

Execute the following steps:

(a)

Let
\bm{\rho}_{0}=\bm{\sigma}
.

(b)

For every
j\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
, apply
\mathsf{Commit}_{1}
with key
\mathsf{pk}_{j}
to register
{\cal S}_{j}
of the state
\bm{\rho}_{j-1}
, obtaining an outcome
\mathbf{y}_{j}
and a post-measurement state
(\bm{\rho}_{j})_{{\cal S}_{1},\dots,{\cal S}_{\ell},{\cal Z}_{1}\dots,{\cal Z}_{j}}
.

4.

Output
(\mathbf{y},(\bm{\rho}_{\ell})_{{\cal S}_{1}\dots{\cal S}_{\ell},{\cal Z}_{1},\dots{\cal Z}_{\ell}})
, where
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell})
.

•

\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho}_{{\cal S}_{1}\dots{\cal S}_{\ell},{\cal Z}_{1},\dots{\cal Z}_{\ell}},(j,b_{j}))
:

1.

Apply
\mathsf{Open}_{1}
with basis
b_{j}
to registers
\{{\cal S}_{j},{\cal Z}_{j}\}
of
\bm{\rho}_{{\cal S}_{1}\dots{\cal S}_{\ell},{\cal Z}_{1},\dots{\cal Z}_{\ell}}
, obtaining an outcome
\mathbf{z}_{j}
and post-measurement state
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j}
.

2.

Output
(\mathbf{z}_{j},\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j})
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(j,b_{j}),\mathbf{z}_{j})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sk}_{\ell})
and
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell})
.

2.

Output
\mathsf{Ver}_{1}(\mathsf{sk}_{j},\mathbf{y}_{j},b_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j})
.

•

\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(j,b_{j}),\mathbf{z}_{j})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sk}_{\ell})
,
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell})
.

2.

Output
m_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{Out}_{1}(\mathsf{sk}_{j},\mathbf{y}_{j},b_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j})
.

We consider a
semi-succinct
version of the commitment scheme described above, which is used as a stepping stone for proving soundness of the fully succinct commitment scheme constructed in
Section

A.3
, below.

Definition A.3
.

A semi-succinct classical commitment scheme to quantum states is a commitment scheme obtained from a single qubit commitment scheme by applying the algorithms qubit-by-qubit, as in
Construction

A.2
, but with a single public key
\mathsf{pk}
. Namely, it is similar to
Construction

A.2
but where
\mathsf{Gen}
generates a single key pair
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{1}(1^{\lambda})
(as opposed to
\ell
such pairs), and sets
(\mathsf{pk}_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{i})=(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})
for every
i\in[\ell]
.

Remark A.4
.

In
Section

B.2
, when we prove that our (non-succinct and semi-succinct) multi-qubit commitment scheme satisfies the binding property, we assume that
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
successfully opens all the
\ell
qubits in the standard basis with high probability, and successfully opens all the
\ell
qubits in the Hadamard basis with high probability
1-\delta
. Namely, we assume that for every
b\in\{0,1\}
,
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}\left(\bm{\rho},b^{\ell}\right)
generates an accepting opening with probability
1-\delta
. To ensure that this assumption holds, we repeat the commitment phase
O(\lfloor{1/\delta\rfloor})
times.
29
29

29

We assume that
{\mathsf{C}}.\mathsf{Commit}
can generate
\bm{\sigma}^{\otimes\lfloor{1/\delta}\rfloor}
.
For each of these commitments, we ask
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
with probability
1/3
to open all the qubits in the standard basis, with probability
1/3
to open all the qubits in the Hadamard basis, and with probability
1/3
to open in desired basis
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

We emphasize that even if
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
opens only a small subset of the qubits, we still require that
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}\left(\bm{\rho},b^{\ell}\right)
generates a valid opening for every
b\in\{0,1\}
. The reason is that our binding property states that there exists an extractor
{\cal E}
that uses any
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
to extract a state
\bm{\tau}
. We want the guarantee that even if
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
only opens a small subset, still the extractor can extract an
\ell
-qubit state
\bm{\tau}
. This is important in some applications, such as compiling any quantum
X/Z

\mathsf{PCP}
into a succinct interactive argument (see
Section

C.2
).
Importantly, we need to do this without increasing the communication complexity in the opening phase, and in particular it should not grow with
\ell
.
In what follows we show how this can be done succinctly.

A.3
Construction of Succinct Multi-Qubit Commitments

Before we present our construction of a succinct multi-qubit commitment scheme, we define one of the main building blocks used in our construction.

State-Preserving Succinct Arguments of Knowledge

Our scheme uses a state-preserving succinct argument of knowledge system, defined and constructed in
[
LMS22
]
.

Definition A.5
.

[
LMS22
]

A publicly verifiable argument system
\Pi
for an
\mathsf{NP}
language
{\cal L}
(with witness relation
\mathcal{R}
) is an
\epsilon
-state-preserving succinct argument-of-knowledge if it satisfies the following properties.

•

Succinctness
: when invoked on a security parameter
\lambda
, instance size
n
, and a relation
\mathcal{R}
decidable in time
T
, the communication complexity of the protocol is
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda,\log T)
. The verifier computational complexity is
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda,\log T)+\tilde{O}(n)
.

•

State-Preserving Extraction
. There exists an extractor
{\cal E}
, with oracle access to a cheating prover
P^{*}
and a corresponding quantum state
\bm{\rho}
, with the following properties:

–

Efficiency
:
{\cal E}
on input
(\mathbf{x},1^{\lambda},\epsilon)
runs in time
\mathsf{poly}(|\mathbf{x}|,\lambda,1/\epsilon)
, and outputs a classical transcript
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}}
, a classical string
\mathbf{w}
, and a residual state
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{Sim}}
.

–

State-preserving
: The following two games are
\varepsilon
-indistinguishable to any
\mathsf{QPT}
distinguisher:

*

Game 0
(Real): Generate a transcript
\mathbb{T}
by running
P^{*}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{x})
with the honest verifier
V
. Output
\mathbb{T}
along with the residual state
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}
.

*

Game 1
(Simulated): Generate
((\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}},\mathbf{w}),\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{Sim}})\leftarrow{\cal E}^{P^{*},\bm{\rho}}(\mathbf{x},1^{\lambda},\epsilon)
. Output
(\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}},\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{Sim}})
.

–

Extraction correctness
: for any
P^{*}
as above, the probability that
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}}
is an accepting transcript but
\mathbf{w}
is
not
in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{x}}
is at most
\epsilon+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

The following is an immediate corollary.

Corollary A.6
.

An
\epsilon
-state-preserving succinct argument-of-knowledge protocol for an
\mathsf{NP}
language
{\cal L}
(with witness relation
\mathcal{R}
) and extractor
{\cal E}
satisfies that for every cheating prover
P^{*}
and a corresponding quantum state
\bm{\rho}
, and every
\mathbf{x}
, if
P^{*}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{x})
convinces the verifier
V
to accept with probability
1-\delta
then for

((\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}},\mathbf{w}),\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{Sim}})\leftarrow{\cal E}^{P^{*},\bm{\rho}}(\mathbf{x},1^{\lambda},\epsilon),

\Pr[(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w})\in{\cal R}]\geq 1-\delta-2\epsilon-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

Theorem A.7

(
[
LMS22
]
)
.

Assuming the post-quantum
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda,1/\epsilon)
hardness of learning with errors, there exists a (4-message, public coin)
\epsilon
-state preserving succinct argument of knowledge for
\mathsf{NP}
.

A.3.1
Construction

We are now ready to present our construction of a succinct classical commitment for multi-qubit quantum states. Our construction uses the following ingredients:

•

Collapsing hash family with local opening
(\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{H}},\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{H}},\mathsf{Open}_{\mathsf{H}},\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathsf{H}})
, as defined in
Definitions

3.4
and
3.6
.

•

A semi-succinct commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathbf{ss}},\mathsf{Commit}_{\mathbf{ss}},\mathsf{Open}_{\mathbf{ss}},\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathbf{ss}},\mathsf{Out}_{\mathbf{ss}})
, as defined in
Definition

A.3
, corresponding to an underlying single-qubit commitment scheme

(\mathsf{Gen}_{1},\mathsf{Commit}_{1},\mathsf{Open}_{1},\mathsf{Ver}_{1},\mathsf{Out}_{1})

•

An
\epsilon
-state-preserving succinct argument of knowledge protocol
(P,V)
, as defined in
Definition

A.5
, for the
\mathsf{NP}
languages
{\cal L}^{*}
and
{\cal L}^{**}
with a corresponding
\mathsf{NP}
relations
\mathcal{R}_{{\cal L}^{*}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{{\cal L}^{**}}
, respectively, defined as follows:

((\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt}),\mathbf{y})\in\mathcal{R}_{{\cal L}^{*}}~\mbox{ if and only if }~\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{y})=\mathsf{rt}

(15)

and

((\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt},\mathsf{rt}^{\prime},b),(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}))\in\mathcal{R}_{{\cal L}^{**}}

if and only if

\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{y})=\mathsf{rt}~\wedge~\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{z})=\mathsf{rt}^{\prime}~\wedge~\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathbf{ss}}\left(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathbf{y},b^{\ell},\mathbf{z}\right)=1.

Construction A.8

(Succinct Commitment to Multi-Qubit Quantum States)
.

In what follows we use the ingredients above to construct a succinct commitment scheme to multi-qubit quantum states.

•

\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
:

1.

Sample
(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\mathsf{sk}_{1})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{1}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Sample
\mathsf{hk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{H}}(1^{\lambda})
.

3.

Let
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\mathsf{hk})
and
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathsf{hk})
.

4.

Output
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})
.

•

\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\mathsf{hk})
.

2.

Compute
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}_{\mathbf{ss}}(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\bm{\sigma})
.

3.

Let
\mathsf{rt}=\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{y})
.

4.

Output
(\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
runs the
\epsilon
-state-preserving succinct argument of knowledge protocol for the
\mathsf{NP}
language
{\cal L}^{*}
, where
P
and
V
take as input the instance
(\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt})
and
P
takes an additional input the witness
\mathbf{y}
.
30
30

30

Note that in this protocol both the prover and the verifier are classical.
If
V
rejects then this commitment string
\mathsf{rt}
is declared invalid, and the protocol aborts.

•

\mathsf{Test}
is an interactive protocol between
P_{\mathsf{Test}}
with input
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})
and
V_{\mathsf{Test}}
with input
(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt})
that proceeds as follows:

1.

V_{\mathsf{Test}}
samples a uniformly random bit
b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
, and sends
b
to
P_{\mathsf{Test}}
.

2.

P_{\mathsf{Test}}
generates
\mathbf{z}\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}_{\mathbf{ss}}\left(\bm{\rho},b^{\ell}\right)
and sends
\mathsf{rt}^{\prime}=\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{z})
to
V_{\mathsf{Test}}
.

3.

Run the
\epsilon
-state-preserving succinct argument of knowledge protocol for the
\mathsf{NP}
language
{\cal L}^{*}
, where
P
and
V
take as input the instance
(\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt}^{\prime})
and
P
takes the additional input
\mathbf{z}
. If
V
rejects then this commitment is declared invalid and the protocol aborts.

4.

V_{\mathsf{Test}}
sends
\mathsf{sk}_{1}
to the prover.

5.

Run the
\epsilon
-preserving succinct argument of knowledge protocol
(P,V)
for the
\mathsf{NP}
language
{\cal L}^{**}
, where
P
and
V
take as input the instance
(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt},\mathsf{rt}^{\prime},b)
and
P
takes as additional input the witness
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})
.
If
V
rejects then this commitment is declared invalid.

6.

If
V
accepts in both steps 2 and 4 above then
V_{\mathsf{Test}}
outputs
1
, and otherwise it outputs
0
.

Remark A.9
.

Note that the
\mathsf{Test}
protocols runs the
\epsilon
-state-preserving succinct argument of knowledge twice: once to prove knowledge of
\mathbf{z}
and once to prove knowledge of
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})
. It may seem that it suffices to run this protocol once, since there is no need to prove knowledge of
\mathbf{z}
twice. However, in the first protocol the cheating prover does not know
\mathsf{sk}_{1}
and hence the
\mathbf{z}
that is extracted, using the extractor from
Definition

A.5
, can be efficiently computed without knowing
\mathsf{sk}_{1}
. Then we use the security guarantee of the underlying collision resistant hash family to argue that the vector
\mathbf{z}
extracted from the second protocol is identical to that extracted from the first protocol, and hence can be computed efficiently without knowing
\mathsf{sk}_{1}
. This fact is crucial for the soundness proof to go through.

•

\mathsf{Open}((\bm{\rho},\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{y}),(j,b_{j}))
:

1.

Parse
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell})
.

2.

Compute
\mathbf{o}_{j}=\mathsf{Open}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{y},j)
.
31
31

31

\mathsf{Open}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{y},j)
denotes an opening to the
j
’th chunk of the preimage
\mathbf{y}
, consisting of
\mathbf{y}_{j}
which is the commitment to the
j
’th qubit.

3.

Compute
(\mathbf{z}_{j},\bm{\rho}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}_{\mathbf{ss}}(\bm{\rho},(j,b_{j}))
.

4.

Output
((\mathbf{y}_{j},\mathbf{o}_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j}),\bm{\rho}^{\prime})
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(j,b_{j}),(\mathbf{y}_{j},\mathbf{o}_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j}))
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathsf{hk})
.

2.

Let
v_{0}=\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt},j,\mathbf{y}_{j},\mathbf{o}_{j})
.
32
32

32

\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt},j,\mathbf{y}_{j},\mathbf{o}_{j})
denotes the verification of the opening for
\mathbf{y}_{j}
, which is the
j
’th chunk of the hashed preimage.

3.

Let
v_{1}=\mathsf{Ver}_{1}(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{j},b_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j})
.

4.

Output
v_{0}~\wedge~v_{1}
.

•

\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(j,b_{j}),(\mathbf{y}_{j},\mathbf{o}_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j}))
:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathsf{hk})
.

2.

Output
m_{j}=\mathsf{Out}_{1}(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{j},b_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j})
.

Appendix B
Analysis of the Multi-Qubit Commitment Schemes from
Appendix

A

B.1
Correctness

In this section we prove the correctness of
Construction

A.2
and
Construction

A.8
.

Theorem B.1
.

The multi-qubit commitment scheme described in
Construction

A.2
satisfies the correctness property given in
Definition

5.4
.

Section

A.2
commits to each qubit of a multi-qubit state independently by using the single-qubit protocol given in construction
Construction

A.1
as a black-box. Therefore, to prove
Theorem

B.1
it suffices to prove the following theorem.

Theorem B.2
.

The single-qubit commitment scheme described in
Construction

A.1
satisfies the correctness property given in
Definition

5.4
.

We make use of the following lemma about
\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}
throughout the proof.

Lemma B.3

(Correctness of
\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}
)
.

For any
\ell
-qubit quantum state
\ket{\varphi}=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}\alpha_{\mathbf{s}}\ket{\vecs}_{\cal S}
and any basis
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell})\in\{0\}^{\ell}\cup\{1\}^{\ell}
,

\mathsf{Real}_{W}(1^{\lambda},\ket{\varphi},\mathbf{b})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)}}{{\equiv}}\bm{\sigma}(\mathbf{b})

(16)

where
\bm{\sigma}(\mathbf{b})
is the distribution obtained by measuring each qubit
j
of
\ket{\varphi}
in the basis specified by
b_{j}
(standard if
b_{j}=0
, Hadamard if
b_{j}=1
), and
\mathsf{Real}_{W}(1^{\lambda},\ket{\varphi},\mathbf{b})
is the distribution resulting from honestly opening the commitment. Specifically,
\mathsf{Real}_{W}(1^{\lambda},\ket{\varphi},\mathbf{b})
is defined by:

1.

For every
i\in\{0,1,\dots,\ell\}
, sample
(\mathsf{pk}_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Compute
(\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell}),\ket{\varphi'})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}((\mathsf{pk}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{pk}_{\ell}),\ket{\varphi})
, where
\ket{\varphi'}
is of the same form as
Equation

14
.

3.

If
\mathbf{b}=\{0\}^{\ell}
:

(a)

Measure
\ket{\varphi'}
in the standard basis to get
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{z}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{z}_{\ell})
. Parse each
\mathbf{z}_{i}=(s_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i,s_{i}})
.

If
\mathsf{Check}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{pk}_{i},s_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i,s_{i}},\mathbf{y}_{i})\neq 1
for some
i\in[\ell]
, output
\bot
. Otherwise, output
\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{\ell})
.

4.

If
\mathbf{b}=\{1\}^{\ell}
:

(a)

Measure
\ket{\varphi'}
in the Hadamard basis to get
\mathbf{d}=(\mathbf{d}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{d}_{\ell})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell(n+1)}
. For each
j\in[\ell]
, compute
((0,\mathbf{x}_{j,0}),(1,\mathbf{x}_{j,1}))=\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{sk}_{j},\mathbf{y}_{j})
. If
\mathbf{d}_{j}\notin\mathsf{Good}_{\mathbf{x}_{j,0},\mathbf{x}_{j,1}}
for some
j\in[\ell]
, output
\bot
. Otherwise, output
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{\ell})
, where each
m_{j}=\mathbf{d}_{j}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{j,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{j,1})
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the proof of Lemma 5.3 in
[
Mah18
]
, where the
\mathbf{b}=\{0\}^{\ell}
case corresponds to the
Test
round and the
\mathbf{b}=\{1\}^{\ell}
case corresponds to the
Hadamard
round.
∎

Recall that in
Construction

A.1
, the final state
\ket{\phi_3}

is the result of applying
\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}
to the state

\ket{\phi_2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n+1}}}\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}}\beta_{\mathbf{d}}\ket{\vecd}.

in the commitment procedure (pre-measurement)

We now show that the outcome of opening
\ket{\varphi_3}
in a basis
b\in\{0,1\}
is statistically indistinguishable from measuring the initial state,
\ket{\psi}
, in the basis
b
. We proceed with the proof for pure states, which extends to the case of mixed and entangled states by linearity, and show correctness for each basis separately. We treat the correctness of
\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}
as a black-box. Namely, we make use of
Lemma

B.3
throughout the proof.

Lemma B.4

(Opening in the Hadamard basis,
b=1
)
.

For any pure single-qubit quantum state
\ket{\psi}=\alpha_{0}\ket{0}+\alpha_{1}\ket{1}
and any
\mathsf{NTCF}
family, the distribution over the outcomes of the following two experiments are statistically indistinguishable under
Construction

A.1
:

•

Experiment 1.
Measure
\ket{\psi}
in the Hadamard basis and report the outcome.

•

Experiment 2.
Execute
\mathsf{Real}(1^{\lambda},\ket{\psi},b_{1}=1)
, as described in
Definition

5.4
.

Proof.

By inspection, it can be seen that the distribution of outcomes obtained from
\mathsf{Real}(1^{\lambda},\ket{\psi},b_{1}=1)
here is the same as the outcome obtained from the following procedure:

1.

Generate keys
(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\mathsf{pk}_{0})
.

2.

Apply the weak commitment once to get
\mathbf{y}_{0},\ket{\phi_1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}_{W}(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\ket{\psi})
.

3.

Apply a Hadamard transform to the state to get

\ket{\phi_2}=H^{\otimes(n+1)}\ket{\phi_1}
.

4.

Execute
\mathsf{Real}_{W}(1^{\lambda},\ket{\phi_2},0^{n+1})
to obtain an outcome
\mathbf{d}=(d_{0},\dots,d_{n+1})
.

5.

Report an outcome
\mathbf{d}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{1})
, where
\{(b,\mathbf{x}_{b})\}_{b=0,1}=\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
.

By
Lemma

B.3
for standard basis openings, the distribution over
\mathbf{d}
statistically close to the distribution obtained by measuring
\ket{\phi_2}
in the standard basis. This, in turn, by construction is equal to the distribution obtained by measuring
\ket{\phi_1}
in the Hadamard basis. Finally, by applying
Lemma

B.3
again, this time for Hadamard basis openings, this implies that the distribution of
\mathbf{d}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{1})
is statistically close to the distribution obtained by measuring
\ket{\psi}
in the Hadamard basis.

∎

Lemma B.5

(Opening in the standard basis,
b=0
)
.

For any pure single-qubit quantum state
\ket{\psi}=\alpha_{0}\ket{0}+\alpha_{1}\ket{1}
and any
\mathsf{NTCF}
family, the distribution over the outcomes of the following two experiments are statistically indistinguishable under
Construction

A.1
:

•

Experiment 1.
Measure
\ket{\psi}
in the standard basis and report the outcome.

•

Experiment 2.
Execute
\mathsf{Real}(1^{\lambda},\ket{\psi},b_{1}=0)
, as described in
Definition

5.4
.

Proof.

By inspection, it can be seen that the distribution of outcomes obtained from
\mathsf{Real}(1^{\lambda},\ket{\psi},b_{1}=1)
here is the same as the outcome obtained from the following procedure:

1.

Generate keys
(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\mathsf{pk}_{0})
.

2.

Apply the weak commitment once to get
\mathbf{y}_{0},\ket{\phi_1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}_{W}(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\ket{\psi})
.

3.

Apply a Hadamard transform to the state to get

\ket{\phi_2}=H^{\otimes(n+1)}\ket{\phi_1}
.

4.

Execute
\mathsf{Real}_{W}(1^{\lambda},\ket{\phi_2},1^{n+1})
to obtain an outcome
\mathbf{m}=(m_{0},\dots,m_{n})
.

5.

If
\mathsf{Check}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{pk}_{0},m_{0},(m_{1},\dots,m_{n}),\mathbf{y}_{0})=1
, output
m_{0}
; else, output
\bot
.

By
Lemma

B.3
, applied in the Hadamard basis case, the outcome
\mathbf{m}
has a distribution that is statistically close to the outcome of a Hadamard basis measurement of
\ket{\phi_2}
. By construction, this is equal to the distribution of the outcome of a standard basis measurement of
\ket{\phi_1}
. Finally, by
Lemma

B.3
, applied in the standard basis case, the distribution of a standard outcome of
\ket{\phi_1}
will pass the check
\mathsf{Check}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{pk}_{0},m_{0},(m_{1},\dots,m_{n}),\mathbf{y}_{0})
with probability negligibly close to
1
, and the bit
m_{0}
will be distributed close to the distribution obtained by measuring
\ket{\psi}
in the standard basis.

∎

Proof of
Theorem

B.2
.

The theorem follows immediately from
Lemma

B.4
and
Lemma

B.5
.
∎

We now proceed with the proof of correctness for the succinct commitment scheme.

Theorem B.6
.

The succinct multi-qubit commitment scheme described in
Construction

A.8
satisfies the correctness property given in
Definition

5.5
.

Proof.

By correctness of the state-preserving succinct argument of knowledge protocol
[
LMS22
]
,
\mathsf{Ver}.\mathsf{Commit}
in
Construction

A.8
