accepts with probability
1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
. The
\mathsf{Commit}
algorithm in that construction uses
\mathsf{Commit}_{\mathbf{ss}}
as a black box, which consists of applying the
\mathsf{Commit}_{1}
procedure to each qubit of
\bm{\sigma}
under the same public key
\mathsf{pk}_{1}
. Therefore, for the
c=0
case in
Definition

5.5
, correctness holds by
Theorem

B.2
. The
c=1
case follows from the correctness of the state-preserving argument of knowledge
[
LMS22
]
.
∎

B.2
Binding

In this section we prove the following two theorems.

Theorem B.7
.

The non-succinct commitment scheme described in
Section

A.2
satisfies the binding property given in
Definition

5.6
(assuming the existence of a
\mathsf{NTCF}
family).

Theorem B.8
.

The semi-succinct multi-qubit commitment scheme described in
Section

A.2
(
Definition

A.3
) satisfies the binding property given in
Definition

5.6
if the underlying single qubit commitment scheme is the one from
Section

A.1
and the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family is the one from
[
BCM+18
]
and assuming it satisfies the distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit property (see
Definition

4.1
).
33
33

33

We recall that
[
BCM+18
]
satisfies the distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit property under
\mathsf{LWE}
(see
Proposition

E.3
).

To prove the above two theorems we need to prove that both the non-succinct and the semi-succinct commitment schemes described in
Section

A.2
satisfy
Equation

7
and
Equation

8
of the binding property (
Definition

5.6
).

Remark B.9
.

We note that
Equation

8
only relies on the fact that the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family is collapsing (as defined in
Proposition

3.12
), whereas
Equation

7
relies on the adaptive hardcore bit property for the non-succinct scheme and on the specific properties of the
\mathsf{NTCF}
family from
[
BCM+18
]
(specifically, the distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit property) for the semi-succinct scheme.

We start by proving that both the non-succinct and the semi-succinct commitment schemes satisfy
Equation

8
.
We actually prove a stronger version of
Equation

8
, stated below.

Lemma B.10
.

[Stronger version of
Equation

8
]
For any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}
and quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
, any purification
\ket{\varphi}
of
\bm{\sigma}
, any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}
, any
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, and any efficient unitaries
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
there exists a negligible function
\mu=\mu(\lambda)
such that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\end{subarray}}\|U_{1}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{1}V_{\mathsf{ext},1}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}-U_{2}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{2}V_{\mathsf{ext},2}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~{\eta+\epsilon+\mu}

where

•

\epsilon=\E\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\end{subarray}}\|V_{1}\ket{\psi}-V_{2}\ket{\psi}\|_{2}
.

•

\eta=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}2\sqrt{\delta_{j}}
for

\delta_{j}\triangleq\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\\
(\mathbf{z}_{i},\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{i})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b})\end{subarray}}\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\Pr[\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(j,b_{j}),\mathbf{z}_{i,j})=0~|~\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(k,b_{k}),\mathbf{z}_{i,k})=1~\forall k\in[j-1]]

where
\mathbf{z}_{i}=(\mathbf{z}_{i,j})_{j=1}^{\ell}
.

•

\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}=\ket{0^\ell}_{\mathsf{copy}}\otimes\ket{0^\ell}_{\mathsf{out}}\otimes\ket{\vecb}_{\mathsf{basis}}\otimes\ket{\psi}
.

•

For every
i\in\{1,2\}
,
V_{\mathsf{ext},i}=I_{\mathsf{copy}}\otimes I_{\mathsf{out}}\otimes I_{\mathsf{basis}}\otimes V_{i}
.

•

For every
i\in\{1,2\}
,
U_{i}
is the unitary defined by applying
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}
to the registers
\mathsf{open}
and
\mathsf{basis}
.

•

U_{\mathsf{Out}}
is the unitary defined by first applying the unitary corresponding to
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\cdot,\cdot)
to registers
\mathsf{open}
and
\mathsf{basis}
, and controlled on
\mathsf{Ver}
accepting, applying the unitary corresponding to
\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\cdot,\cdot)
to registers
\mathsf{open}
and
\mathsf{basis}
, and writing the output on the register
\mathsf{out}
.

•

\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}
applies a
\mathsf{CNOT}
to registers
\mathsf{copy}
and
\mathsf{out}
(i.e., it copies register
\mathsf{out}
to register
\mathsf{copy}
).

Moreover,
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}
can be
\mathsf{QPT}
given
\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1}
when opening in the standard basis and
\mathsf{QPT}
when opening in the Hadamard basis.
34
34

34

This generalization is needed to obtain
Corollary

B.11
.
Alternatively, they can be
\mathsf{QPT}
given
\mathsf{sk}_{0}
when opening in the Hadamard basis and
\mathsf{QPT}
when opening in the standard basis.

Corollary B.11
.

For any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}
and quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
, any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}
, and any
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
,

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2(\sqrt{\delta_{0}}+\sqrt{\delta_{1}})}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})

where denoting by
I_{b}=\{i\in[\ell]:\mathbf{b}_{i}=b\}
,

\delta_{b}=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})\\
\mathbf{z}_{i}\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b})\end{subarray}}\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\Pr[\mathsf{Ver}\left(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(I_{b},b^{|I_{b}|}),\mathbf{z}_{i,I_{b}}\right)=0]

where
\mathbf{z}_{i,I_{b}}=(\mathbf{z}_{i,j})_{j\in I_{b}}
.

Moreover,
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}
can be
\mathsf{QPT}
given
\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1}
when opening in the standard basis and
\mathsf{QPT}
when opening in the Hadamard basis.

Proof of
Corollary

B.11

Fix any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}
and quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
, any algorithms
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}
as in the statement of
Corollary

B.11
, and any basis
\mathbf{b}
. For every
i\in\{1,2\}
we slightly change
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}
to
{\mathsf{C}}^{**}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}
, as follows:

{\mathsf{C}}^{**}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},(j,b))
coherently computes
\mathbf{z}\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b})
and outputs
\mathbf{z}_{j}
if
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(I_{b},\mathbf{b}_{I_{b}}),\mathbf{z})=1
, and otherwise it outputs
\bot
.
35
35

35

Note that
\mathbf{b}_{I_{b}}=b^{|I_{b}|}

Note that
{\mathsf{C}}^{**}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}
remains a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm when opening in the Hadamard basis since
\mathsf{Ver}
does not use
\mathsf{sk}
when verifying a Hadamard basis opening, whereas it uses
\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1}
when opening in the standard basis. Thus
{\mathsf{C}}^{**}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{**}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}
satisfy the efficiency conditions of
Lemma

B.10
. In addition, note that
for every
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})\equiv\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{**}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma}).

where
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
.
By
Lemma

B.10
for any purification
\ket{\varphi}
of
\bm{\sigma}
there exists a negligible function
\mu
such that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\end{subarray}}\|U_{1}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{1}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}-U_{2}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{2}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~{\eta+\mu}

where
U_{i}
is the unitary defined by
{\mathsf{C}}^{**}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}
, and
U_{\mathsf{Out}}
and
\eta
are as defined in
Lemma

B.10
.
It remains to observe that
\eta\leq 2\sqrt{\delta_{0}}+2\sqrt{\delta_{1}}
, which follows from the the definition of
{\mathsf{C}}^{**}_{i}.\mathsf{Open}
, which asserts that
\delta_{j}=0
if there exists
k\in\{1,\ldots,j-1\}
for which
b_{j}=b_{k}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

B.10

We prove this lemma for the semi-succinct variant of the multi-qubit commitment scheme described in
Section

A.2
.
The proof for the non-succinct variant is identical.
The proof is by induction on
\ell
.

Base case:
\ell=1
.

Fix any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}
, a quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
, algorithms
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{1}.\mathsf{Open}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{2}.\mathsf{Open}
, basis
b\in\{0,1\}
, and efficient unitaries
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, as in the lemma statement. Also fix a purification
\ket{\varphi}
of
\bm{\sigma}
.
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists a non-negligible
\xi=\xi(\lambda)
such that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\end{subarray}}\|U_{1}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{1}V_{\mathsf{ext},1}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}-U_{2}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{2}V_{\mathsf{ext},2}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}\|_{2}\geq

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~{\eta+\epsilon+\xi}

We construct a

\mathsf{QPT}
adversary
\mathsf{A}
that uses the
\mathsf{QPT}
committer
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}
, its purified state
\ket{\varphi}
, and the unitaries
U_{1},U_{2},V_{1},V_{2},U_{\mathsf{Out}}
to break the collapsing property of the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family (
Definition

3.10
). We break the collapsing property as formulated in
Remark

3.11
. We distinguish between the case that
b=0
and the case that
b=1
.

Case 1:
b=0
.

The adversary
\mathsf{A}
operates as follows:

1.

Advserary:
Upon receiving a public key
\mathsf{pk}_{0}
from the challenger, where
(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\mathsf{sk}_{0})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(1^{\lambda})
:

(a)

For every
i\in[n+1]
generate
(\mathsf{pk}_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(1^{\lambda})
.

(b)

Let
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1})
.

(c)

Compute
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})
.

(d)

Parse
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{n+1})

(e)

Let
\ket{\psi'}=U\left(\ket{+}_{\mathsf{coin}}\otimes\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}\right)
, where

U=\ket{0}\bra{0}_{\mathsf{coin}}\otimes U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{1}V_{\mathsf{ext},1}+\ket{1}\bra{1}_{\mathsf{coin}}\otimes U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{2}V_{\mathsf{ext},2}

Recall that
U_{\mathsf{Out}}
first computes
\mathsf{Ver}
which in
Item

4d
computes
\mathbf{m}\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}
.
U_{\mathsf{Out}}
stores in register
\mathsf{out}
the output, which is the first bit of
\mathbf{m}
. We denote by
\mathsf{preimage}
the registers that store the last
n
bits of
\mathbf{m}
.

(f)

Send to the challenger the string
\mathbf{y}_{0}
and the registers
\mathsf{out}
and
\mathsf{preimage}
of
\ket{\psi'}
.

Notice that since
b=0
,
U_{\mathsf{Out}}
(as possibly
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
) use only the secret keys
(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1})
, which
\mathsf{A}
knows, and thus
\mathsf{A}
can efficiently apply the unitary
U
to the state
\ket{+}_{\mathsf{coin}}\otimes\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}}
.

2.

Challenger:
Recall that
the challenger applies in superposition the algorithm
\mathsf{Check}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}
to the state it receives w.r.t. public key
\mathsf{pk}_{0}
and the image string
\mathbf{y}_{0}
, and measures the bit indicating whether the output of
\mathsf{Check}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}
is
1
. If this is not the case it sends
\bot
. Otherwise, it chooses a random bit
u\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and measures this state if and only if
u=1
. It then sends the resulting state to the adversary.

Note that by the two-to-one nature of the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family, measuring the entire state is equivalent to measuring only the first qubit of the state, i.e., register
\mathsf{out}
. Thus, we can assume that the challenger measures only register
\mathsf{out}
if and only if
u=1
.

In addition, note that conditioned on the challenger not outputting
\bot
, the state is projected to
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}\ket{\psi'}
(up to normalization), where
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}\ket{\psi'}
is the state
\ket{\psi'}
projected to the challenger accepting the state. Consider the state
\mathsf{CNOT}^{u}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}\ket{\psi'}
. Note that this state, with the
\mathsf{copy}
register excluded, is indistinguishable from the state returned from the challenger conditioned on choosing the random bit
u
. Thus we think of the adversary as receiving this state.

3.

Adversary:

If the adversary receives
\bot
from the challenger, then it outputs a uniformly random
u^{\prime}
.
Note that this occurs with probability at most
\delta
.

Otherwise, the adversary
\mathsf{A}
receives the registers
\mathsf{out}
and
\mathsf{preimage}
from the challenger (either measured or not, depending on
u
). The joint state of the adversary and challenger at this point is
\mathsf{CNOT}^{u}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}\ket{\psi'}
, where all registers except
\mathsf{copy}
are held by the adversary. The adversary does the following:

(a)

Let

U^{\prime}=\ket{0}\bra{0}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes U_{1}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}+\ket{1}\bra{1}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes U_{2}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}.

(b)

Apply
U^{\prime}
to the adversary’s system, resulting in the joint state

\displaystyle U^{\prime}\mathsf{CNOT}^{u}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}\ket{\psi'}=

\displaystyle U^{\prime}\mathsf{CNOT}^{u}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}U\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}}=

\displaystyle\ket{0}\bra{0}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes U^{\dagger}_{1}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}^{u}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}U_{\mathsf{out}}U_{1}V_{\mathsf{ext},1}(\ket{+}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}})+

\displaystyle\ket{1}\bra{1}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes U^{\dagger}_{2}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}^{u}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}U_{\mathsf{out}}U_{2}V_{\mathsf{ext},2}(\ket{+}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}})

(c)

Output the measurement of the
\mathsf{Coin}
register in the Hadamard basis, denoted by
u^{\prime}
(i.e.,
u^{\prime}=0
if the measurement is
\ket{+}
and is
u^{\prime}=1
if the measurement is
\ket{-}
).

Consider the states

U^{\dagger}_{1}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}^{u}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}U_{\mathsf{out}}U_{1}V_{\mathsf{ext},1}\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}}~~\mbox{ and }~~U^{\dagger}_{2}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}^{u}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}U_{\mathsf{out}}U_{2}V_{\mathsf{ext},2}\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}}

Note that for
u=0
, these states are
(2\sqrt{\delta}+\epsilon)
-close in
\|\cdot\|_{2}
distance. This follows from the fact that by
Lemma

3.1
, together with the assumption that the probability that
\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}}
opens successfully is
\geq 1-\delta
, it holds that for every
i\in\{1,2\}
:

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\end{subarray}}\|U^{\dagger}_{i}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}U_{\mathsf{out}}U_{i}V_{\mathsf{ext},i}\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}}-U^{\dagger}_{i}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{out}}U_{i}V_{\mathsf{ext},i}\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\delta},

and from our assumption that

\displaystyle\epsilon=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\end{subarray}}\|V_{\mathsf{ext},1}\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}}-V_{\mathsf{ext},2}\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}}\|_{2}.

This implies that there exists a negligible function
\mu
such that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\end{subarray}}\|U_{1}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{1}V_{\mathsf{ext},1}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}-U_{2}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{2}V_{\mathsf{ext},2}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~{2\sqrt{\delta}+\epsilon+\mu}

On the other hand, by our contradiction assumption, for
u=1
, these two states are
(2\sqrt{\delta}+\epsilon)
-far. This, together with
Proposition

B.12
below, implies that
\mathsf{A}
indeed breaks the collapsing property of the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family.

Proposition B.12
.

For any two states
\ket{\psi_0}
and
\ket{\psi_1}
such that
\left\|\ket{\psi_0}-\ket{\psi_1}\right\|=\epsilon
, and for
\ket{\varphi}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ket{0}\ket{\psi_0}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ket{1}\ket{\psi_1}
, it holds that

\Pr[H[\varphi]\rightarrow 1]=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4}.

Proof.

We calculate

\displaystyle\Pr[H[\varphi]\mapsto 1]

\displaystyle=\norm{(\bra{1} \otimes I) H \ket{\varphi}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\norm{(\bra{1} \otimes I) \left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \ket{+} \ket{\psi_0} + \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \ket{-} \ket{\psi_1} \right)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{2}\ket{\psi_0}-\frac{1}{2}\ket{\psi_1}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{2}.\qed

Case 2:
b=1
.

We show how to use the adversary
\mathsf{A}
to break the extended collapsing game (see
Proposition

3.13
).
The adversary
\mathsf{A}
operates as follows:

1.

Upon receiving public keys
(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1})
from the challenger, where
(\mathsf{pk}_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(1^{\lambda})
for every
i\in[n+1]
, do the following:

(a)

Generate
(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\mathsf{sk}_{0})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(1^{\lambda})
.

(b)

Let
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1})
.

(c)

Compute
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})
.

(d)

Parse
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{n+1})
.

(e)

Compute
((0,\mathbf{x}_{0}),(1,\mathbf{x}_{1}))=\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
.

(f)

Let
J=\{j\in\{2,\ldots,n+1\}:x_{0,j-1}\oplus x_{1,j-1}=1\}\cup\{1\}
.

(g)

As in the
b=0
case, define

U=\ket{0}\bra{0}_{\mathsf{coin}}\otimes U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{1}V_{\mathsf{ext},1}+\ket{1}\bra{1}_{\mathsf{coin}}\otimes U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{2}V_{\mathsf{ext},2}

and prepare the state
\ket{\psi'}=U(\ket{+}_{\mathsf{coin}}\otimes\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}})
.

Note that since
b=1
it holds that
\ket{\psi'}
can be computed efficiently given
\mathsf{sk}_{0}

(h)

For every
j\in[J]
, denote by
{\cal X}_{j}
and
{\cal Z}_{j}
the registers in
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}
corresponding to
d_{j}
and
x^{\prime}_{j}
, respectively.

(i)

Send
J
,
\{\mathbf{y}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
and the registers
\{{\cal X}_{j},{\cal Z}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of
\ket{\psi'}
.

2.

Recall that the challenger applies in superposition the algorithm
\mathsf{Check}
to the state it received w.r.t. the image strings
\{\mathbf{y}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
, where the
j
’th check is w.r.t
\mathsf{pk}_{j}
, and measures the bit indicating whether the output of
\mathsf{Check}
is
1
. If any of the outputs of
\mathsf{Check}
are
0
, the challenger immediately halts and sends
\bot
to the adversary. Otherwise, it chooses a random bit
u\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and applies
Z^{u}
to every
{\cal X}_{j}
register. It then sends the resulting state to the adversary.

3.

If the adversary receives
\bot
, it returns a uniformly random
u^{\prime}
.
Otherwise, observe that once the adversary receives the state from the challenger, it is in possession of all the quantum registers. At this point, they are, up to normalization, in the state
Z^{u}_{J}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver},J}\ket{\psi'}
, where
Z_{J}=\prod_{j\in J}Z_{{\cal X}_{j}}
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver},J}\ket{\psi'}
is the state
\ket{\psi'}
projected to an accepting state.

It then does the following:

(a)

Let

U^{\prime}=\ket{0}\bra{0}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes U_{1}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}+\ket{1}\bra{1}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes U_{2}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}.

(b)

Apply
U^{\prime}
to its registers, resulting in the state

\displaystyle U^{\prime}Z^{u}_{J}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver},J}\ket{\psi'}=

\displaystyle\left(\ket{0}\bra{0}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes U^{\dagger}_{1}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}Z^{u}_{J}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}U_{\mathsf{out}}U_{1}V_{\mathsf{ext,1}}+\ket{1}\bra{1}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes U^{\dagger}_{2}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}Z^{u}_{J}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}U_{\mathsf{out}}U_{2}V_{\mathsf{ext,2}}\right)(\ket{+}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}})

(c)

Output the measurement of the first register of this state in the Hadamard basis, denoted by
u^{\prime}
.

Consider the states

U^{\dagger}_{1}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}Z^{u}_{J}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}U_{\mathsf{out}}U_{1}V_{\mathsf{ext},1}\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}}~~\mbox{ and }~~U^{\dagger}_{2}U_{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}Z^{u}_{J}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}U_{\mathsf{out}}U_{2}V_{\mathsf{ext},2}\ket{\psi_{\mathsf{ext}}}

Note that similarly to the
b=0
case, for
u=0
these states are
(2\sqrt{\delta}+\epsilon)
-close in
\|\cdot\|_{2}
distance.
On the other hand, by our contradiction assumption, together with
Lemma

3.3
, for
u=1
, these two states are
(2\sqrt{\delta}+\epsilon)
-far in
\|\cdot\|_{2}
distance. This together with
Proposition

B.12
, implies that indeed
\mathsf{A}
breaks the collapsing property of the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family.

Induction step:

Suppose that the multi-qubit commitment scheme is sound for
\ell-1
and we prove that it is sound for
\ell
.
We need to prove that there exists a negligible function
\mu=\mu(\lambda)
such that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\end{subarray}}\|U_{1}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{1}V_{\mathsf{ext},1}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}-U_{2}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{2}V_{\mathsf{ext},2}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~{\eta+\epsilon+\mu}

for
\eta=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}2\sqrt{\delta_{j}}
and
\epsilon=\E\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\end{subarray}}\|V_{1}\ket{\psi}-V_{2}\ket{\psi}\|_{2}
.

To this end, note that for every
i\in\{1,2\}

\displaystyle U_{i}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy},\mathsf{out}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{i}V_{\mathsf{ext},i}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}=

\displaystyle U_{i}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy}_{\ell},\mathsf{out}_{\ell}}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy}_{[1,\ell-1]},\mathsf{out}_{[1,\ell-1]}}U_{\mathsf{Out}}U_{i}V_{\mathsf{ext},i}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}=

\displaystyle U_{i}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}_{\ell}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy}_{\ell},\mathsf{out}_{\ell}}U_{\mathsf{Out}_{\ell}}U_{i}\underbrace{U_{i}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}_{[1,\ell-1]}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy}_{[1,\ell-1]},\mathsf{out}_{[1,\ell-1]}}U_{\mathsf{Out}_{[1,\ell-1]}}U_{i}V_{\mathsf{ext},i}}_{V_{i}^{\prime}}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}

For every
i\in\{1,2\}
, denote by

\ket{\psi'_i}=V_{i}^{\prime}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}

By the induction hypothesis, there exists a negligible function
\mu=\mu(\lambda)
such that

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\end{subarray}}\|\ket{\psi'_1}-\ket{\psi'_2}\|_{2}\leq\eta^{\prime}+\mu

where
\eta^{\prime}=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}2\sqrt{\delta_{j}}+\epsilon
.
Denoting by
\epsilon^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime}
, our base case implies that there exists a negligible function
\nu=\nu(\lambda)
such that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\varphi})\end{subarray}}\|U_{1}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}_{\ell}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy}_{\ell},\mathsf{out}_{\ell}}U_{\mathsf{out}_{\ell}}U_{1}V^{\prime}_{1}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}-U_{1}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{Out}_{\ell}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy}_{\ell},\mathsf{out}_{\ell}}U_{\mathsf{out}_{\ell}}U_{1}V_{2}^{\prime}\ket{\psi_\mathsf{ext}}\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~2\sqrt{\delta_{\ell}}+\eta^{\prime}+\nu

as desired.

∎

7
.

Proof of
Lemmas

and

We prove these two lemmas jointly since much of the proof is identical. In both cases we think of the public and secret keys as being

\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{\ell})~~\mbox{ and }~~\mathsf{sk}=(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{\ell})

where in the non-succinct commitment each
(\mathsf{pk}_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(1^{\lambda})
and in the semi-succinct commitment
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(1^{\lambda})
, and for every
i\in[\ell]

\mathsf{sk}_{i}=\mathsf{sk}~~\mbox{ and }~~\mathsf{pk}_{i}=\mathsf{pk}

Fix any
\mathsf{QPT}
cheating committer
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}
with auxiliary quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
that commits to an
\ell
-qubit state. Denote by

(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma}),

where
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell})
, each
\mathbf{y}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i,0},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{i,n+1})
and each
\mathbf{y}_{i,j}
is in
\mathsf{R}_{\mathsf{pk}_{j}}
which is the range of the
\mathsf{NTCF}
function
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk}_{j},\cdot)
.
Fix any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
.
We start by defining the
\mathsf{QPT}
extractor
\mathsf{Ext}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})
. We do so
in two steps:

1.

First, we define
2\ell
“operational observables”
\{P_{X_{i}},P_{Z_{i}}\}_{i\in\ell]}
such that for every
i\in[\ell]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
,

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{ideal},i,b})\equiv(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m}_{i,b})

where
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell})
,
36
36

36

In the semi-succinct setting
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
.

(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
,
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{ideal},i,b}
is obtained by measuring
\bm{\rho}
in the
P_{X_{i}}
basis if
b=1

P_{Z_{i}}
basis if

, and
\mathbf{m}_{i,b}

\mathbf{z}\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},b^{\ell})
and setting
\mathbf{m}_{i,b}=\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(i,b),\mathbf{z}_{i})
.

2.

We then use these operational observables to extract a state
\bm{\tau}
. This is done following the approach of
[
Mah18
,
Vid20
,
BKL+22
]
,

Defining the operational observables
\{P_{X_{i}},P_{Z_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]}
.

To define these operational observables formally, we add
L=\ell\cdot\left((n+1)^{2}+1\right)
ancilla registers to
\bm{\rho}
, which we initialize to
0
. We denote by

\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{Ext}}=\bm{\rho}\otimes\ket{0^{L}}\bra{0^{L}},

where the first
\ell\cdot(n+1)^{2}
ancilla registers are denoted by
\mathsf{open}=(\mathsf{open}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{open}_{\ell})
, and these registers store the output
(\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{\ell})
generated by
\mathsf{Open}
, where
\mathbf{z}_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{(n+1)^{2}}
is stored in
\mathsf{open}_{i}
. The last
\ell
ancilla registers are denoted by
\mathsf{out}=(\mathsf{out}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{out}_{\ell})
