(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell})
generated by
\mathsf{Out}
, where
v_{i}\in\{0,1\}
is stored in register
\mathsf{out}_{i}
.

Definition B.15
.

For any
(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y})
and any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
we define the operational observables
(P_{X_{i}},P_{Z_{i}})_{i\in[\ell]}
to be

P_{X_{i}}=U_{1}^{\dagger}\mathsf{Out}_{i,1}^{\dagger}Z_{\mathsf{out}_{i}}\mathsf{Out}_{i,1}U_{1}

and

P_{Z_{i}}=U_{0}^{\dagger}\mathsf{Out}_{i,0}^{\dagger}Z_{\mathsf{out}_{i}}\mathsf{Out}_{i,0}U_{0}

where for every
i\in[\ell]
and every
b\in\{0,1\}
,

•

U_{b}
is the unitary corresponding to
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\cdot,(b,\ldots,b))
. The output is recorded in registers
\mathsf{open}
.

•

\mathsf{Out}_{i,b}
computes
\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(i,b),\cdot)
and records the output in the ancilla register
\mathsf{out}_{i}
.

•

Z_{\mathsf{out},i}
is the Pauli
Z
operator applied only on the register
\mathsf{out}_{i}
.

We next define the extractor
\mathsf{Ext}
which uses the operational observables
\{P_{X_{i}},P_{Z_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]}
, defined above.
For the sake of simplicity, we define
\mathsf{Ext}
to operate on pure states. The definition easily generalizes to mixed states by linearity.

\mathsf{Ext}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},{\ket{\varphi}})
operates as follows:

1.

Consider the operational observables
\{P_{X_{i}},P_{Z_{i}}\}_{i\in[\ell]}
corresponding to
(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y})
.

2.

Prepare the state

\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\sum_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in\{01\}^{\ell}}\ket{\vecr,\vecs}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes\ket{0^\ell}_{\cal A}\otimes\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}.

3.

Denote by

\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}=X_{\ell}^{r_{\ell}}\ldots X_{1}^{r_{1}}~\mbox{ and }\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}=Z_{\ell}^{s_{\ell}}\ldots Z_{1}^{s_{1}}.

Similarly, denote by

P_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{r}}=P_{X_{\ell}}^{r_{\ell}}\ldots P_{X_{1}}^{r_{1}}~\mbox{ and }P_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathbf{s}}=P_{Z_{\ell}}^{s_{\ell}}\ldots P_{Z_{1}}^{s_{1}}.

4.

Controlled on the values
\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}
of the
\mathsf{Coin}
register, apply
\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}
to the
{\cal A}
register and apply
P_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{r}}P_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathbf{s}}
to the
{\cal B}
register to obtain the state

\frac{1}{2^{\ell}}\sum_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}\ket{\vecr,\vecs}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\ket{0^\ell}_{\cal A}\otimes P_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{r}}P_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathbf{s}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}

5.

Apply Hadamard gates
H^{\otimes 2\ell}
to the
\mathsf{Coin}
register in the to obtain the state

\frac{1}{4^{\ell}}\sum_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}(-1)^{\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\prime}+\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\ket{\vecr',\vecs'}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{r}\ket{0^\ell}_{\cal A}\otimes P_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{r}}P_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathbf{s}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}

where

\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}r_{i}\cdot r^{\prime}_{i}~\mathsf{mod}~2~~\mbox{ and }~~\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}s_{i}\cdot s^{\prime}_{i}~\mathsf{mod}~2.

6.

Apply
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}
to the
{\cal A}
register. Note that

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\ket{0^\ell}=

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\ket{0^\ell}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\ket{0^\ell}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\ket{0^\ell}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\prime}+\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\ket{0^\ell}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\prime}+\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\ket{0^\ell}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\prime}+\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\ket{\vecs'}.

Therefore the state obtained is

\frac{1}{4^{\ell}}\sum_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}\ket{\vecr',\vecs'}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\ket{\vecs'}_{\cal A}\otimes P_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{r}}P_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathbf{s}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}

which is equal to the state

\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}^{\ell}}(\ket{0}+\ket{1})^{\otimes\ell}\right)\otimes\frac{1}{(2\sqrt{2})^{\ell}}\sum_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}\ket{\vecs'}\otimes\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\ket{\vecs'}_{\cal A}\otimes P_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{r}}P_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathbf{s}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}.

7.

Discard the first
\ell
registers to obtain the state

\frac{1}{({2\sqrt{2}})^{\ell}}\sum_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}\ket{\vecs'}\otimes\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\ket{\vecs'}_{\cal A}\otimes P_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathbf{r}}P_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathbf{s}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}.

8.

Output the state
\bm{\tau}_{{\cal A},{\cal B}}
that is the reduced state of the above on registers
{\cal A},{\cal B}
.

We next prove that

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 10\sqrt{\delta}}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma}).

(17)

To this end, for a given
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, denote by

I=\{i\in[\ell]:\mathbf{b}_{i}=0\}~\mbox{ and }~J=\{j\in[\ell]:\mathbf{b}_{j}=1\},

so that
I
and
J
partition
[\ell]
.

Next we define a new opening algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}
. We first give a “buggy” definition of
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}
, and then show how to fix it in
Remark

B.16
.
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}
on input
(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b})
does the following:

1.

Compute
\bm{\rho}_{1}=U_{0}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy}_{I},\mathsf{open}_{I}}U_{0}[\bm{\rho}]
,
where
\mathsf{open}_{I}
is the register that contains the openings
\{\mathbf{z}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, and
\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy}_{I},\mathsf{open}_{I}}
copies the content of this register to a fresh register denoted by
\mathsf{copy}_{I}
.

2.

Measure the registers
\mathsf{copy}_{I}
of
\bm{\rho}_{1}
to obtain
\{\mathbf{z}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and denote the resulting state by
\bm{\rho}_{2}
.

3.

Compute
\bm{\rho}_{3}=U_{1}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy}_{J},\mathsf{open}_{J}}U_{1}[\bm{\rho}_{2}]
,
where
\mathsf{open}_{J}
is the register that contains the openings
\{\mathbf{z}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
, and
\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy}_{J},\mathsf{open}_{J}}
copies the content of this register to a fresh register denoted by
\mathsf{copy}_{J}
.

4.

Measure the registers
\mathsf{copy}_{J}
of
\bm{\rho}_{3}
to obtain
\{\mathbf{z}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
and denote the resulting state by
\bm{\rho}_{4}
.

5.

Output
((\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{\ell}),\bm{\rho}_{4})
.

Remark B.16
.

We remark that as written,
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b})
may be rejected with high probability. The reason is that, while the standard basis openings of
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
are identical,
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}
can completely fail to open in the Hadamard basis, since after computing the standard basis openings its state becomes
U_{0}^{\dagger}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{copy}_{I},\mathsf{open}_{I}}U_{0}[\bm{\rho}]
, with the
\mathsf{copy}_{I}
registers measured. This is a disturbed state and it is no longer clear that computing the Hardamard basis opening on it will give an accepting opening.

To ensure that
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b})
is accepted with the same probability as
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b})
, up to negligible factors, we need to ensure that the state after computing the standard basis openings remains undisturbed, or at least that this disturbance is undetected by the algorithms that compute the Hadamard basis opening and verify whether this opening is valid. To achieve this we slightly modify
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}
and allow it to compute the standard basis opening using
(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1})
. We note that such opening algorithms are allowed in
Corollary

B.11
(which we will later use in our analysis).

Specifically,
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}
, rather than placing the output of
U_{0}
in the
\mathsf{open}_{I}
registers, which when measured may disturb the state, we use
(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1})
to apply the following post-opening unitary to each
\mathsf{open}_{i}
register, to ensure that when measured the disturbance will not be noticed. Recall that
\mathsf{open}_{i}
contains a vector
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{n+1})\in\{0,1\}^{(n+1)^{2}}
where each
\mathbf{z}_{j}\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}
. The post-opening unitary does the following:

1.

Coherently compute for every
j\in[n+1]
the bit
m_{j}=\mathbf{z}_{j}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j,1})
, where
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j,0}
and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j,1}
are the two preimages of
\mathbf{y}_{i,j}
that are computed using
\mathsf{sk}_{j}
.

2.

Let
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n+1})\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}
.

Note that if
\mathbf{z}
is a successful opening (i.e., it is accepted) then
\mathbf{m}
is a preimage of
\mathbf{y}_{i,0}
, and whether a preimage is measured or not is undetectable without knowing
\mathsf{sk}_{0}
, due to the collapsing property of the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family.

3.

On an ancila register, compute a super-position over all
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{n+1})\in\{0,1\}^{(n+1)^{2}}
such that for every
j\in[n+1]

m_{j}=\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{j}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j,1})
.

4.

Swap register
\mathsf{open}_{i}
with the ancila register above, so that now
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{n+1})
is in register
\mathsf{open}_{i}
.

Now we can argue that the residual state after computing the standard basis opening seems undisturbed for anyone who does not know
\mathsf{sk}_{0}
due to the collapsing property of the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family, and computing the Hadamard opening and verification of it does not use
\mathsf{sk}_{0}
(and is done publicly given only
\mathsf{pk})
.

Note that since

\delta=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell})\\
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})\end{subarray}}\max_{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\in\{\mathbf{b},{\bf 0},{\bf 1\}}}\Pr[\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b}^{\prime},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}))=0].

it holds that

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell})\\
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})\end{subarray}}\max_{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\in\{\mathbf{b},{\bf 0},{\bf 1\}}}\Pr[\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b}^{\prime},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}))=0]\leq 2\delta.

(18)

This is the case since the probability that
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b})
is rejected is bounded by the sum of the probabilities that
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},0^{\ell})
is rejected and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},1^{\ell})
is rejected.

By
Corollary

B.11
, we conclude that for every
\mathbf{b}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
,

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 6\sqrt{\delta}}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})

(19)

This implies that to prover
Equation

17
it suffices to prove

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 4\sqrt{\delta}}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})

(20)

To this end, we first compute the distribution of measurement outcomes on the extracted state. While in general the input to the extractor is a mixed state
\bm{\rho}
, we will perform the calculations for a general pure state
\ket{\varphi}
instead. The results we obtain will hold for any pure state
\ket{\varphi}
. Thus, they will extend by convexity to the post-commitment state
\bm{\rho}
as well, since we can always write
\bm{\rho}=\sum_{k}p_{k}\ket{\varphi_k}\bra{\varphi_k}
for some collection of pure states
\{\ket{\varphi_k}\}
.

As a first step in the computation, we remark that for every
i,j\in[\ell]
, it holds that
P_{Z_{i}}
and
P_{Z_{j}}
commute and
P_{X_{i}}
and
P_{X_{j}}
commute. This follows from the fact that we defined all the
P_{Z_{i}}
with respect to the same unitary
U_{0}
and defined all the
P_{X_{i}}
with respect to the same unitary
U_{1}
.
Thus, for an input state
\ket{\varphi}
, the output of the extractor can be written as

\frac{1}{{(2\sqrt{2})^{\ell}}}\sum_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}\ket{\vecs'}_{\mathsf{Coin}}\otimes\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}}\ket{\vecs'}_{\cal A}\otimes P_{\mathbf{X}_{I}}^{\mathbf{r}_{I}}P_{\mathbf{X}_{J}}^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}P_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I}}^{\mathbf{s}_{I}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}.

Measuring the
I
registers of
{\cal A}
in the standard basis.

Now, we imagine measuring the
I
registers of
{\cal A}
in the standard basis; we denote these registers by
{\cal A}_{I}
. When we measure them we obtain an outcome which we will denote
\mathbf{a}_{I}
. The unnormalized post-measurement state is obtained by applying the projector
I\otimes\ket{\veca_I}\bra{\veca_I}_{{\cal A}_{I}}
to the state, where the factor of identity acts on all registers other than
{\cal A}_{I}
. To calculate what happens, let us examine what happens when we apply the projector
\ket{\veca_I}\bra{\veca_I}
to
\mathbf{Z}^{\mathbf{s}_{I}}_{I}\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{r}_{I}}_{I}\ket{\vecs'_I}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\bra{\veca_I}\mathbf{Z}_{I}^{\mathbf{s}_{I}}\mathbf{X}_{I}^{\mathbf{r}_{I}}\ket{\vecs'_I}=

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}\bra{a_i}Z_{i}^{s_{i}}X_{i}^{r_{i}}\ket{s'_i}=

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}\bra{a_i}Z_{i}^{s_{i}}\ket{s'_i\oplus r_i}=

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}\bra{a_i}(-1)^{s_{i}\cdot a_{i}}\ket{s'_i\oplus r_i}=

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}(-1)^{s_{i}\cdot a_{i}}\bra{a_i}\ket{s'_i\oplus r_i},

where for every
i\in I
,
\bra{a_i}\ket{s'_i\oplus r_i}
is
1
if
a_{i}\oplus r_{i}=s^{\prime}_{i}
, and
0
otherwise. This means that if we obtain an outcome
\mathbf{a}_{I}
, then we force the
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{I}
register to be
\mathbf{a}_{I}\oplus\mathbf{r}_{I}
. This means that the sum over
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
collapses to a sum over
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}
, since
J
is the complement of
I
.
Thus, we obtain the unnormalized post-measurement state

\frac{1}{({2\sqrt{2}})^{\ell}}\sum_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}_{I}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{I}}\ket{\veca_I\oplus\vecr_I}_{\mathsf{Coin}_{I}}\ket{\vecs'_J}_{\mathsf{Coin}_{J}}\otimes\ket{\veca_I}_{{\cal A}_{I}}\otimes Z_{J}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}X_{J}^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}\ket{\vecs'_J}_{{\cal A}_{J}}\otimes P_{\mathbf{X}_{I}}^{\mathbf{r}_{I}}P_{\mathbf{X}_{J}}^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}P_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I}}^{\mathbf{s}_{I}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}

Note that

\frac{1}{2^{|I|}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{I}\in\{0,1\}^{|I|}}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}_{I}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{I}}P_{Z_{I}}^{\mathbf{s}_{I}}=\frac{1}{2^{|I|}}\prod_{i\in I}\sum_{s_{i}\in\{0,1\}}(-1)^{s_{i}\cdot a_{i}}P_{Z_{i}}^{s_{i}}=\prod_{i\in I}\frac{I+(-1)^{a_{i}}P_{Z_{i}}}{2}

and thus the state we obtain after the projection is equal to

\frac{1}{2^{|J|}\cdot{2}^{\ell/2}}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell},\mathbf{s}_{J},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}\ket{\veca_I\oplus\vecr_I}\otimes\ket{\vecs'_J}\otimes\ket{\veca_I}_{{\cal A}_{I}}\otimes Z_{J}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}X_{J}^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}\ket{\vecs'_J}_{{\cal A}_{J}}\otimes P_{\mathbf{X}_{I}}^{\mathbf{r}_{I}}P_{\mathbf{X}_{J}}^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}P_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}\prod_{i\in I}\left(\frac{I+(-1)^{a_{i}}P_{Z_{i}}}{2}\right)\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}.

Denoting by
\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}=\prod_{i\in I}\frac{I+(-1)^{a_{i}}P_{Z_{i}}}{2}
, the above projected state is equal to

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{\veca_I}}=\frac{1}{2^{|J|}\cdot{2}^{\ell/2}}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell},\mathbf{s}_{J},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}

\displaystyle\ket{\veca_I\oplus\vecr_I}_{\mathsf{Coin}_{I}}\otimes\ket{\vecs'_J}_{\mathsf{Coin}_{J}}

\displaystyle\otimes\ket{\veca_I}_{{\cal A}_{I}}\otimes Z_{J}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}X_{J}^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}\ket{\vecs'_J}_{{\cal A}_{J}}\otimes P_{\mathbf{X}_{I}}^{\mathbf{r}_{I}}P_{\mathbf{X}_{J}}^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}P_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}.

(21)

The square norm of this unnormalized state is the probability that the measurement returns outcome
\mathbf{a}_{I}
. We now calculate this:

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbf{a}_{I}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2|J|}\cdot 2^{\ell}}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}\|\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}Z^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}_{J}X^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}_{J}\ket{\vecs'_J}_{{\cal{A}}_{J}}\otimes P^{\mathbf{r}_{I}}_{\mathbf{X}_{I}}P^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}_{\mathbf{X}_{J}}P^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}\ket{\varphi}_{{\cal B}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2|J|}\cdot 2^{\ell}}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}\|\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}(-1)^{\mathbf{s}_{J}\cdot(\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}+\mathbf{r}_{J})}\ket{\vecs'_J + \vecr_J}_{{\cal{A}}_{J}}\otimes P^{\mathbf{r}_{I}}_{\mathbf{X}_{I}}P^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}_{\mathbf{X}_{J}}P^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}\ket{\varphi}_{{\cal B}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2|J|}\cdot 2^{\ell}}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{J},\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}(-1)^{(\mathbf{s}_{J}+\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}_{J})\cdot(\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}+\mathbf{r}_{J})}\bra{\varphi}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}P^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}_{J}}_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}P^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}\Pi_{P_{{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}}\ket{\varphi}_{{\cal B}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2|J|}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}\bra{\varphi}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}\Pi_{P_{{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}}\ket{\varphi}_{{\cal B}}

\displaystyle=\bra{\varphi}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}\Pi_{P_{{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}}\ket{\varphi}_{{\cal B}}.

(22)

Thus, we have shown that the outcome distribution from the extracted state is identical to the outcome distribution from measuring the original state
\ket{\varphi}
.

Measuring the
J
registers of
{\cal A}
in the Hadamard basis.

Now, we imagine taking the standard basis post-measurement state
\ket{\Psi_{\veca_I}}
, and then further measuring the
J
registers of
{\cal A}
in the Hadamard basis. We denote these registers by
{\cal A}_{J}
and the outcome by
\mathbf{a}_{J}
. To obtain the unnormalized post-measurement state after this measurement, we apply the projector
H^{\otimes|J|}\ket{\veca_J}\bra{\veca_J}H^{\otimes|J|}
to the
J
registers of
{\cal A}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\bra{\veca_J}H^{\otimes|J|}Z_{J}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}X_{J}^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}\ket{\vecs'_J}=

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in J}\bra{a_j}HZ_{j}^{s_{j}}X_{j}^{r_{j}}\ket{s'_j}=

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in J}\bra{a_j}HZ_{j}^{s_{j}}\ket{s'_j\oplus r_j}=

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in J}\bra{a_j}H(-1)^{s_{j}\cdot(s^{\prime}_{j}\oplus r_{j})}\ket{s'_j\oplus r_j}=

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in J}\frac{(-1)^{s_{j}\cdot(s^{\prime}_{j}\oplus r_{j})}}{\sqrt{2}}\bra{a_j}(\ket{0}+(-1)^{s^{\prime}_{j}\oplus r_{j}}\ket{1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{|J|/2}}\prod_{j\in J}(-1)^{(s_{j}\oplus a_{j})\cdot(s^{\prime}_{j}\oplus r_{j})}\triangleq\beta_{J}^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}}

Thus we obtain the state

\frac{1}{2^{|J|}\cdot{2}^{\ell/2}}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell},\mathbf{s}_{J},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}\ket{\veca_I\oplus\vecr_I}\otimes\beta_{J}^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}}\ket{\vecs'_J}\otimes\ket{\veca_I}_{{\cal A}_{I}}\otimes H^{\otimes|J|}\ket{\veca_J}_{{\cal A}_{J}}\otimes P_{\mathbf{X}_{I}}^{\mathbf{r}_{I}}P_{\mathbf{X}_{J}}^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}P_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}.

Next, we observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}}\beta_{J}^{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}}\ket{\vecs'_J}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{|J|}}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}}(-1)^{(\mathbf{s}_{J}\oplus\mathbf{a}_{J})\cdot(\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}\oplus\mathbf{r}_{J})}\ket{\vecs'_J}

(23)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\mathbf{s}_{J}\oplus\mathbf{a}_{J})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{J}}H^{\otimes|J|}\ket{\vecs_J \oplus\veca_J}

(24)

Thus, applying
Equation

24
to simplify the sum over
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}_{J}
, we can write this as

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{\veca_I, \veca_J}}=\frac{1}{2^{\ell/2}2^{|J|}}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell},\mathbf{s}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}(-1)^{(\mathbf{s}_{J}\oplus\mathbf{a}_{J})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{J}}

\displaystyle\ket{\veca_I \oplus\vecr_I}_{\mathsf{Coin}_{I}}\otimes H^{\otimes|J|}\ket{\vecs_J \oplus\veca_J}_{\mathsf{Coin}_{J}}

\displaystyle\otimes\ket{\veca_I}_{{\cal A}_{I}}\otimes H^{\otimes|J|}\ket{\veca_J}_{{\cal A}_{J}}\otimes P^{\mathbf{r}_{I}}_{\mathbf{X}_{I}}P^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}_{\mathbf{X}_{J}}P^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}_{\mathbf{Z}_{j}}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}.

Note that

\frac{1}{2^{|J|}}\sum_{\mathbf{r}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}(-1)^{(\mathbf{s}_{J}\oplus\mathbf{a}_{J})\cdot\mathbf{r}_{J}}P_{\mathbf{X}_{J}}^{\mathbf{r}_{J}}=\prod_{j\in J}\frac{I+(-1)^{s_{j}\oplus a_{j}}P_{X_{j}}}{2}.

Let us define

\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{X}_{j}},\mathbf{s}_{J}\oplus\mathbf{a}_{J}}=\prod_{j\in J}\frac{I+(-1)^{s_{j}\oplus a_{j}}P_{X_{j}}}{2}.

Then we can rewrite
\ket{\Psi_{\veca_I, \veca_J}}
as

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{\veca_I, \veca_J}}=\frac{1}{{2}^{\ell/2}}\sum_{\mathbf{r}_{I}\in\{0,1\}^{|I|},\mathbf{s}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}\ket{\veca_I\oplus\vecr_I}_{\mathsf{Coin}_{I}}

\displaystyle\otimes H^{\otimes|J|}\ket{\vecs_J\oplus\veca_J}_{\mathsf{Coin}_{J}}\otimes\ket{\veca_I}_{{\cal A}_{I}}\otimes H^{\otimes|J|}\ket{\veca_J}_{{\cal A}_{J}}

\displaystyle\otimes P_{\mathbf{X}_{I}}^{\mathbf{r}_{I}}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{X}_{J}},\mathbf{a}_{J}\oplus\mathbf{s}_{J}}P_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}.

The square norm of this unnormalized state is the probability that the measurement returns outcome
\mathbf{a}_{J}
. We now calculate this:

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbf{a}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{J}]

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{|I|}}{2^{\ell}}\left\|\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}H^{\otimes|J|}\ket{\vecs_J\oplus\veca_J}_{\mathsf{Coin}_{J}}\otimes\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{X}_{J}},\mathbf{a}_{J}\oplus\mathbf{s}_{J}}P_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{|J|}}\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}\|\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{X}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{J}\oplus\mathbf{s}_{J}}}P_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\E_{\mathbf{s}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}}\|\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{X}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{J}\oplus\mathbf{s}_{J}}}P_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}}}\ket{\varphi}_{\cal B}\|^{2}.

(25)

This equation can be interpreted operationally as follows: the probability of obtaining an outcome
(\mathbf{a}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{J})
by measuring the extracted state is
equal
to the probability of obtaining this outcome by the following procedure acting on
\ket{\varphi}
:

1.

First, measure the observables
P_{Z_{i}}
for every
i\in I
on
\ket{\varphi}
, obtaining an outcome
\mathbf{a}_{I}
.

2.

Next, sample a string
\mathbf{s}_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{|J|}
uniformly at random and apply
P_{\mathbf{Z}_{J}}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}
to the state.

3.

Next, measure the observables
P_{X_{j}}
for every
j\in J
on the state, obtaining an outcome
\mathbf{u}_{J}
.

4.

Set
\mathbf{a}_{J}=\mathbf{u}_{J}\oplus\mathbf{s}_{J}
and return
(\mathbf{a}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{J})
.

The proof of
Equation

20

We first define a new distribution, which we denote by
\widehat{\mathsf{Real}}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})
. This distribution is identical to
\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})
except that it does not run the
\mathsf{Ver}
algorithm (i.e., it does not run
Item

2
of the definition of
\mathsf{Real}
in
Definition

5.6
), and simply sets
\mathbf{m}=\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{z})
. We note that by
Lemma

3.2
,

\widehat{\mathsf{Real}}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sqrt{2\delta}}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})

(26)

where recall
2\delta
is the probability that
\mathsf{Ver}
rejects
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})
(see
Equation

18
).
Therefore to prove
Equation

20
it suffices to prove that

\widehat{\mathsf{Real}}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2\sqrt{\delta}}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})

(27)

To this end, we first claim that

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},I})\equiv(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathsf{Real}},I})

where
(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},I})
is distributed by generating

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m})\leftarrow{\mathsf{Ideal}}^{\mathsf{Ext},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})

and outputting
(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I})
, and

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathsf{Real}},I})
is distributed by generating

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m})\leftarrow\widehat{\mathsf{Real}}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})

and outputting
(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I})
.
To see why this is true, recall that
Equation

22
implies that for a given
\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b}
, the outcome
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},I}
, which is equal to
\mathbf{a}_{I}
in the notation used in that equation, is distributed according to the outcome of measuring
P_{Z_{i}}
on the qubits
i\in I
qubits of the post-commitment state. Moreover,
P_{Z_{i}}
is defined so that it exactly matches the action of
\widehat{\mathsf{Real}}
since both do not run
\mathsf{Ver}
.

Remark B.17
.

We note that the observable
P_{Z_{i}}
was defined with respect to the opening algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
and we are considering
\widehat{\mathsf{Real}}
with respect to the opening algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}
. The observable
P_{Z_{i}}
corresponding to
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
, when viewed as a unitary, is different from observable corresponding to
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}
, denoted by
P^{\prime}_{Z_{i}}
, when viewed as a unitary.
In particular, recall that

P_{Z_{i}}=U_{0}^{\dagger}\mathsf{Out}_{i,0}^{\dagger}Z_{\mathsf{out}_{i}}\mathsf{Out}_{i,0}U_{0}

whereas

P^{\prime}_{Z_{i}}=U_{0}^{\dagger}U_{\mathsf{post}}^{\dagger}\mathsf{Out}_{i,0}^{\dagger}Z_{\mathsf{out}_{i}}\mathsf{Out}_{i,0}U_{\mathsf{post}}U_{0},

where
U_{\mathsf{post}}
is the unitary that does some post-processing to the
\mathsf{open}_{i}
register to ensure that measuring it will not disturb the state in a detectable way. Despite the fact that
P_{Z_{i}}
and
P^{\prime}_{Z_{i}}
are different unitaries, on the subspace where the ancila registers are initialized to
\ket{0}
, they are identical operators. In particular,
P^{\prime}_{Z_{i}}
preserves the subspace where the ancila registers are initialized to
\ket{0}
.

To avoid cluttering of notation, from now on we denote
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathsf{Real}},I}
and
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},I}
by
\mathbf{m}_{I}
. Denote by

\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}=\Pi_{P_{\mathbf{Z}_{I}},\mathbf{m}_{I}}[\bm{\rho}]

where
\bm{\rho}
is post-commitment state and
\mathbf{m}_{I}
is distributed as
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},I}
.
We note that in the experiment
\widehat{\mathsf{Real}}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}}(\lambda,\mathbf{b},\bm{\sigma})
, the post state after measuring
\mathbf{m}_{I}
is
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}
. This follows from
Remark

B.17
.
We prove that

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathsf{Real}},J})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2\sqrt{\delta}}}{{\approx}}(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},J})

(28)

where
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathsf{Real}},J}
is obtained as follows:

1.

Compute
\mathbf{z}_{J}\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}(\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}))
.

2.

For every
j\in J
let
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathsf{Real}},j}=\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(j,1),\mathbf{z}_{j})

3.

Output
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathsf{Real}},J}=\{\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathsf{Real}},j}\}_{j\in J}
.

To describe how
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},J}
is obtained, we take the procedure obtained immediately below
Equation

25
, and apply the definitions of the operational observable
P_{X}
, to obtain the following:

1.

Sample at random
\mathbf{s}_{J}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{|J|}
.

2.

Compute
\mathbf{z}_{J}\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}(P_{Z_{J}}^{\mathbf{s}_{J}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}],(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}))
.

3.

For every
j\in J
let
u_{j}=\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(j,1),\mathbf{z}_{j})
.

4.

For every
j\in J
let
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},j}=u_{j}\oplus\mathbf{s}_{j}
.

5.

Output
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},J}=(\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},j})_{j\in J}
.

We prove
Equation

28
separately for the non-succinct and the semi-succinct versions. For the non-succinct version we rely on the adaptive hardcore bit property and for the semi-succinct version we rely on the distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit property. In both cases, we assume without loss of generality that
\mathsf{Open}_{[\ell]}
opens in the Hadamard basis honestly, by measuring the relevant qubits in the standard basis. For every
j\in J
, we denote by
{\cal O}_{j}
the
n+1
registers that are measured to obtain the opening of the
j
’th committed qubit in the Hadamard basis.

Proof of
Equation

28
in the non-succinct setting.

Let
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
denote the state
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}
projected to

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(J,1^{|J|}),\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I},(J,1^{|J|})))=1.

By
Lemma

3.2
,

\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sqrt{\delta}}}{{\equiv}}\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}.

Therefore to prove
Equation

28
it suffices to prove that

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{*}_{{\mathsf{Real}},J})\approx(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{*}_{\mathsf{Sim},J})

(29)

where
\mathbf{m}^{*}_{{\mathsf{Real}},J}
is distributed as
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathsf{Real}},J}
except that
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}
is replaced with
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
. Similarly,
\mathbf{m}^{*}_{\mathsf{Sim},J}
is distributed as
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},J}
except that
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}
is replaced with
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
.

To prove
Equation

29
it suffices to prove that

\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\{\mathbf{m}_{j,0}\}_{j\in J}\right)\approx\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\{\mathbf{m}_{j,1}\oplus 1\}_{j\in J}\right)

(30)

where for every
j\in J
and
u\in\{0,1\}
,

(\mathbf{z}_{j,u},\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j,u})={\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(P^{u}_{Z_{j}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}],(j,1))~~\mbox{ and }~~\mathbf{m}_{j,u}=\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(j,1),\mathbf{z}_{j,u}).

We prove that for every
j\in J
,

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{j,0},\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j,0}\right)\approx\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{j,1}\oplus 1,\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j,1}\right),

(31)

where
\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)}
denotes all the secret keys except
\mathsf{sk}_{j,0}
. Namely,

\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)}\triangleq\left(\mathsf{sk}_{[\ell],1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{[\ell],n+1},\mathsf{sk}_{[\ell]\setminus\{j\},0}\right).

We next argue that
Equation

31
implies
Equation

30
.
To this end, we first notice that
P_{Z_{j}}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\cdot,(j,1))
only touch the registers corresponding to the
j
’th committed qubit. This follows from our assumption that
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
behaves honestly when opening in the Hadamard basis. This in turn implies that for every
u\in\{0,1\}
it holds that
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j,u}
and
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
are distributed identically on the registers that do not correspond to the
j
’th committed qubit.
Thus,
Equation

31
implies that

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{j,0},\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]_{\{{\cal O}_{j}\}_{j\in J\setminus\{j\}}}\right)\approx\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{j,1}\oplus 1,\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]_{\{{\cal O}_{j}\}_{j\in J\setminus\{j\}}}\right).

(32)

We next note that
\mathbf{m}_{j,u}
is a
\mathsf{QPT}
function of
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[{\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}}]_{{\cal O}_{j}}
and
\mathsf{sk}_{j}
.
This, together with a hybrid argument implies that indeed

Equation

32
implies
Equation

30
, as desired.

Thus, we focus on proving
Equation

31
.
Fix
j\in J
and consider the mixed state

\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{mix,j}}=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]+\frac{1}{2}P_{Z_{j}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]

Note that this state can be generated efficiently, with probability
1-\delta
, from
\bm{\rho}
given
(\mathsf{sk}_{[\ell],1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{[\ell],n+1})
. In addition, note that
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{mix,j}}

is identical to the state
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
after measuring it in the
P_{Z_{j}}
basis.
Recall that we assume that
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
behaves honestly on the Hadamard basis. Thus, the
(n+1)^{2}
qubits of this projected state
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{mix,j}}
corresponding to the
j
’th committed qubit are in superposition over
\ket{d_1,\vecx'_1}\ldots\ket{d_{n+1},\vecx'_{n+1}}
such that for every
i\in[n+1]
,
\mathbf{y}_{j,i}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk}_{j,i},d_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i})
.

Let

(\mathbf{z}^{*},\bm{\rho}^{*})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}\left(\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{mix,j}},(j,1)\right).

By the adaptive hardcore bit property (w.r.t.
\mathsf{pk}_{j,0}
), letting
m^{*}=\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(j,1),\mathbf{z}^{*})
,

(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},m^{*},\bm{\rho}^{*})\approx(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},U,\bm{\rho}^{*})

(33)

where
U
is the uniform distribution over
\{0,1\}
.
Note that
m^{*}
is a random variable that
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
is distributed identically to
\mathbf{m}_{j,0}
and with probability
\frac{1}{2}
is distributed identically to
\mathbf{m}_{j,1}
.
We next argue that
Equation

33
implies that

\displaystyle(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{j,0},\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j,0})\approx

\displaystyle(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{j,1}\oplus 1,\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j,1}),

as desired.
To this end, suppose for contradiction that there exists a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathsf{A}
and a non-negligible
\epsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{j,1}\oplus 1,\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j,1})=1]-

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{j,0},\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j,0})=1]\geq\epsilon.

Denote by

p_{u}=\Pr[\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{j,u},\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j,u})=1]

and denote by

p^{\prime}_{1}=\Pr[\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}_{j,1}\oplus 1,\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{j,1})=1]

Note that

\Pr[\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{*},\bm{\rho}^{*})=1]=\frac{1}{2}p_{0}+\frac{1}{2}p_{1}.

On the other hand

\Pr[\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},U,\bm{\rho}_{j,1}^{\prime})=1]=\frac{1}{2}p^{\prime}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}p_{1},

which by the collapsing property implies that there exists a negligible function
\mu
such that

\Pr[\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk}_{-(j,0)},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},U,\bm{\rho}^{*})=1]=\frac{1}{2}p^{\prime}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}p_{1}\pm\mu

This contradicts
Equation

33
since

\left(\frac{1}{2}p^{\prime}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}p_{1}\pm\mu\right)-\left(\frac{1}{2}p_{0}+\frac{1}{2}p_{1}\right)=\frac{1}{2}(p^{\prime}_{1}-p_{0})-\mu\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\pm\mu,

which is non-negligible.

The proof of
Equation

28
for the semi-succinct version.

We prove
Equation

28
holds if the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family satisfies the strong adaptive hardcore bit property (see
Definition

3.8
) and the distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit property (
Definition

4.1
).
Let
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
denote the state
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}
projected to

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(J,0^{|J|}),{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I},(J,0^{|J|})))=1.

By
Lemma

3.2
,

\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sqrt{\delta}}}{{\equiv}}\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}.

Therefore to prove
Equation

28
it suffices to prove that

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{*}_{{\mathsf{Real}},J})\approx(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{*}_{\mathsf{Sim},J})

(34)

where
\mathbf{m}^{*}_{{\mathsf{Real}},J}
is distributed as
\mathbf{m}_{\widehat{\mathsf{Real}},J}
except that
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}
is replaced with
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
. Similarly,
\mathbf{m}^{*}_{\mathsf{Sim},J}
is distributed as
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},J}
except that
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}
is replaced with
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
.

We prove
Equation

34
via a hybrid argument. Namely, denoting by
k=|J|
, we prove that for every
\alpha\in[k]
,

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{(\alpha-1)}_{J}\right)\approx\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{(\alpha)}_{J}\right)

(35)

where
\mathbf{m}^{(\beta)}_{J}
is distributed exactly as
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Sim},J}
accept that rather than choosing
\mathbf{s}_{J}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{|J|}
at random, we only choose the first
\beta
coordinates randomly and the rest we set to zero. Namely, we choose
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{\beta}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and set
\mathbf{s}_{\beta+1}=\ldots=\mathbf{s}_{k}=0
.

Equation

35
implies that

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{(0)}_{J}\right)\approx\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{(k)}_{J}\right)

thus proving
Equation

30
since

\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{(0)}_{J}\right)\equiv\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{*}_{\widehat{\mathsf{Real}},J}\right)~~\mbox{ and }~~\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{(k)}_{J}\right)\equiv\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{*}_{\mathsf{Sim},J}\right).

Fix any
\alpha\in[k]
and we prove
Equation

35
for this
\alpha
. Denote by

J=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\}\subseteq[\ell]
. For every
\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{\alpha-1})\in\{0,1\}^{\alpha-1}
consider the states

\bm{\rho}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}=\prod_{i=1}^{\alpha-1}P^{s_{i}}_{Z_{j_{i}}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]~~\mbox{ and }~~\bm{\rho}^{*}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}=\frac{1}{2}\bm{\rho}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}+\frac{1}{2}P_{Z_{j_{\alpha}}}[\bm{\rho}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}].

(36)

The main ingredient in the proof of
Equation

35
is following proposition.

Proposition B.18
.

\displaystyle(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},(\mathbf{x}_{j_{i},0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{j_{i},1})_{i\in[k]},\mathbf{d}^{*}_{J\setminus\{j_{\alpha}\}},\mathbf{d}^{*}_{j_{\alpha}}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{j_{\alpha},0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{j_{\alpha},1}))\approx

(37)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},(\mathbf{x}_{j_{i},0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{j_{i},1})_{i\in[k]},\mathbf{d}^{*}_{J\setminus\{j_{\alpha}\}},U)

where
\mathbf{d}^{*}_{J}
is distributed by measuring the registers
{\cal O}_{j_{1}},\ldots,{\cal O}_{j_{k}}
of
\bm{\rho}^{*}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}
in the standard basis.

In what follows, we use
Proposition

B.18
to prove
Equation

35
and then we prove
Proposition

B.18
. To this end, for every
u\in\{0,1\}
, denote by

\mathbf{d}^{(u)}_{J}=\left(\mathbf{d}^{(u)}_{j_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{d}^{(u)}_{j_{k}}\right)

the values obtained by measuring registers
{\cal O}_{j_{1}},\ldots,{\cal O}_{j_{k}}
of
P^{(u)}_{Z_{j_{\alpha}}}[\bm{\rho}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}]
in the standard basis. For every
i\in[k]
, denote by

m^{(u)}_{j_{i}}=\mathbf{d}^{(u)}_{j_{i}}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{j_{i},0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{j_{i},0})~~\mbox{ and }~~m^{*}_{j_{i}}=\mathbf{d}^{*}_{j_{i}}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{j_{i},0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{j_{i},0})

and let

\mathbf{m}^{(u)}=\left(m^{(u)}_{j_{1}},\ldots,m^{(u)}_{j_{k}}\right)~~\mbox{ and }~~\mathbf{m}^{*}=\left(m^{*}_{j_{1}},\ldots,m^{*}_{j_{k}}\right).

Fix any
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary
\mathsf{A}
. For every
u\in\{0,1\}
denote by

p_{u}=\Pr[\mathsf{A}\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{(u)}_{J}\right)=1],

and denote by

p^{\prime}_{1}=\Pr[\mathsf{A}\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\left\{\mathbf{m}^{(1)}_{J\setminus\{\alpha\}},m^{(1)}_{\alpha}\oplus 1\right\}\right)=1].

To prove
Equation

35
we need to prove that

|p^{\prime}_{1}-p_{0}|\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

(38)

Note that

\Pr\left[\mathsf{A}\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{*}_{J}\right)=1\right]=\frac{1}{2}p_{0}+\frac{1}{2}p_{1}

which by
Proposition

B.18
implies that

\Pr[\mathsf{A}\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\left\{\mathbf{m}^{*}_{J\setminus\{\alpha\}},U\right\}\right)=1]=\frac{1}{2}p_{0}+\frac{1}{2}p_{1}\pm\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

In addition note that

\Pr[\mathsf{A}\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\left\{\mathbf{m}^{(1)}_{J\setminus\{\alpha\}},U\right\}\right)=1]=\frac{1}{2}p^{\prime}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}p_{1}.

It remains to note that

\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{*}_{J\setminus\{\alpha\}}\right)\equiv\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{I},\mathbf{m}^{(1)}_{J\setminus\{\alpha\}}\right),

which together with the equation above, implies that
Equation

38
indeed holds,
thus proving
Equation

35
, as desired.

The rest of the proof is dedicated to proving
Proposition

B.18
, which we prove assuming the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family has the strong adaptive hardcore bit property (see
Definition

3.8
) and the distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit property (
Definition

4.1
).

Proof of
Proposition

B.18
.

We start by defining
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms
\mathsf{A}
and
C
for the distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit property.

Algorithm
\mathsf{A}
.

It takes as on input
\mathsf{pk}_{0}
generated by
\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(1^{\lambda})
, and does the following:

1.

For every
i\in[n+1]
generate
(\mathsf{pk}_{i},\mathsf{sk}_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{NTCF}}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Set
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1})
.

3.

Compute
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
.

4.

Use
\mathsf{sk}_{1}
to generate

\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}=\Pi_{P_{Z_{I}},\mathbf{m}_{I}}(\bm{\rho})
.

5.

Use
\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1}
to compute
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
, which is the state
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}
projected to

\mathsf{Ver}\left(\left(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1}\right),\mathbf{y},\left(J,0^{|J|}\right),{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}\left(\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I},\left(J,0^{|J|}\right)\right)\right)=1.

This step fails with probability
\delta
, in which case
\mathsf{A}
outputs
\bot
.

6.

Use
\mathsf{sk}_{1}
to compute
\bm{\rho}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}=\prod_{i=1}^{\alpha-1}P^{s_{i}}_{Z_{j_{i}}}\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
.

7.

Use
\mathsf{sk}_{1}
to compute
\bm{\rho}^{*}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}=\frac{1}{2}\bm{\rho}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}+\frac{1}{2}P_{Z_{\alpha}}[\bm{\rho}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}]
.

8.

Parse
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell})
and parse
\mathbf{y}_{j_{\alpha}}=(\mathbf{y}_{j_{\alpha},0},\mathbf{y}_{j_{\alpha},1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{j_{\alpha},n+1})

9.

Measure the
P_{Z_{j_{\alpha}}}
observable of the state
\bm{\rho}^{*}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}
to obtain a preimage
(b,\mathbf{x}_{j_{\alpha},b})
of
\mathbf{y}_{j_{\alpha},0}
.

Denote the post-measurement state by
\bm{\rho}^{**}
. We assume without loss of generality that this state includes
(\mathsf{pk},(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1}),\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m}_{I})
.

10.

Output
(\mathbf{y}_{j_{\alpha},0},b,\mathbf{x}_{j_{\alpha},b},\bm{\rho}^{**})
.

We rename the register
{\cal O}_{j_{\alpha}}
of
\bm{\rho}^{**}
by
{\cal O}_{1}
and rename all the other registers by
{\cal O}_{2}
.

Algorithm
C
.

It takes as input the secret vector
\mathsf{sk}_{0,\mathsf{pre}}
, corresponding to
\mathsf{sk}_{0}
, and the
{\cal O}_{2}
registers of
\bm{\rho}^{**}
, denoted by
\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}}^{**}
, and does the following:

1.

For every
i\in[k]\setminus\{\alpha\}
use
\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1}
to coherently compute a preimage of
\mathbf{y}_{i,0}
as follows:

•

Coherently compute
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho}^{**},(j_{i},0))
.

•

Coherently run
\mathsf{Ver}((\mathsf{sk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{sk}_{n+1}),\mathbf{y}_{j_{i}},(j_{i},0),\cdot)
to to coherently generate a preimage.

Note that since the state
\Pi_{\mathsf{Ver}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
is the state
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}
projected to the state where
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
is always accepted when the opening is in the standard basis, we indeed get a coherent preimage of
\mathbf{y}_{j_{i},0}
with probability
1
.

2.

Use
\mathsf{sk}_{0,\mathsf{pre}}
to compute (and measure)
\mathbf{x}_{j_{i},0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{j_{i},1}
.

Note that this is a deterministic quantity and hence does not disturb the state.

3.

Measure all the registers in
{\cal O}_{2}
corresponding to
\{{\cal O}_{j_{i}}\}_{i\in[k]\setminus\{\alpha\}}
to obtain
\mathbf{d}^{*}_{J\setminus\{j_{\alpha}\}}
.

4.

Output
\mathsf{aux}=\left(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{m}_{I},(x_{j_{i},0}\oplus x_{j_{i},1})_{i\in[k]\setminus\{\alpha\}},\mathbf{d}^{*}_{J\setminus\{j_{\alpha}\}}\right)
.

Denote the residual state by
{\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}^{**}}
.

To finish the proof of
Proposition

B.18
it remains to prove that
\mathbf{d}^{*}_{j_{\alpha}}
satisfies the desired min-entropy requirement.
This is captured in the proposition below.

Proposition B.19
.

With overwhelming probability over
\mathsf{aux}\leftarrow C(\mathsf{sk}_{0,\mathsf{pre}},{\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}}^{**}})
it holds that for every
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{k}
,

\Pr[\mathsf{Good}(\mathbf{x}_{j_{\alpha},0},\mathbf{x}_{j_{\alpha},1},\mathbf{d}^{*}_{j_{\alpha}})=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}]=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

where
\mathbf{d}^{*}_{j_{\alpha}}
is obtained by measuring the
{\cal O}_{1}
registers of
{\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}^{**}}
in the standard basis.

The proof of
Proposition

B.19
relies on the adaptive hardcore bit property of the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family. It also makes use the following fact, which was used previously, and which follows immediately from the definition of
P_{Z_{j}}
.
38
38

38

Recall that
P_{Z_{j}}
does not check if
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(j,0),\cdot)=1
.

Fact B.20
.

For every
\alpha\in[k]
and every
\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}\in\{0,1\}^{\alpha-1}
, the state
\bm{\rho}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}
and the state
\bm{\rho}^{*}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}
can be efficiently constructed from
(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})
and
\mathsf{sk}_{1}
.

Proof of
Proposition

B.19
.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there is a non-negligible
\epsilon=\epsilon(\lambda)
such that with probability
\epsilon
over
(\mathsf{aux},{\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}^{**}})\leftarrow C(\mathsf{sk}_{0,\mathsf{pre}},{\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}}^{**}})
it holds that there exists a vector
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{aux}}\in\{0,1\}^{k}
such that

\Pr[\mathsf{Good}(\mathbf{x}_{j_{\alpha},0},\mathbf{x}_{j_{\alpha},1},\mathbf{d}^{*}_{j_{\alpha}})=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}]\geq\epsilon

(39)

where
\mathbf{d}^{*}_{j_{\alpha}}
is obtained by measuring the
{\cal O}_{1}
registers of
{\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}^{**}}
in the standard basis.
Denote the set of all
\mathsf{aux}
that satisfy
Equation

39
by
\mathsf{BAD}
.
In the rest of this proof, for the sake of ease of notation, we denote by

\mathbf{d}^{*}\triangleq\mathbf{d}^{*}_{j_{\alpha}}~~\mbox{and }~~(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1})\triangleq(\mathbf{x}_{j_{\alpha},0},\mathbf{x}_{j_{\alpha},1}).

We next denote by

\mathbf{d}^{*}=(d^{*}_{0},d^{*}_{1},\ldots,d^{*}_{n})

and we argue that there exists a subset

S=\left\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{m}\right\}\subseteq[n]

(40)

of size
m=k^{0.1}
(where
k=|\mathbf{d}^{\prime}|
) for which there exists an (all powerful) algorithm
\mathsf{B}
such that for every
\mathsf{aux}\in\mathsf{BAD}
and for every
i\in[m]

\Pr[\mathsf{B}\left(\mathsf{aux},\left(d^{*}_{1},\ldots,d^{*}_{j^{\prime}_{\beta_{i}}-1}\right)\right)=d^{*}_{j^{\prime}_{\beta_{i}}}]\geq 1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}

(41)

where the probability is over
\mathbf{d}^{*}
obtained by measuring the
{\cal O}_{1}
registers of
{\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}^{**}}

in the standard basis.
The existence of such a set
S
follows from

Equation

39
, since otherwise, by the fact that each coordinate of
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
is of the form
\mathbf{a}[i]\cdot\mathbf{d}[i]
, where
\mathbf{a}_{i}
is a non-zero vector and
\mathbf{d}[1],\ldots,\mathbf{d}[k]
forms a partition of the coordinates of
\mathbf{d}
, it holds that for every
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
,

\Pr[\mathsf{Good}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{d}^{*})=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}]\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\right)^{k-m}=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),

contradicting
Equation

39
.

We construct
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathsf{A}
that on input
(\mathsf{pk}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1})
generates with non-negligible probability images
\left\{\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i}\right\}_{i\in S}
corresponding to
\{\mathsf{pk}_{i+1}\}_{i\in S}
, preimages
\{(d^{*}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i})\}_{i\in S}
, and equations
\{\mathbf{z}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{i}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{i,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{i,1})\}_{i\in S}
, such that each
\mathbf{z}_{i}\in\mathsf{Good}_{\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{sk}_{i},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i})}
and
(\mathbf{x}_{i,0},\mathbf{x}_{i,1})=\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{sk}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{i})
, thus breaking the adaptive hardcore bit property. Algorithm
\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1})
does the following:

1.

Sample
(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\mathsf{sk}_{0}),(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\mathsf{sk}_{1})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Let
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\mathsf{pk}_{1},\mathsf{pk}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1})
.

3.

Generate
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
.

4.

Parse
\mathbf{y}=\left(\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell}\right)
and parse
\mathbf{y}_{j_{\alpha}}=\left(\mathbf{y}^{*}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}^{*}_{n+1}\right)
.

5.

Compute
((0,\mathbf{x}_{0}),(1,\mathbf{x}_{1}))\leftarrow\mathsf{Invert}\left(\mathsf{sk}_{0},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{0}\right)
.

6.

Use
\mathsf{sk}_{1}
to generate

\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}=\Pi_{P_{Z_{I}},\mathbf{m}_{I}}(\bm{\rho})
.

7.

Use
\mathsf{sk}_{1}
to compute
\bm{\rho}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}=\prod_{i=1}^{\alpha-1}P^{s_{i}}_{Z_{j_{i}}}[\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{I}]
.

8.

Use
\mathsf{sk}_{1}
to compute
\bm{\rho}^{*}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}=\frac{1}{2}\bm{\rho}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}+\frac{1}{2}P_{Z_{\alpha}}[\bm{\rho}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}]
.

9.

Measure registers
{\cal O}_{j_{\alpha}}
of
\bm{\rho}^{*}_{\mathbf{s}_{[\alpha-1]}}
in the standard basis to obtain
\mathbf{d}^{*}=(d^{*}_{0},d^{*}_{1},\ldots,d^{*}_{n})
.

10.

For every
i\in S
measure the registers corresponding to
\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i}
. We note that with probability
1-\delta
it holds that for every
i\in S
,

\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i+1}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk}_{i+1},d^{*}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i}).

Denote the resulting state by
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}
.

11.

Compute
(\mathbf{z},\bm{\rho}^{\prime\prime})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}(\mathsf{pk},(j_{\alpha},0),\bm{\rho}^{\prime})
.

12.

Parse
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{n+1})\in\{0,1\}^{(n+1)^{2}}
.

13.

Compute
(0,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1,0}),(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1,1}))\leftarrow\mathsf{Invert}\left(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{1}\right)
.

14.

Let
\gamma=\mathbf{z}_{1}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1,1})
.

15.

Output
\left\{\mathsf{pk}_{i+1},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i+1},d^{*}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{i+1},\mathbf{x}_{\gamma,i}\right\}_{i\in S}
.

We next argue that with non-negligible probability it holds that for every
i\in S
:

\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i+1}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk}_{i+1},(d^{*}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i}))~~\wedge~~\mathbf{z}_{i+1}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1})=\mathbf{x}_{\gamma,i}~~\wedge~~\mathbf{z}_{i+1}\in\mathsf{Good}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1}},

where
((0,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0}),(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1}))=\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{sk}_{i+1},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i+1})
,
contradicting the adaptive hardcore bit property.

The fact that the underlying
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}
algorithm is accepted by
\mathsf{Ver}
with probability
\geq 1-\delta
implies that

\Pr[\forall i\in S:~\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i+1}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk}_{i+1},(d^{*}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i}))]\geq 1-\delta.

Moreover, if we did not measure
\{(d^{*}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
it would also imply that,

\Pr[\forall i\in S:~\mathbf{z}_{i+1}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1})=\mathbf{x}_{\gamma,i}~~\wedge~~\mathbf{z}_{i+1}\in\mathsf{Good}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1}}]\geq 1-\delta

By the collapsing property applied to
\{\mathsf{pk}_{i}\}_{i\in\{2,\ldots,n+1\}\setminus\{i+1:~i\in S\}}
, even if we measured
\{(d^{*}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i})\}_{i\in[n]\setminus S}
,

\Pr[\forall i\in S:~\mathbf{z}_{i+1}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1})=\mathbf{x}_{\gamma,i}~~\wedge~~\mathbf{z}_{i}\in\mathsf{Good}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1}}]\geq 1-2\delta-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

We note however, that by
Equation

41
, for every
i\in S
measuring
(d^{*}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i})
disturbs the state by at most
k^{-1/4}
.
Hence

\displaystyle\Pr[\forall i\in S:~\mathbf{y}^{*}_{i+1}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk}_{i+1},(\mathbf{d}^{*}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i}))~~\wedge~~\mathbf{z}_{i+1}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1})=\mathbf{x}_{\gamma,i}~~\wedge~~\mathbf{z}_{i+1}\in\mathsf{Good}_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,0},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i,1}}]\geq

\displaystyle 1-2\delta-\frac{k^{0.1}}{k^{1/4}}-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),

contradicting the adaptive hardcore bit property.

∎

∎

Remark B.21
.

We next argue that relying on some form of the adaptive hardcore bit is necessary. Specifically, if there exists a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathsf{A}
and a non-negligible function
\epsilon=\epsilon(\lambda)
such that

\Pr[\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1})=(b_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{d}_{i},m_{i})_{i=1}^{n+1}~:~\forall i\in[n+1]~~\mathbf{d}_{i}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}_{i,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}_{i,1})=m_{i}]\geq\epsilon(\lambda)

where
(\mathbf{x}_{i,0},\mathbf{x}_{i,1})=\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{sk}_{i},\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk}_{i},(b_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}))
,
then one can use this adversary
\mathsf{A}
to attack the scheme, as follows:

1.

Given
(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1})
, compute

(b_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{d}_{i},m_{i})_{i=1}^{n+1}=\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{pk}_{n+1}).

2.

Let
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n+1})
and set
\mathbf{y}_{0}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk}_{0},\mathbf{m})
.

3.

For every
i\in[n+1]
, set
\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk}_{i},(b_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}))
.

4.

Output
(\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{n+1})
as the commitment.

5.

If asked to open in the Hadamard basis output
((b_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1}),\ldots,(b_{n+1},\mathbf{x}_{n+1}))
.

6.

If asked to open in the Standard basis output
(\mathbf{d}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{d}_{n+1})
.

The adversary is accepted with probability
\epsilon
and the openings are distinguishable from a qubit, since the standard basis opening is deterministic and the Hadamard opening is biased, both in a detectable way.

B.3

In this section we prove the following theorem.

.

The succinct multi-qubit commitment scheme described in

.

If

Specifically, Denoting

A.

Coherently compute for every

, where

B.

Let
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n+1})\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}
.
Note that if
\mathbf{z}
is a successful opening (i.e., it is accepted) then

is a preimage of
\mathbf{y}_{i,0}
, and whether a preimage is measured or not is undetectable without knowing

, due to the collapsing property of the underlying
\mathsf{NTCF}
family.

C.

On an ancila register, compute a super-position over all
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{n+1})\in\{0,1\}^{(n+1)^{2}}
such that for every
j\in[n+1]

m_{j}=\mathbf{z}^{\prime}_{j}\cdot(1,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j,0}\oplus\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{j,1})
.

D.

Swap register
\mathsf{open}_{i}
with the ancila register above, so that now

is in register
\mathsf{open}_{i}
.

(b)

Compute
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}=U^{\dagger}_{b}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{open}_{j},\mathsf{copy}_{j}}U_{b}[\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}}]

(c)

Measure register
\mathsf{copy}_{j}
in the standard basis to obtain

.

(d)

Output
\mathbf{z}_{j}
.

So far, we defined

. We define

42

Output
\bm{\tau}_{{\cal A},{\cal B}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ext}_{\mathsf{ss}}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}})

We need to argue that for every
\mathsf{QPT}

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Commit},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta^{*}}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext}_{\mathsf{ss}},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Commit},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})

where
\eta^{*}\leq C\cdot\sqrt{\delta^{*}}

and

\delta^{*}=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})\\
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})\end{subarray}}\max_{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\in\{\mathbf{b}_{J},{\bf 0}^{|J|},{\bf 1}^{|J|}\}}\Pr[\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathsf{ss}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},(J,\mathbf{b}^{\prime}),{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b}^{\prime}))=0].

),

\delta^{*}\leq\epsilon^{*}_{0}+\epsilon^{*}_{1}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

(54)

where

\epsilon^{*}_{b}=\Pr[\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathsf{ss}}(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathbf{y},(J,b^{|J|}),\mathbf{z}_{b})=0],

and where
\mathbf{y}
is distributed as in

, and
\mathbf{z}_{b}
is distributed as in
