when computed coherently by
U_{b}
.
We next argue that

\epsilon^{*}_{0}+\epsilon^{*}_{1}\leq\Pr[\mathbf{y}=\bot]+\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{0}=\bot]+\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{1}=\bot]+2\delta^{\prime}_{0}+8\epsilon_{0}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

(55)

The reason
Equation

55
holds is that after extracting
\mathbf{y}
the residual state is
\epsilon_{0}
-indistinguishable from the state obtained without extraction. After further extracting
\mathbf{z}_{b}
the residual state is
2\epsilon_{0}
-indistinguishable from the state obtained without extraction. By
Definitions

A.5
and
A.6
, this implies that the probability that in the third argument-of-knowledge, the extractor outputs a valid witness
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}_{b})
, corresponding to the instance
(\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt},\mathsf{rt}^{\prime},b)
, is at most
2\epsilon_{0}+\delta^{\prime}_{0,b}+2\epsilon_{0}=\delta^{\prime}_{0,b}+4\epsilon_{0}
, up to negligible factors, where
\delta^{\prime}_{0,b}
is the probability that
P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}
is rejected given that the fist message sent by
V_{\mathsf{Test}}
is
b\in\{0,1\}
. This, together with the collision resistance property of the underlying hash family, and with the fact that
\delta^{\prime}_{0,0}+\delta^{\prime}_{0,1}=2\delta^{\prime}_{0}
, implies that
Equation

55
indeed holds.

Note that

\Pr[\mathbf{y}=\bot]\leq\delta_{0}+2\epsilon_{0}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

(56)

This follows from the following calculation:

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbf{y}=\bot]=

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbf{y}=\bot~\wedge~\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}}~\mbox{ is rejecting}]+\Pr[\mathbf{y}=\bot~\wedge~\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}}~\mbox{ is accepting}]\leq

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}}~\mbox{ is rejecting}]+\Pr[\mathbf{y}=\bot~\wedge~\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}}~\wedge~\mbox{ is accepting}]\leq

\displaystyle\delta_{0}+\epsilon_{0}+\epsilon_{0}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

where the latter equation follows from the definition of
\delta_{0}
and from
Definition

A.5
. Similarly,

\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{b}=\bot]\leq\delta^{\prime}_{0,b}+3\epsilon_{0}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

(57)

This follows from the following calculation:

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{b}=\bot]=

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{b}=\bot~\wedge~\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}}~\mbox{ is rejecting}]+\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{b}=\bot~\wedge~\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}}~\mbox{ is accepting}]\leq

\displaystyle\Pr[\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}}~\mbox{ is rejecting}]+\Pr[\mathbf{z}_{b}=\bot~\wedge~\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}}\mbox{ is accepting}]\leq

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}_{0,b}+2\epsilon_{0}+\epsilon_{0}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

This, together with
Equations

54
and
55
, implies that

\delta^{*}\leq(\delta_{0}+2\epsilon_{0})+(2\delta^{\prime}_{0}+6\epsilon_{0})+2\delta^{\prime}_{0}+8\epsilon_{0}=\delta_{0}+4\delta^{\prime}_{0}+16\epsilon_{0}.

We conclude that

\eta^{*}\leq O(\sqrt{\delta_{0}+\delta^{\prime}_{0}})+C\cdot\sqrt{16\epsilon_{0}}\leq O(\sqrt{\delta_{0}+\delta^{\prime}_{0}})+\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

In order to use
Equation

52
, with
\eta^{*}
as above, we define

\mathsf{Succ}\mbox{-}\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext}_{\mathsf{ss}},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Commit}_{\epsilon_{0}},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})

to be the distribution obtained by sampling

(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\mathbf{y},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{m})\leftarrow\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext}_{\mathsf{ss}},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Commit}_{\epsilon_{0}},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma}),

sampling
\mathsf{hk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{H}}(1^{\lambda})
, computing
\mathsf{rt}=\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{y})
, and outputing

((\mathsf{pk}_{1},\mathsf{hk}),\mathsf{rt},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{m}_{J}).

Similarly, we define

\mathsf{Succ}\mbox{-}\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Commit},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})

to be the distribution obtained by sampling

(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\mathbf{y},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{m})\leftarrow\mathsf{Real}^{\mathsf{Ext}_{\mathsf{ss}},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Commit}_{\epsilon_{0}},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma}),

sampling
\mathsf{hk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}_{\mathsf{H}}(1^{\lambda})
, computing
\mathsf{rt}=\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{y})
, and outputting

((\mathsf{pk}_{1},\mathsf{hk}),\mathsf{rt},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\mathbf{m}_{J}).

Note that by the definition of the extractor
\mathsf{Ext}
it holds that

\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma},\epsilon)\equiv\mathsf{Succ}\mbox{-}\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext}_{\mathsf{ss}},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Commit}_{\epsilon_{0}},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma}).

This is the case since

\mathsf{Ideal}^{\mathsf{Ext},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma},\epsilon)

extracts the state

\bm{\tau}_{{\cal A},{\cal B}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ext}_{\mathsf{ss}}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}}),

where
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Commit}_{\epsilon_{0}}(\mathsf{pk}_{1},\bm{\sigma})
.

Therefore, to prove
Equation

51
it suffices to prove that

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\zeta^{*}}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Succ}\mbox{-}\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Commit},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma}),

(58)

where
\zeta^{*}=O(\sqrt{\delta_{0}+\delta^{\prime}_{o}+\delta})+\frac{\epsilon}{2}
.

To this end, we use
P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}
to define a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
{\mathsf{C}}^{**}.\mathsf{Open}
. We mention that
{\mathsf{C}}^{**}.\mathsf{Open}
bears similarity to
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}
(defined in
Item

5
of the definition of
\mathsf{Ext}
), with the difference being that the latter was defined for the semi-succinct commitment, whereas
{\mathsf{C}}^{**}.\mathsf{Open}
is defined for the succinct commitment. In particular, recall that for the succinct commitment, an opening to the
j
’th qubit consists of a tuple
(\mathbf{y}_{j},\mathbf{o}_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j})
.
{\mathsf{C}}^{**}.\mathsf{Open}
uses
P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}
to generate this opening as follows:

1.

Use the state-preservation extractor
{\cal E}
(from
Definition

A.5
) for the
\mathsf{NP}
language
{\cal L}^{*}
(defined in
Equation

15
) to generate

(\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}})\leftarrow{\cal E}^{P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}},\bm{\rho}}\left((\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt}),1^{\lambda},\epsilon_{0}\right).

Let
(\mathbf{y}_{j},\mathbf{o}_{j})=\mathsf{Open}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{y},j)
.

2.

Let
U_{b}
be the unitary as defined in the definition of the extractor
\mathsf{Ext}
above.

3.

Compute
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}=U^{\dagger}_{b}\mathsf{CNOT}_{\mathsf{open}_{j},\mathsf{copy}_{j}}U_{b}[\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post}\mathsf{Sim}}]
.

4.

Measure register
\mathsf{copy}_{j}
in the standard basis to obtain
\mathbf{z}_{j}
.

5.

Output
(\mathbf{y}_{j},\mathbf{o}_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j})
.

Proposition B.23
.

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\zeta_{1}}}{{\approx}}\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},{\mathsf{C}}^{**}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})

where
\zeta_{1}=O\left(\sqrt{\delta_{0}+\delta^{\prime}_{0}+\delta}\right)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}
.

We note that
Proposition

B.23
completes the proof of
Equation

58
since by the definition of
{\mathsf{C}}^{**}.\mathsf{Open}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}

\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit},P^{*},{\mathsf{C}}^{**}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma})\equiv\mathsf{Succ}\mbox{-}\mathsf{Real}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Commit}_{\epsilon_{0}},{\mathsf{C}}^{*}_{\mathsf{ss}}.\mathsf{Open}}(\lambda,(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}),\bm{\sigma}).

Proof of
Proposition

B.23
.

Equation

11
(which we proved above) implies that it suffices to prove the following:

\Pr[\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(J,b_{J}),(\mathbf{y}_{J},\mathbf{o}_{J},\mathbf{z}_{J}))=0]\leq O({\delta_{0}+\delta^{\prime}_{0}})+\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2C}\right)^{2}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

(59)

where
(\mathbf{y}_{J},\mathbf{o}_{J},\mathbf{z}_{J})={\mathsf{C}}^{**}.\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post}},(J,\mathbf{b}_{J}))
and where
C\in\mathbb{N}
is defined in
Item

1
of the definition of
\mathsf{Ext}
.
We first note that by
Definitions

A.5
and
A.6
,

\Pr[\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt},J,\mathbf{y}_{J},\mathbf{o}_{J})=0]\leq\delta_{0}+2\epsilon_{0}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

(60)

Moreover, the residual state, denoted by
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}}
satisfies that

(\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}},\mathsf{sk})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon_{0}}}{{\approx}}(\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post}},\mathsf{sk})

(61)

which implies that
P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}})
, upon receiving
b\in\{0,1\}
from
V_{\mathsf{Test}}
is rejected with probability at most
\delta^{\prime}_{0,b}+\epsilon_{0}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
. By
Definitions

A.5
and
A.6
, this implies that the tuple

(\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{Sim}},\mathbf{z}_{b},\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}})

generated in
Equation

50
satisfies that

\Pr[\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{z}_{b})\neq\mathsf{rt}^{\prime}_{b}]\leq\delta^{\prime}_{0,b}+3\epsilon_{0}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

By the union bound, we conclude that for every
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\Pr[\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{y})\neq\mathsf{rt}~~\vee~~\mathsf{Eval}_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{hk},\mathbf{z}_{b})\neq\mathsf{rt}^{\prime}_{b}]\leq\delta_{0}+\delta^{\prime}_{0,b}+5\epsilon_{0}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

(62)

By
Definition

A.5
, for every
b\in\{0,1\}
it holds that the state
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}}
, generated in
Equation

50
as part of
U_{b}
, is
\epsilon_{0}
-indistinguishable from the state of
P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}},b)
after executing the first state-preserving argument-of-knowledge. This, together with
Equation

61
, implies that the state
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}}
is
2\epsilon_{0}
-indistinguishable from the state of
P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post}})
after executing the first state-preserving argument-of-knowledge. Since
P^{*}_{\mathsf{Test}}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{rt},\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{post}},b)
is accepted in both its state-preserving argument-of-knowledge protocols with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}_{0,b}
, it holds that it is accepted in the second state-preserving argument-of-knowledge protocol (w.r.t. the language
{\cal L}^{**}
) when it starts with the state
\bm{\rho}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{post},\mathsf{Sim}}
with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}_{0,b}-2\epsilon_{0}
.
This, together with
Definitions

A.5
and
A.6
, implies that

\Pr[((\mathsf{sk}_{1},\mathsf{hk},\mathsf{rt},\mathsf{rt}^{\prime},b),(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}))\in\mathcal{R}_{{\cal L}^{**}}]\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}_{0,b}-4\epsilon_{0},

which together with
Equation

62
and the collision resistant property of the underlying hash family implies that

\Pr[\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathsf{rt},(J,b_{J}),(\mathbf{y}_{J},\mathbf{o}_{J},\mathbf{z}_{J}))=0]\leq O(\delta_{0}+\delta^{\prime}_{0})+9\epsilon_{0}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

Thus it remains to note that

9\epsilon_{0}\leq\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2C}\right)^{2}
, as desired.

∎

Appendix C
Applications

C.1
Succinct Interactive Arguments for
QMA

In this section we construct a succinct interactive argument for
QMA
. To this end, we construct a
semi-succinct
interactive argument for
QMA
, where only the verifier’s messages are short but the messages from the prover may be long.
We then rely on a black-box transformation from
[
BKL+22
]
which shows a generic transformation for converting any post-quantum computationally sound semi-succinct interactive argument for
QMA
into a fully succinct one.

We refer to
[
BKL+22
, Definition 3.1]
for a formal definition of a succinct argument system. That definition requires the soundness error to be negligible; when this is not the case for our protocols, we say so explicitly.

Ingredients

Our semi-succinct interactive argument consists of the following three ingredients:

•

A pseudorandom generator
\mathsf{PRG}:\{0,1\}^{\lambda}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, where
\ell=\ell(\lambda)
is a polynomial specified in
Lemma

C.1
below.

•

A semi-succinct (qubit-by-qubit) commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Commit},\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Ver},\mathsf{Out})
, as defined in
Section

5
and constructed in
Appendix

A
.

•

The information-theoretic QMA verification protocol of Fitzsimons, Hajdušek, and Morimae
[
FHM18
]
. As in
[
BKL+22
]
, we use an “instance-independent” version due to
[
ACGH20a
]
and assume the soundness gap is
1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
, where the latter can can be achieved by standard
QMA
amplification.

Lemma C.1

(
[
FHM18
,
ACGH20a
,
BKL+22
]
)
.

For all languages
{\cal L}=({\cal L}_{\mathsf{yes}},{\cal L}_{\mathsf{no}})\in\textbf{QMA}
there exists a polynomial
k(\lambda)
, a function
\ell(\lambda)
that is polynomial in the time
T(\lambda)
required to verify instances of size
\lambda
, a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
P_{\mathsf{FHM}}
, and a
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithm
V_{\mathsf{FHM}}
such that the following holds.

–

P_{\mathsf{FHM}}(\mathbf{x},\ket{\psi})\to\ket{\pi}
: on input an instance
\mathbf{x}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
and a quantum state
\ket{\psi}
,
P_{\mathsf{FHM}}
outputs an
\ell(\lambda)
-qubit state
\ket{\pi}
.

–

Completeness.
For all
\mathbf{x}\in{\cal L}_{\mathsf{yes}}
and
\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{R}_{\cal L}(x)
it holds that for a random
\mathbf{h}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell(\lambda)}

\Pr[V_{\mathsf{FHM}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v})=\mathsf{acc}:\ket{\pi}\leftarrow P_{\mathsf{FHM}}\left(\mathbf{x},\ket{\phi}^{\otimes k(\lambda)}\right)]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

where
\mathbf{v}
is the result of measuring
\ket{\pi}
in basis
\mathbf{h}
.

–

Soundness.
For all
\mathbf{x}\in{\cal L}_{\mathsf{no}}
and all
\ell
-qubit states
\ket{\pi^*}
it holds that for a random
\mathbf{h}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell(\lambda)}
,

\Pr[V_{\mathsf{FHM}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}^{*})=\mathsf{acc}]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

where
\mathbf{v}^{*}
is the result of measuring
\ket{\pi^*}
in basis
\mathbf{h}
.

The semi-succinct interactive argument for
QMA

In the following protocol
P
and
V
are given an instance
\mathbf{x}
and
P
is given
k
copies of the
QMA
witness
\ket{\psi}
.

V\rightarrow P
: Generate
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
, and send
\mathsf{pk}
.

P\rightarrow V
:

1.

Compute
\ket{\bpi}=P_{\mathsf{FHM}}\left(\mathbf{x},\ket{\bpsi}^{\otimes{k}}\right)
.

2.

Compute
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\bpi})
.

Denote by
\ell
the number of qubits in
\ket{\bpi}
, and denote by
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell})
, where
\mathbf{y}_{i}
is a commitment to the
i
’th qubit of
\bm{\sigma}
.

3.

Send
\mathbf{y}
.

V\rightarrow P
:
Send a random bit
b\in\{0,1\}
.

If
b=0
:
43
43

43

This should be thought of as a “test round.”

1.

V\rightarrow P
: Send a random bit
h\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

2.

P\rightarrow V
: Send
\mathbf{z}\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},h^{\ell})
.

3.

V\rightarrow P
: Compute
v=\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},h^{\ell},\mathbf{z})
and accept if
v=1
and otherwise, reject.

If
b=1
:

1.

V\rightarrow P
: Send a random seed
\mathbf{s}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

2.

P\rightarrow V
: Compute
\mathbf{b}=\mathsf{PRG}(\mathbf{s})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and send the openings
(\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{\ell})\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},\mathbf{b})
.

3.

V
does the following:

(a)

Compute
\mathbf{b}=\mathsf{PRG}(\mathbf{s})
.

(b)

For every
i\in[\ell]
compute
u_{i}=\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y}_{i},b_{i},\mathbf{z}_{i})
and
v_{i}=\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y}_{i},b_{i},\mathbf{z}_{i})
.

(c)

If there exists
i\in[\ell]
such that
u_{i}=0
then reject.

(d)

Else, accept if and only if
V_{\mathsf{FHM}}
would accept
(\mathbf{x},(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}),(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}))
.

Theorem C.2
.

The above scheme is a computationally sound semi-succinct interactive argument for
QMA
, with completeness
1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
and soundness
1-1/\lambda^{2}
.

Proof.

The completeness property is straightforward and hence we focus on proving the soundness property, which will follow from the binding property of the commitment. Fix a
QMA
promise problem
{\cal L}=({\cal L}_{\mathsf{yes}},{\cal L}_{\mathsf{no}})
. Fix
P^{*}
, an input
\mathbf{x}^{*}
and an auxiliary state
\bm{\sigma}
, such that
P^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\bm{\sigma})
is accepted with probability
1-\delta
, for
\delta\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}
. We argue that it must be the case that
\mathbf{x}^{*}\notin{\cal L}_{\mathsf{no}}
. To this end, we use
P^{*}
to construct
P^{*}_{\mathsf{FHM}}
that is accepted with high probability in the protocol
(P_{\mathsf{FHM}},V_{\mathsf{FHM}})
on input
\mathbf{x}^{*}
. The algorithm
P^{*}_{\mathsf{FHM}}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\bm{\sigma})
proceeds as follows:

1.

Generate
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Generate
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow P^{*}(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{x}^{*},\bm{\sigma})
.

3.

Use the extractor
\mathsf{Ext}
from the binding property of the commitment scheme to extract a state
\bm{\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ext}^{P^{*}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})

4.

Send
\bm{\tau}
.

We next argue that
V_{\mathsf{FHM}}
accepts
\bm{\tau}
with high probability on a random basis. To this end, it suffices to argue that it accepts
\bm{\tau}
with high probability on a pseudorandom basis, since otherwise one can distinguish a pseudorandom string from a truly random one, thus breaking the underlying
\mathsf{PRG}
.
Denote by

\mathsf{Good}=\{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{\lambda}:~P^{*}\mbox{ is accepted w.p.}\geq 1-\lambda\delta\mbox{ when $V$ sends $\mathbf{s}$}\}

Note that

p\triangleq\Pr[\mathbf{s}\in\mathsf{Good}]\geq 1-\frac{2}{\lambda}

(63)

which follows from the following Markov argument:

\displaystyle 1-2\delta\leq\Pr[P^{*}\mbox{ is accepted}~|~b=1]=

\displaystyle\Pr[P^{*}\mbox{ is accepted}~|~b=1~\wedge~\mathbf{s}\in\mathsf{Good}]\cdot\Pr[\mathbf{s}\in\mathsf{Good}~|~b=1]+

\displaystyle\Pr[P^{*}\mbox{ is accepted}~|~b=1~\wedge~\mathbf{s}\notin\mathsf{Good}]\cdot\Pr[\mathbf{s}\notin\mathsf{Good}~|~b=1]\leq

\displaystyle p+(1-\lambda\delta)(1-p)=

\displaystyle 1-\lambda\delta(1-p)

which implies that
-2\delta\leq-\lambda\delta(1-p)
and in turn that
\lambda(1-p)\leq 2
, thus implying
Equation

63
.
By the binding property of the underlying commitment scheme, for any basis
\mathbf{b}=\mathsf{PRG}(\mathbf{s})
such that
\mathbf{s}\in\mathsf{Good}
, it holds that

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Real}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle O(\sqrt{\lambda\delta})}}{{\approx}}(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Ideal}})

where
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Ideal}}
is the result of measuring
\bm{\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ext}^{P^{*}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})
in basis
\mathbf{b}
, and
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Real}}
is the output corresponding to the opening of
P^{*}
. The fact that the measurements

\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Real}}
are accepted by
V_{\mathsf{FHM}}
with probability
\geq 1-\lambda\delta
(for any basis
\mathsf{PRG}(\mathbf{s})
such that
\mathbf{s}\in\mathsf{Good}
) implies that
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Ideal}}
is accepted by
V_{\mathsf{FHM}}
with probability
\geq 1-\lambda\delta-O(\sqrt{\lambda\delta})
(for any such basis). This, together with
Equation

63
implies that
\bm{\tau}
is accepted by
V_{\mathsf{FHM}}
on a pseudorandom basis with probability

\displaystyle\Pr[V_{\mathsf{FHM}}\mbox{ accepts $\bm{\tau}$ on basis }\mathsf{PRG}(\mathbf{s})]\geq

\displaystyle\Pr[V_{\mathsf{FHM}}\mbox{ accepts $\bm{\tau}$ on basis }\mathsf{PRG}(\mathbf{s})~|~\mathbf{s}\in\mathsf{Good}]\cdot\Pr[\mathbf{s}\in\mathsf{Good}]\geq

\displaystyle\Pr[V_{\mathsf{FHM}}\mbox{ accepts $\bm{\tau}$ on basis }\mathsf{PRG}(\mathbf{s})~|~\mathbf{s}\in\mathsf{Good}]\cdot\left(1-\frac{2}{\lambda}\right)\geq

\displaystyle\left(1-\lambda\delta-O(\sqrt{\lambda\delta})\right)\cdot\left(1-\frac{2}{\lambda}\right)

This, together with
Lemma

C.1
and our assumption that
\delta\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}
, implies that
\mathbf{x}^{*}\notin{\cal L}_{\mathsf{No}}
, as desired.

∎

Theorem C.3
.

There exists a computationally sound succinct interactive argument for
QMA
, with completeness
1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
and soundness
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

To achieve full succinctness, we apply
[
BKL+22
, Theorem 9.3]
, which transforms a semi-succinct protocol into a fully succinct one. As input, this theorem expects a semi-succinct argument with soundness
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
, whereas the argument system given by
Theorem

C.2
only has soundness
1-1/\lambda^{2}
. However, it is easy to see that this can be made
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
by sequentially repeating the protocol
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
times. This does not harm succinctness since it blows up the communication by a factor of
\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
, which is permitted. (Alternatively, following the techniques of
[
ACGH20b
]
as used in
[
BKL+22
]
, parallel repetition can be employed to avoid an increase in round complexity.)
∎

C.2
Succinct Interactive Arguments from
X/Z
Quantum PCPs

In this section we show how to convert any
X/Z
quantum PCP for a language
{\cal L}
into an succinct interactive argument
(P,V)
for
{\cal L}
. As in
Section

C.1
we construct a semi-succinct interactive argument, and then use the black-box transformation from
[
BKL+22
]
to convert it into a fully succinct one.

Ingredients

Our semi-succinct interactive argument consists of the following ingredients.

•

A semi-succinct (qubit-by-qubit) commitment scheme
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Commit},\mathsf{Open},\mathsf{Ver},\mathsf{Out})
, as defined in
Section

5
and constructed in
Appendix

A
.

•

An
X/Z
quantum PCP for the language
{\cal L}
, with verifier
V_{\mathsf{QPCP}}
.

The semi-succinct interactive argument for
{\cal L}

In the following protocol
(P,V)
are given an instance
\mathbf{x}
and
P
is also given an
X/Z
quantum PCP
\ket{\pi}
.

V\rightarrow P
: Generate
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
, and send
\mathsf{pk}
.

P\rightarrow V
: Compute
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit}(\mathsf{pk},\ket{\bpi})
and send
\mathbf{y}

Denote by
\ell
the number of qubits in
\ket{\bpi}
, and denote by
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{\ell})
, where
\mathbf{y}_{i}
is a commitment to the
i
’th qubit of
\bm{\sigma}
.

V\rightarrow P
:
Send a random bit
b\in\{0,1\}
.

If
b=0
:
44
44

44

This should be thought of as a “test round.”

1.

V\rightarrow P
: Send a random bit
h\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

2.

P\rightarrow V
: Send
\mathbf{z}\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},h^{\ell})
.

3.

V\rightarrow P
: Compute
v=\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},h^{\ell},\mathbf{z})
and accept if
v=1
and otherwise, reject.

If
b=1
:

1.

V\rightarrow P
: Send a sample
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{c},b_{1},\ldots,b_{c})\leftarrow V_{\mathsf{QPCP}}(\mathbf{x},1^{\lambda})
.

2.

P\rightarrow V
: Send the openings
(\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{c})\leftarrow\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\rho},(i_{1},b_{1}),\ldots,(i_{c},b_{c}))
.

3.

V
does the following:

(a)

For every
j\in[c]
compute
v_{j}=\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y}_{i_{j}},b_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j})
and
u_{j}=\mathsf{Out}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y}_{i_{j}},b_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j})
.

(b)

If there exists
j\in[c]
such that
v_{j}=0
then reject.

(c)

Else, accept if and only if
V_{\mathsf{QPCP}}
would accept
(\mathbf{x},(i_{1},\ldots,i_{c}),(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}),(u_{1},\ldots,u_{c}))
.

Theorem C.4
.

The above scheme is a semi-succinct interactive argument for
{\cal L}
with completeness
1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
and soundness
1-1/\lambda^{2}
.

Proof of
Theorem

C.4
.

The completeness property is straightforward and hence we focus on proving the binding property. Fix a
\mathsf{QPT}
cheating prover
P^{*}
, an input
\mathbf{x}^{*}
and an auxiliary state
\bm{\sigma}
, such that
P^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{*},\bm{\sigma})
is accepted with probability
1-\delta
, for
\delta\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}
. We use
P^{*}
to extract an
X/Z
quantum PCP
\bm{\pi}
for
x^{*}\in{\cal L}
that is accepted with high probability, thus implying that indeed
x^{*}\in{\cal L}
as desired. This is done as follows:

1.

Generate
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Generate
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow P^{*}(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{x}^{*},\bm{\sigma})
.

3.

Use the extractor
\mathsf{Ext}
from the binding property of the commitment scheme to extract a state
\bm{\pi}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ext}^{P^{*}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})

4.

Output
\bm{\pi}
.

The fact that
P^{*}
is accepted with probability
1-\delta
implies that for every
h\in\{0,1\}
it opens in an accepted way on
h^{\ell}
with probability at least
1-4\delta
.
Denote by
\mathsf{Good}
the event that
V_{\mathsf{QPCP}}
samples
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{c},b_{1},\ldots,b_{c})
such that
P^{*}
is accepted with probability
\geq 1-\lambda\delta
when
V
sends
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{c},b_{1},\ldots,b_{c})
.
Note that

p\triangleq\Pr[\mathsf{Good}]\geq 1-\frac{2}{\lambda}

(64)

which follows from the following Markov argument:

\displaystyle 1-2\delta\leq\Pr[P^{*}\mbox{ is accepted}~|~b=1]=

\displaystyle\Pr[P^{*}\mbox{ is accepted}~|~b=1~\wedge~\mathsf{Good}]\cdot\Pr[\mathsf{Good}~|~b=1]+

\displaystyle\Pr[P^{*}\mbox{ is accepted}~|~b=1~\wedge~\neg\mathsf{Good}]\cdot\Pr[\neg\mathsf{Good}~|~b=1]\leq

\displaystyle p+(1-\lambda\delta)(1-p)=

\displaystyle 1-\lambda\delta(1-p)

which implies that
-2\delta\leq-\lambda\delta(1-p)
and in turn that
\lambda(1-p)\leq 2
, thus implying
Equation

64
. In what follows we say that
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{c},b_{1},\ldots,b_{c})\in\mathsf{Good}
if
P^{*}
is accepted when
V
sends
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{c},b_{1},\ldots,b_{c})
with probability
\geq 1-\lambda\delta
.
By the binding property of the underlying commitment scheme, for any
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{c},b_{1},\ldots,b_{c})\in\mathsf{Good}
, it holds that

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},(i_{1},\ldots,i_{c},b_{1},\ldots,b_{c}),\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Real}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle O(\sqrt{\lambda\delta})}}{{\approx}}(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},(i_{1},\ldots,i_{c},b_{1},\ldots,b_{c}),\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Ideal}})

where
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Ideal}}
is the result of measuring
\bm{\pi}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ext}^{P^{*}}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})
in locations
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{c})
and basis
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{c})
, and
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Real}}
is the output corresponding to the opening of
P^{*}
. The fact that the measurements

\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Real}}
are accepted by
V_{\mathsf{QPCP}}
with probability
\geq 1-\lambda\delta
(for any
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{c},b_{1},\ldots,b_{c})\in\mathsf{Good}
) implies that
\mathbf{m}_{\mathsf{Ideal}}
is accepted by
V_{\mathsf{QPCP}}
with probability
\geq 1-\lambda\delta-O(\sqrt{\lambda\delta})
(for any such basis). This, together with
Equation

64
implies that

\displaystyle\Pr[V_{\mathsf{QPCP}}\mbox{ accepts $\bm{\pi}$}]\geq

\displaystyle\Pr[V_{\mathsf{QPCP}}\mbox{ accepts $\bm{\pi}$}~|~\mathsf{Good}]\cdot\Pr[\mathsf{Good}]\geq

\displaystyle\Pr[V_{\mathsf{QPCP}}\mbox{ accepts $\bm{\pi}$ }~|~\mathsf{Good}]\cdot\left(1-\frac{2}{\lambda}\right)\geq

\displaystyle\left(1-\lambda\delta-O(\sqrt{\lambda\delta})\right)\cdot\left(1-\frac{2}{\lambda}\right)

This, together with our assumption that
\delta\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}
, implies that indeed
\bm{\pi}
is an
X/Z
PCP that is accepted with high probability, and thus
\mathbf{x}\in{\cal L}
, as desired.

Theorem C.5
.

There exists a succinct interactive argument for
{\cal L}
with completeness
1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
and soundness
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

We apply
[
BKL+22
, Theorem 9.3]
to the sequential repetition of the argument system from
Theorem

C.4
, exactly as in the proof of
Theorem

C.3
.
∎

∎

Appendix D
Weak commitments to Quantum States (WCQ)

In this section, we recall the Measurement Protocol from Mahadev
[
Mah18
]
, which was formalized by
[
BKL+22
]
. We refer to such a protocol as a
weak commitment to quantum states
(WCQ) protocol, and define it formally below.

Definition D.1

(Weak Commitment to Quantum States (WCQ))
.

An
\ell
-qubit WCQ protocol is specified by the five algorithms
(\mathsf{Gen_{\text{W}}},\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}},\mathsf{Open_{\text{W}}},\mathsf{Test_{\text{W}}},\mathsf{Out_{\text{W}}})
:

1.

\mathsf{Gen_{\text{W}}}
is a
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithm that takes as input the security parameter
\lambda
(in unary) and a string
h\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, and outputs a pair
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen_{\text{W}}}(1^{\lambda},h)
, where
\mathsf{pk}
is referred to as the public key and
\mathsf{sk}
is referred to as the secret key.

2.

\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}
is a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm that takes as input a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and a quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
and outputs a pair
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
, where
\mathbf{y}
is a classical string, referred to as the commitment string, and
\bm{\rho}
is a quantum state.

3.

\mathsf{Open_{\text{W}}}
is a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm that takes as input a bit
c\in\{0,1\}
and a quantum state
\bm{\rho}
and outputs a classical string
z\leftarrow\mathsf{Open_{\text{W}}}(\bm{\rho},c)
, referred to as the opening string.

4.

\mathsf{Test_{\text{W}}}
is a polynomial time algorithm that takes as input a public key
\mathsf{pk}
and a pair
(\mathbf{y},z)
, where
\mathbf{y}
is a commitment string and
z
is an opening string, and it outputs
\{\mathsf{acc},\mathsf{rej}\}\leftarrow\mathsf{Test_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{pk},(\mathbf{y},z))
.

5.

\mathsf{Out_{\text{W}}}
is a polynomial time algorithm that takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
and a pair
(\mathbf{y},z)
, where
\mathbf{y}
is a commitment string and
z
is an opening string, and it outputs a classical string
m\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

The commitment protocol associated with the tuple
(\mathsf{Gen_{\text{W}}},\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}},\mathsf{Open_{\text{W}}},\mathsf{Test_{\text{W}}},\mathsf{Out_{\text{W}}})
is a two party protocol between a
\mathsf{QPT}
committer
{\mathsf{C}}
which takes as input a quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
, and a
\mathsf{BPP}
verifier
{\mathsf{V}}
which takes as input a classical string
h\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
. Both parties also take as input the unary security parameter
\lambda
. The protocol consists of two phases
\mathsf{COMMIT}
and
\mathsf{OPEN}
, proceeding as follows:

•

\mathsf{COMMIT}
phase:

1.

[{\mathsf{C}}\leftarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{V}}
samples
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen_{\text{W}}}(1^{\lambda},h)
and sends the public key
\mathsf{pk}
to
{\mathsf{C}}
.

2.

[{\mathsf{C}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{C}}
computes
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
and sends the commitment string
\mathbf{y}
to the verifier.

•

\mathsf{OPEN}
phase:

1.

[{\mathsf{C}}\leftarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{V}}
samples a random challenge bit
c\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and sends
\mathsf{ct}
to
{\mathsf{C}}
.

2.

[{\mathsf{C}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{V}}]
:
{\mathsf{C}}
sends
z\leftarrow\mathsf{Open_{\text{W}}}(\bm{\rho},c)
to
{\mathsf{V}}
.

3.

If
c=0
,
{\mathsf{V}}
outputs
\{\mathsf{acc},\mathsf{rej}\}\leftarrow\mathsf{Test_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{pk},y,z)
. If
c=1
,
{\mathsf{V}}
outputs
m\leftarrow\mathsf{Out_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{sk},y,z)
.

A WCQ protocol acts over registers
{\mathcal{P}},{\mathcal{Y}},{\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{W}}
where
{\mathcal{P}}
contains the public component of the output of
\mathsf{Gen_{\text{W}}}
,
{\mathcal{Y}}
contains the output of
\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}
,
{\mathcal{Z}}
contains the output of
\mathsf{Open_{\text{W}}}
, and
{\mathcal{W}}
are additional work registers. Additionally, the commitment protocol satisfies the following properties for
correctness
and
binding
.

Definition D.2

(WCQ correctness)
.

Let
\mathsf{RealW}(1^{\lambda},\bm{\sigma},h)
be the distribution resulting from running
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen_{\text{W}}}(1^{\lambda},h)
,
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
,
z\leftarrow\mathsf{Open_{\text{W}}}(\bm{\rho},1)
, and outputting
m\leftarrow\mathsf{Out_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{sk},y,z)
. Let
\bm{\sigma}(h)
denote the distribution resulting from measuring each qubit
i
of a quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
in the basis specified by
h_{i}
for
i\in[\ell]
. A WCQ protocol is correct if, for all
\ell
-qubit quantum states
\bm{\sigma}
and for every
h\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, the following two properties are satisfied:

1.

(Test Round Completeness):

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\!\begin{aligned} &(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen_{\text{W}}}(1^{\lambda},h);\\
\mathsf{acc}\leftarrow\mathsf{Test_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{pk},y,z):\quad&(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma});\\
&z\leftarrow\mathsf{Open_{\text{W}}}(\bm{\rho},0)]\\
\end{aligned}\right]=1-\mathsf{negl}({\lambda})

(65)

2.

(Measurement Round Completeness):

\left\{\!\begin{aligned} &(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen_{\text{W}}}(1^{\lambda},h);\\
m\leftarrow\mathsf{Out_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{sk},y,z):\quad&(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma});\\
&z\leftarrow\mathsf{Open_{\text{W}}}(\bm{\rho},1)\\
\end{aligned}\right\}\approx_{c}\bm{\sigma}(h)

(66)

Definition D.3

(WCQ Binding)
.

[
BKL+22
]
A WCQ protocol is
binding
if there exists a
\mathsf{PPT}
classical algorithm
\mathsf{SimGen}
and a
\mathsf{QPT}
oracle machine
\mathsf{WExt}
such that, for any cheating
\mathsf{QPT}
committer
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}
with quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
that satisfies that for every
h\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
:

\mathrm{Pr}\left[\!\begin{aligned} &(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen_{\text{W}}}(1^{\lambda},h);\\
\mathsf{acc}\leftarrow\mathsf{Test_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{pk},y,z):\quad&(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma});\\
&z\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open_{\text{W}}}(\bm{\rho},0)]\\
\end{aligned}\right]=1-\mathsf{negl}({\lambda}),

(67)

it holds that for every
h\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
,

\mathsf{Sim}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}}(1^{\lambda},h)\approx_{c}\mathsf{RealW}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}}(1^{\lambda},h)

where

•

\mathsf{Sim}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}}(1^{\lambda},h)
is the output distribution of the following procedure:

1.

Sample
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{SimGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

Execute the commitment round to obtain
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}(\bm{\sigma})
.

3.

Execute
\bm{\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{WExt}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}}(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk},y,\bm{\rho})
.

4.

Measure
\bm{\tau}
in the basis specified by
h
, where
h_{i}=0
corresponds to the standard basis and
h_{i}=1
corresponds to the Hadamard, and output these measurement values.

•

\mathsf{RealW}^{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}}(1^{\lambda},h)

is the output distribution of the following procedure:

1.

Sample
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen_{\text{W}}}(1^{\lambda},h)
.

2.

Emulate the commitment round to obtain
(\mathbf{y},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Commit_{\text{W}}}(\bm{\sigma})
.

3.

Emulate the opening phase round corresponding to
c=1
to obtain
z\leftarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}.\mathsf{Open_{\text{W}}}(\bm{\rho},1)
.

4.

Compute
m\leftarrow\mathsf{Out_{\text{W}}}(\mathsf{sk},y,z)
and output
m
.

Appendix E
A
\mathsf{NTCF}
construction with the distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit property

We argue that the
\mathsf{NTCF}
from
[
BCM+18
]
, defined below, satisfies the distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit property (under
\mathsf{LWE}
).

The
\mathsf{NTCF}
family from
[
BCM+18
]

The
\mathsf{NTCF}
family from
[
BCM+18
]
is a lattice based construction and makes use of the following theorem from
[
MP11
]
.

Theorem E.1

(Theorem 5.1 in
[
MP11
]
)
.

Let
n,m\geq 1
and
q\geq 2
be such that
m=\Omega(n\log q)
. There is
an efficient randomized algorithm
\mathsf{TrapGen}_{\mathsf{MP}}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
that returns a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times n}
together with a trapdoor
\mathbf{t}_{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
such that the distribution of
\mathbf{A}
is negligibly (in
n
) close to the uniform distribution. Moreover, there is
an efficient algorithm
\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{MP}}
that, on input
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{e})
, where
\norm{\vece}\leq\frac{q}{C\sqrt{n\log q}}
and where
C
is a universal constant, returns
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{e}
with overwhelming probability over
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{t}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}_{\mathsf{MP}}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
.

The
\mathsf{NTCF}
family
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Invert},\mathsf{Check},\mathsf{Good})
from
[
BCM+18
]
is defined as follows:

•

\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
is associated with the following:

–

Prime
q\leq 2^{\lambda}
of size super-polynomial in
\lambda
.

–

n=n(\lambda)
and
m=m(\lambda)
, both polynomially bounded functions of
\lambda
, such that
m\geq n\log q
and
n\geq\lambda
.

–

Two error distributions
\chi,\chi^{\prime}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, that are associated with bounds
B,B^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that:

1.

\frac{B}{B^{\prime}}=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

2.

B^{\prime}\leq\frac{q}{2C\sqrt{n\cdot m\cdot\log q}}
, where
C
is the universal constant from
Theorem

E.1
.

3.

\Pr_{\mathbf{e}\leftarrow\chi^{m}}[\norm{\vece}>B]=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

4.

\Pr_{\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\chi^{\prime})^{m}}[\norm{\vece'}>B^{\prime}]=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

5.

\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\equiv\mathbf{e}^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}
, for
\mathbf{e}\leftarrow\chi^{m}
and
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\chi^{\prime})^{m}
.

It does the following:

1.

Generate
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{t}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}_{\mathsf{MP}}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
.

2.

Choose a random bit string
\mathbf{s}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
and a random error vector
\mathbf{e}\leftarrow\chi^{m}
.

3.

Let
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{e}
.

4.

Output
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{u})
and
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{t}_{\mathbf{A}})
.

•

\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},b,\cdot)
is a function with domain
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and range
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
.

\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x})
parses
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{u})
, samples
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\chi^{\prime})^{m}
, and outputs
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}+b\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{e}^{\prime}
(where all the operations are done modulo
q
).

Equivalently, we think of
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},b,\cdot)
as a function with domain
\{0,1\}^{w}
for
w\triangleq n\cdot\lceil{\log q}\rceil
, where each element
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is matched to its bit decomposition.
Namely, denote by

J:\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{q}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{w}

the bit decomposition function where each element in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
is converted to its bit decomposition in
\{0,1\}^{\lceil{\log q}\rceil}
. We think
\mathsf{Eval}
as taking as input an element
\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{w}
, computing
\mathbf{x}=J^{-1}(\mathbf{z})\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,
45
45

45

J^{-1}:\{0,1\}^{w}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is the function that breaks its input into
n
blocks of length
\lceil{\log q}\rceil
each, and replaces each such block
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\lceil{\log q}\rceil})
with the element
(\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil{\log q}\rceil}b_{i}\cdot 2^{i-1})\mod q
, which is an element in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.
and then applying
\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x})
.

Remark E.2
.

We note the change in notation: In the definition of a
\mathsf{NTCF}
family, we denoted the input length by
n
, and here we denote it by
w
.

•

\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y})
does the following:

1.

Parse
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{t}_{\mathbf{A}})
.

2.

Compute
\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{MP}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{t}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{y})=\mathbf{x}

3.

If
\norm{\vecy-\vecA\cdot\vecx}\leq 2\sqrt{m}\cdot B^{\prime}
then output
((0,J(\mathbf{x})),(1,J(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s})))
.

•

\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{pk},b,J(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{y})
outputs
1
if and only if
\norm{\vecy-\vecA\vecx-b\vecu}\leq 2\sqrt{m}\cdot B^{\prime}
.

•

\mathsf{Good}(J(\mathbf{x}_{0}),J(\mathbf{x}_{1}),\mathbf{d})
outputs
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that

\mathbf{d}\cdot(1,J(\mathbf{x}_{0})\oplus J(\mathbf{x}_{1}))=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{s}

where the inner product in both sides of the equation above is done modulo
2
.
The vector
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
is computed as follows, using the fact that

\mathbf{x}_{1}=\mathbf{x}_{0}-\mathbf{s}
(where subtraction is modulo
q
) and the fact that
\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.

1.

Partition
\mathbf{d}\in\{0,1\}^{w+1}
into its first bit, denoted by
d_{0}
, and the following
n
blocks, each of size
\lceil{\log q\rceil}
, denoted by
\mathbf{d}[1],\ldots,\mathbf{d}[n]\in\{0,1\}^{\lceil{\log q\rceil}}
.

2.

For every
b\in\{0,1\}
, partition
J(\mathbf{x}_{b})
into blocks
J(\mathbf{x}_{b,1}),\ldots,J(\mathbf{x}_{b,n})
, each of size
\lceil{\log q\rceil}
.

3.

For every
i\in[n]
let

\mathbf{d}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathbf{d}[i]\cdot(J(\mathbf{x}_{0,i})\oplus J(\mathbf{x}_{0,i}-1)),

where
\cdot
denotes inner product mod
2
and where
\mathbf{x}_{0,i}-1
is done mod
q

4.

Output the string
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.

Note that

\mathbf{d}\cdot(1,J(\mathbf{x}_{0})\oplus J(\mathbf{x}_{1}))=d_{0}+\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{s}\mod 2.

Proposition E.3
.

The
\mathsf{NTCF}
family from
[
BCM+18
]
satisfies the distributional strong adaptive hardcore bit property assuming the post-quantum hardness of
\mathsf{LWE}
.

The proof of
Proposition

E.3
makes use of the following lemma from
[
BCM+18
]
.

Lemma E.4
.

[
BCM+18
]

Let
q
be a prime,
k,n\geq 1
integers, and
\mathbf{C}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}^{k\times n}_{q}

a uniformly random matrix. With probability
at least
1-q^{k}\cdot 2^{-\frac{n}{8}}

over the choice of
\mathbf{C}
the following holds for the fixed
\mathbf{C}
. For all
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}_{q}
and any distinct vectors
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{0^{n}\}
, the distribution of
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{s},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{s})
, where both inner produces are done
\mod 2
and where
\mathbf{s}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
is uniform
conditioned on
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{v}
, is within
statistical distance
O(q^{\frac{3k}{2}}\cdot 2^{\frac{-n}{40}})
of the uniform distribution over
\{0,1\}^{2}
.

Remark E.5
.

We note that
[
BCM+18
]
proved this lemma for a single vector
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
(as opposed to two distinct ones). Their proof carries over to this setting as well, and we include it in
Appendix

E
for completeness.

Proof of
Proposition

E.3
.

We define
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{pre}}
, corresponding to
\mathsf{sk}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{t}_{\mathbf{A}})
, to be
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{pre}}=\mathsf{Invert}_{\mathsf{MP}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{t}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{u})
. Thus
\mathsf{sk}_{\mathsf{pre}}=\mathbf{s}
, where
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{e}
and
\mathbf{e}
is a low norm vector.

Fix any
\mathsf{QPT}
circuit
C
and any
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathsf{A}
that takes as input
\mathsf{pk}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{e})
and a quantum state
\bm{\sigma}
, and outputs a tuple
(\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{x},\bm{\rho})
, such that
\mathsf{Check}(\mathsf{pk},b,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=1
and
\bm{\rho}
is a state that has registers
{\cal O}_{1}
and
{\cal O}_{2}
, where
{\cal O}_{1}
contains
w+1
qubits, and with overwhelming probability over
\mathsf{aux}\leftarrow C(\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}},\mathbf{s})
it holds that the residual state
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}
satisfies that for every
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
the probability of measuring registers
{\cal O}_{1}
of
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}
in the standard basis and obtaining
\mathbf{d}\in\{0,1\}^{w+1}
such that
\mathsf{Good}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1})=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
is negligible in
\lambda
.

We need to prove that

(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},J(\mathbf{x}_{0})\oplus J(\mathbf{x}_{1}),\mathbf{d}\cdot(1,J(\mathbf{x}_{0})\oplus J(\mathbf{x}_{1})),\mathsf{aux})\approx(\mathsf{pk},\mathbf{y},J(\mathbf{x}_{0})\oplus J(\mathbf{x}_{1}),U,\mathsf{aux})

(68)

where
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
,
(\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{x},\bm{\rho},C)\leftarrow\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
,
((0,\mathbf{x}_{0}),(1,\mathbf{x}_{1}))=\mathsf{Invert}(\mathsf{sk},\mathbf{y})
,
(\mathbf{d},\mathsf{aux})
is obtained by letting
\mathsf{aux}\leftarrow C(\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}},\mathbf{s})
and
\mathbf{d}
is the outcome of measuring the
{\cal O}_{1}
registers of the residual state
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}
in the standard basis, and
U
is uniformly distributed in
\{0,1\}
.

To this end, we define an alternative algorithm
\widehat{\mathsf{Gen}}(1^{\lambda})
, which is the same as
\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
with the only difference being that rather than choosing
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{t}_{\mathbf{A}})
via the
\mathsf{TrapGen}
algorithm, it chooses
\mathbf{A}
to be close to a low rank matrix. Specifically,
\widehat{\mathsf{Gen}}(1^{\lambda})
does the following:

1.

Let
\delta=\epsilon/2
and let
k=n^{\delta}
.

2.

Sample
(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{t}_{\mathbf{B}})\leftarrow\mathsf{TrapGen}_{\mathsf{MP}}(1^{k},1^{m},q)
.

3.

Sample
\mathbf{C}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{k\times n}
and
\mathbf{N}\leftarrow\chi^{m\times n}
.

4.

Let
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{B}\cdot\mathbf{C}+\mathbf{N}
.

5.

Sample
\mathbf{s}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
and
\mathbf{e}\leftarrow\chi^{m}
.

6.

Let
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}=\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{e}
.

7.

Output
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}=(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\widehat{\mathbf{u}})
and
\widehat{\mathsf{sk}}=(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{t}_{\mathbf{B}},\mathbf{s})
.

The
\mathsf{LWE}
implies that
\mathbf{A}\approx\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
which in turn implies that

(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{x},\bm{\rho})\approx(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\widehat{\mathbf{u}},\mathbf{s},\widehat{\mathbf{y}},\widehat{b},\widehat{\mathbf{x}},{\widehat{\bm{\rho}}})

(69)

where

•

\mathbf{s}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
and
\mathbf{e}\leftarrow\chi^{m}
.

•

\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{e}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{u}}=\widehat{\mathbf{A}}\cdot\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{e}
.

•

(\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{x},\bm{\rho})=\mathsf{A}(\mathsf{pk},\bm{\sigma})
for

\mathsf{pk}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{u})
.

•

(\widehat{\mathbf{y}},\widehat{b},\widehat{\mathbf{x}},{\widehat{\bm{\rho}}})=\mathsf{A}(\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\bm{\sigma})
for

\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}=(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\widehat{\mathbf{u}})
.

We next argue that

(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{y},J(\mathbf{x}_{0})\oplus J(\mathbf{x}_{1}),\mathbf{d}\cdot(1,J(\mathbf{x}_{0})\oplus J(\mathbf{x}_{1})),\mathsf{aux})\approx(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{y},J(\mathbf{x}_{0})\oplus J(\mathbf{x}_{1}),U,\mathsf{aux})

(70)

where in the above equation, to avoid cluttering of notation, we omit some of the “hat” notation, and denote by
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}=(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{u})
distributed according to
\widehat{\mathsf{Gen}}(1^{\lambda})
,
(\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{x},\bm{\rho})\leftarrow\mathsf{A}(\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\bm{\sigma})
for
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}=(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{u})
,
\mathbf{x}_{b}=\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{x}_{1-b}=\mathbf{x}_{b}-(-1)^{b}\mathbf{s}
, and
(\mathbf{d},\mathsf{aux})
is obtained by measuring computing
\mathsf{aux}\leftarrow C(\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}},\mathbf{s})
and
\mathbf{d}
is the outcome of measuring the
{\cal O}_{1}
registers of the residual state
\bm{\rho}_{\mathsf{aux}}
in the standard basis.

Equation

68
follows immediately from
Equation

70
, together with the fact that
\mathbf{A}\approx\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
and the fact that the distributions in
Equations

70
and
68
can be generated efficiently from
\mathbf{A}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{A}}
, respectively.

Let

\mathbf{d}^{\prime}=\mathsf{Good}(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{d})\in\{0,1\}^{n},

then by the definition of
\mathsf{Good}
,

\mathbf{d}\cdot(1,J(\mathbf{x}_{0})\oplus J(\mathbf{x}_{1}))=d_{0}\oplus\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{s}.

Therefore, to prove
Equation

70
it suffices to prove that

(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{x},d_{0},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{s},\mathsf{aux})\approx(\widehat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{y},b,\mathbf{x},d_{0},U,\mathsf{aux}).

To prove the above equation it suffices to prove that with overwhelming probability over
\mathbf{C}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{k\times n}

it holds that for every
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{k}
, for every distribution
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbf{v}}
(that depends on
\mathbf{v}
) that outputs
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}})
such that with overwhelming probability
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
has min-entropy
\omega(\log\lambda)
given
\mathsf{aux}\leftarrow C(\bm{\rho}_{{\cal O}_{2}},\mathbf{s})
,

(\mathbf{s},\mathsf{aux},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{s})\equiv(\mathbf{s},\mathsf{aux},U),

(71)

where
\mathbf{s}
is sampled randomly from
\{0,1\}^{n}
conditioned on
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{v}
.
We note that the distribution of
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
may not have min-entropy
\omega(\log\lambda)
(conditioned on
\mathsf{aux}
) since it is generated w.r.t.
\widehat{A}
and not w.r.t.
A
, and while
A\approx\widehat{A}
, checking if a distribution has min-entropy cannot be done efficiently.
Nevertheless, the distribution
\mathsf{D}_{\mathbf{v}}
is indistinguishable from having min-entropy
\omega(\log\lambda)
, and thus it suffices to prove
Equation

71
.
To this end, for every
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{k\times n}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{v}
consider the sets

{\cal S}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\{0,1\}^{n}~:~\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{v}\}~~\mbox{ and }~~{\cal X}=\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{0^{n}\}

and the hash function

h:{\cal S}\times{\cal X}\rightarrow\{0,1\},

defined by

h(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})=\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\mod 2.

Lemma

E.4
implies that for all but negligible fraction of
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{v}
it holds that
h
is 2-universal, which together with the leftover hash lemma, implies that
Equation

71
holds, as desired.

∎

To show our modified version of Lemma 4.6 of
[
BCM+18
]
, it suffices to show a version of their Lemma 4.9 modified to handle two arbitrary binary strings
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2}
. Once this has been shown, the remaining argument proceeds unchanged. In this section will only present our modified version of Lemma 4.9 and its proof.

First, let us recall some basic properties of the discrete Fourier transform. Define the
q
th root of unity

\omega_{q}=e^{2\pi i/q}.

The “standard Fourier identity” is that

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\omega_{q}^{x}=0.

For a function
f:\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\mathbb{C}
, the Fourier transform
\hat{f}
is defined by

\hat{f}(\mathbf{x},y)=\sum_{\mathbf{v},z}\omega_{q}^{\mathbf{v}\cdot x}(-1)^{y\cdot z}f(\mathbf{v},z).

With this normalization, we have the following version of Plancherel’s theorem:

\sqrt{2q^{\ell}}\|f\|_{2}=\|\hat{f}\|_{2}.

Now, in the context of Lemma 4.9 of
[
BCM+18
]
, we are given a random matrix
\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\ell\times n}
, and arbitrary distinct nonzero binary vectors
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.
Define

g(\mathbf{v},z_{1},z_{2})=\Pr_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}[\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{s},z_{1}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{s},z_{2}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{s}].

Then, to prove the Lemma, it suffices to show that with high probability over the choice of the matrix
\mathbf{C}
, the probability distribution whose density is
g
is close to the uniform distribution over the space
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Specifically, denoting by
U
the uniform distribution and denoting by
D
the Total Variation Distance, we wish to show that

D(g,U)\leq q^{\ell/2}\cdot 2^{-n/40}.

To do so, we relate the TVD distance to the
L_{2}
norm of the difference
g-U
:

\displaystyle D(g,U)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|g-U\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2q^{\ell}}\|g-U\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{g}-\hat{U}\|_{2},

where the second line follows from Cauchy-Schwarz.
Note that for
any
probability density
g
over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, we have that
\hat{g}(0^{\ell},0,0)=1
. This is because

\hat{f}(0^{\ell},0,0)=\sum_{\mathbf{v},z_{1},z_{2}}g(\mathbf{v},z_{1},z_{2})=1.

Moreover, for the uniform density
U
, we further have
\hat{U}(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})=0
for all
(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})\neq(0^{\ell},0,0)
. This follows by the standard Fourier identity.

Thus, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{g}-\hat{U}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\sum_{\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2}}|\hat{g}(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})-\hat{U}(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\sum_{(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})\neq(0^{\ell},0)}|\hat{g}(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})|^{2}}.

To bound this sum, we will now calculate
\hat{g}
, using the identities
(-1)^{yz}=(e^{\pi i})^{yz}=e^{(2\pi i/2)\cdot yz}
to simplify the resulting sums.

\displaystyle\hat{g}(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{v},z_{1},z_{2}}\omega_{q}^{\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}}(-1)^{y_{1}z_{1}+y_{2}z_{2}}g(\mathbf{v},z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{v},z_{1},z_{2}}e^{2\pi i\cdot(\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}/q+(y_{1}z_{1}+y_{2}z_{2})/2)}g(\mathbf{v},y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{v},z_{1},z_{2}}e^{2\pi i\cdot(2\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}+q(y_{1}z_{1}+y_{2}z_{2}))/2q}g(\mathbf{v},z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{v},z_{1},z_{2}}\omega_{2q}^{2\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}+q(y_{1}z_{1}+y_{2}z_{2})}g(\mathbf{v},z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle=\E_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\sum_{\mathbf{v},z_{1},z_{2}}\omega_{2q}^{2\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{x}+q(y_{1}z_{1}+y_{2}z_{2})}\mathbf{1}[\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{C}\mathbf{s},z_{1}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{s},z_{2}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2}]

\displaystyle=\E_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\omega_{2q}^{2\mathbf{x}\cdot(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{s})+q((y_{1}\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}+y_{2}\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{s})}.

Define
\mathbf{w}=2\mathbf{C}^{T}\mathbf{x}+q(y_{1}\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}+y_{2}\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2})

so that
\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{s}
is equal to the exponent in the last line above. Then

\hat{g}(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})=\E_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\omega_{2q}^{\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{s}}.

Our goal is to show that
\hat{g}
, with the
(0^{\ell},0,0)
entry deleted, is small in 2-norm. We are going to do this by bounding the entries individually.

Case 1:
(\mathbf{x},y)=(0^{\ell},1)
.

In this case, we have

\displaystyle\hat{g}(0^{\ell},y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle=\E_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\omega_{2q}^{q(y_{1}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1})^{T}+y_{2}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2})^{T})\mathbf{s}}

\displaystyle=\E_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}(-1)^{(y_{1}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1})^{T}+y_{2}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2})^{T})\mathbf{s}}

\displaystyle=0,

where in the last line we used the fact that at least one of
y_{1},y_{2}
is nonzero, and that
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}\neq\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2}
, to say that
y_{1}\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}+y_{2}\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2}
is a nonzero binary vector.

Case 2:
\mathbf{x}\neq 0^{\ell}
.

In this case, we will use the fact that
\mathbf{C}
is a random matrix. Specifically, in Lemma 4.8 of
[
BCM+18
]
it is shown that with probability
1-q^{\ell}\cdot 2^{-n/8}
,
\mathbf{C}
is
moderate
. To define this, we start by defining a moderate
scalar
: for
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, let its
centered representative
be its unique representative in
(-q/2,q/2]
. Then we say
x
is
moderate
if its centered representative lies in the range
[-3q/8,-q/8)\cup(q/8,3q/8]
. This is true for a uniformly random
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
with probability
1/2
. A
moderate vector
is one for which at least
1/4
of the entries are moderate: for a uniformly random vector in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, the chance that it is moderate is exponentially close to
1
, by a Chernoff bound. A
moderate matrix
is one for which every nonzero vector in the row span is moderate.

Now, observe:

\displaystyle\left|\E_{s\in\{0,1\}}\omega_{q}^{sx}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{2}(\omega_{q}^{0}+\omega_{q}^{x})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{2}(\omega_{q}^{-x/2}+\omega_{q}^{x/2})\right|

\displaystyle=|\cos( \pi x / q)|.

Thus, if
x
is moderate, then
|x/q|\in(1/8,3/8]
, and
|\cos(\pi x/q)|\leq|\cos( \pi/8)|
. So for any moderate vector
\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, it holds that

\left|\E_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\omega_{q}^{\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{s}}\right|\leq|\cos(\pi/8)|^{n/4}.

We will need a slightly refined version of this. Let
r
be a moderate scalar
\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, and
e_{1},e_{2}\in\{0,1\}
be arbitrary. Then

2r+q(e_{1}+e_{2})\in[-3q/4,-q/4)\cup[q/4,3q/4)\cup[q+q/4,q+3/q4)\cup(q/4,3q/4]\cup(q+q/4,q+3q/4]\cup(2q+q/4,2q+3/q4].

Thus,

|(2r+q(e_{1}+e_{2}))/q|\in[1/4,3/4]\pmod{1}.

Therefore,

\left|\E_{s\in\{0,1\}}\omega_{2q}^{(2r+qe)s}\right|=\left|\cos(\frac{\pi}{2q} (2r + qe))\right|\leq|\cos(\pi/8)|.

Thus, if
\mathbf{r}
is moderate and
\mathbf{e}_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2}
are arbitrary binary vectors, then by the same reasoning

\displaystyle\left|\E_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\omega_{2q}^{(2\mathbf{r}+q(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}))\cdot\mathbf{s}}\right|\leq|\cos(\pi/8)|^{n/4}.

Now, to finish the argument, let’s return to
\hat{g}
. For any
\mathbf{x}\neq 0^{\ell}
, since
\mathbf{C}
is moderate, we know
\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{C}
is a moderate vector. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\hat{g}(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})=\E_{\mathbf{s}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\omega_{2q}^{2(\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{C})\mathbf{s}+q(y_{1}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{s})+y_{2}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{s}))}

Now setting
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{e}_{1,2}=y_{1,2}\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1,2}
, we conclude that

|\hat{g}(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})|\leq|\cos(\pi/8)|^{n/4}.

So in the end we get

\displaystyle D(g,U)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\sum_{(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})\neq(0^{\ell},0,0)}|\hat{g}(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2})|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\sum_{\mathbf{x}\neq 0^{\ell}}\sum_{(y_{1},y_{2})\in\{0,1\}^{2}}|\cos(\pi/8)|^{n/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{4(q^{\ell}-1)|\cos(\pi/8)|^{n/2}}

\displaystyle\leq q^{\ell/2}\cdot 2^{-n/40}

This is the desired bound.
